# CodeYourPCB tracker - the control center

Last updated: 2026-08-09. Update after every material step: add to DONE, pull the next item into NEXT-ACTION, in the same commit as the change.

Read this file first. It is the source of truth for what is in flight and what comes next.

## Where the project stands

- Version 0.2.0-beta, ~44k lines (Rust workspace + TypeScript viewer). Last release commit `746bd63` (2026-04-15).
- GSD milestones M001-M005 are marked complete in `.gsd/STATE.md`. That status was written in March and predates the 2026-07-10 audit, which ran the code and found crash-tier defects. **Where the two disagree, the audit wins** - it executed commands, the milestone summaries did not.
- Development happens in the `code-server` container on the build host (12 cores, 10GB cap). Full clean release build: 34s. Full debug build: 20s.

## Phase map

Each box is checked against a command, not against memory. Three of them had
been ticked off in the vectors below for weeks while the map still called P1
current, which is how a fresh session was told to work on a phase that was over.

- [x] P0 - Establish verified ground truth (2026-07-10 audit)
- [x] P1 - Stop the bleeding: crashes, lying commands, broken fresh clone.
  Measured 2026-08-08: `git clone` into an empty directory, then
  `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **119 suites, 1467
  passed, 0 failed, 1m18s from cold**. The commands that lied - `check` on a
  file with no board, `parse -o json` printing the AST, `score` guessing a
  fabricator, three build scripts printing success over a failed build - are
  each fixed with a test in V1 and V3 below.
- [x] P2 - Green quality gate: clippy, fmt, all tests including WASM E2E.
  `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`, all eight stages,
  playwright 100 passed / 17 skipped. First green on 2026-08-06 and green on
  every run since.
- [ ] P3 - Structural: one parser, one width formula, no orphan crates.  <- current
  **One parser: done** - `parseSource` and its helpers are deleted, the Rust
  reader is the only one, `docs/one-parser.md` carries the drift table.
  **One width formula: done** - the viewer's copy of IPC-2221 is gone and
  `viewer/src/__tests__/one-width-formula.test.ts` fails if it comes back.
  **Orphan crates: blocked on D3**, which is the owner's call, not a fire's.
- [ ] P4 - Roadmap features. Copper pour, KiCad in and out, modules, imports
  and assertions are done and have tests; `interface` parses and builds
  nothing, and what it should build is a question for the owner.
- [ ] P5 - Optimization passes (measured, per GP-002). Seventeen routing
  instruments measured and written up, two kept; the 3x grid pass measured and
  kept. Nothing here is finished, and nothing here is guessed.

## Vectors (parallel branches - keep ALL moving)

### V1 - CLI and core correctness

- DONE: **D6's outstanding page-read is done, and the answer is that only one of the ten presets ever had a published pad land.** The action was to read each fab's capability page for the row that pairs a hole with a diameter. OSH Park's two-layer page states a 6mil trace, 6mil space, 10mil drill and a **5mil annular ring**, and no pad diameter at all; its four-layer page states 5mil, 5mil, 10mil and a **4mil ring**, again no diameter. PCBWay's capabilities page states a minimum drill and a **0.15mm (6mil) annular ring**, and no pad size. The IPC classes are a design standard rather than a fabricator and have no capability page to read.
- **Every one of those numbers was this project's own arithmetic, rounded.** `min_drill_size + 2 * min_annular_ring` against what the table carried: OshPark2Layer 0.508 against 0.508, PcbWayStandard 0.500 against 0.500, IpcClass1 0.600 against 0.600 - and OshPark4Layer 0.454 against 0.450, IpcClass2 0.504 against 0.500, IpcClass3 0.454 against 0.450, which are the same figure rounded to two decimals. `PadLandRule` was refusing boards against a floor no fabricator published, in that fabricator's name.
- **`DesignConstraints::min_pad_size` is `Option<Nm>` now**, which is the pattern this crate already uses for `min_via_diameter`, `min_silk_clearance` and `min_courtyard_clearance`: `None` means the fab did not state one and the checker derives it. JLCPCB keeps its four published figures. `DesignRules::from_constraints` resolves through `land_minimum` - published, else `derived_land` - and `cypcb check` names a derived one: `oshpark_2layer does not state a minimum pad size. The 0.508mm above is this tool's own value, not the fab's.` The numbers barely move; whose they are moves entirely.
- **A regression I introduced and the suite caught before it left.** `min_via_diameter` used to fall back to `min_pad_size`, and I pointed the fallback at the derived land instead - which re-derived JLCPCB's 0.554mm and made a via at its published 0.5mm illegal again, the exact thing D6 had fixed. `drc_sandbox::via_diameter::multiple_vias_mixed` failed `left: 3, right: 2`. Both fields go through `land_minimum` now, so a published land is believed once rather than twice.
- **Three of the four presets `two_numbers_from_the_same_table` reported as having a land-versus-ring tension never had one.** The 2000nm shortfalls on `IpcClass2`, `IpcClass3` and `OshPark4Layer` were the rounding step in a derived number, not two rows of a table disagreeing. The pinned list is now `JlcpcbStandard2Layer 27000` alone, and that one is real: a published 0.5mm land on a published 0.3mm drill leaves 0.1mm where the same page asks for 0.127mm. The test asserts the other three publish no land and that a derived land leaves exactly the ring it came from.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-rules --test two_numbers_from_the_same_table -- --nocapture` -> **2 passed**, printing seven presets as `derived` and four JLCPCB rows with figures. `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test whose_number_is_the_pad_land` -> **2 passed** - a 0.4mm land on a 0.25mm hole gives `0.508mm required` plus the note under `oshpark`, and `0.500mm required` with no note under `jlcpcb`. Mutation, restoring OSH Park's land as a stated number -> `a number this tool computed must not read as the fab's`, 1 failed. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **PCBWay's minimum drill is the next number with the same shape.** Its capabilities page states 0.15mm for CNC drilling and the preset carries 0.2mm - which may be the difference between what the machine can do and what the standard process quotes, exactly like the land-versus-ring pairing D6 settled, or may be a figure nobody checked. One page-read decides it, and the same question is unasked for every other drill and ring in the four non-JLCPCB tables.

- DONE: **`cypcb export` checked every board on its way out against the wrong list, and the reason is that `--preset` means two different things in one binary.** On `check`, `route`, `score` and `watch` it names a **design-rule** table - what a house can etch - and knows ten fabs. On `export` it names a **file convention** - what a house wants the Gerbers called - and knows two. The pre-export check built its rules from the export flag, so the two lists were read as one.
- **Two ways that went wrong, and neither was visible.** `--preset oshpark` is refused by `resolve_preset` long before the check runs, so a board written for OSHPark could only ever be measured against JLCPCB on the way out. And `pcbway` is the one name both lists share, so exporting with it quietly changed which rules the board was measured against - a file-naming choice moving the design-rule table, which nobody asked for and nothing said.
- **The board decides now**, the same way it decides for `cypcb check`: `crate::preset_choice::resolve(None, &world)?.rules()`. The export flag keeps its default and its own list, and its help text says which question it answers rather than leaving the two spellings to be told apart by whoever reads the source.
- Proof, read off the warning the command already prints - two traces 0.14mm apart, a gap JLCPCB images and OSHPark does not. Same geometry, same flag: a board naming no fab -> **4 violations**, `fab oshpark` -> **7**. Same board, two conventions: `--preset jlcpcb` and `--preset pcbway` -> the same count. `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test the_export_check_reads_the_board_not_the_flag` -> **2 passed**. Mutation, restoring the flag as the rules source -> **2 failed**, `left: 4, right: 7` in both, which is the old behaviour caught from both directions at once.
- **`docs/SYNTAX.md` was understating by one command and two surfaces.** It said `check`, `route`, `score` and `watch` read `fab`; `export` reads it always, and the editor and the language server read it and fall back out loud. Written down rather than left for the next reader to discover the doc was true when it was written and is not now.
- Proof: `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`. The first run failed on `cargo-clippy` - `doc list item without indentation`, five errors from one line of the new test's module doc that began with `- ` and swallowed the paragraph under it. Reflowed, not silenced.
- NEXT-ACTION: none on `fab` or on the two meanings of `--preset`. Every command that checks a board reads the board, and the one flag that still means something else says so in its own help.
- DONE: **the layer comparison has a real sample now, and the importer and the writer both pass it.** The per-layer test written for the F.Cu finding could only run on the benchmark fixtures, and those hold one copper segment between them - they are placed and unrouted, which is what makes them benchmarks. The tracker's second option was to route one in-house, write it back as KiCad, and count what came out. That is done, in `cypcb-cli` rather than `cypcb-kicad`, because routing is what makes the copper and only this crate has the router.
- **`plane_board` routed writes 204 segments: 143 on F.Cu, 61 on B.Cu.** Both sides carry copper, nothing landed on a layer the two-layer board does not have, and `parse-kicad` counts the same 204 the file's own text does. Counted out of the text rather than through this project's reader, because using the reader to say what the writer produced compares the pair with itself.
- **What that settles and what it does not.** At 204 segments the writer puts copper on the layer it says and the importer reads it back the same - so neither half is misassigning layers on a board this project routed itself. The owner's F.Cu finding is still not reproduced, and it is now narrowed to two remaining readings: something specific to that board, or the renderer drawing on a layer the model does not put copper on. The importer is the half this measurement can now speak for.
- Proof: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-cli --test routed_copper_lands_on_the_layer_it_says -- --nocapture` -> **1 passed**, printing `{"B.Cu": 61, "F.Cu": 143}, 204 segments total`. Mutation, pointing the KiCad writer's `Layer::BottomCopper` arm at `"F.Cu"`: `no bottom copper was written: {"F.Cu": 204}`, 0 passed 1 failed. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **177 suites ok, zero failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-cli --all-targets` clean.
- The test asserts its own sample size - over 100 segments - because a routing change that collapsed the output would otherwise leave the comparison passing on nothing, which is the failure two tests in this project have already had.
- NEXT-ACTION on F.Cu: **the renderer, or the owner's board.** The cheap half is one command on the board that showed it - `grep -c '(segment' ` per layer - and settles whether the file itself carries that copper. The other half is the viewer's own draw path: the model says which layer a trace is on and the canvas draws it, so a fan of diagonals present on screen and absent from KiCad's plot is either geometry the model holds and KiCad does not, or geometry the canvas invents. Nothing local can separate those two without the board.
- DONE: **two numbers from the same fab table were measured against each other, and one pair does not agree.** D6 made `min_pad_size` load-bearing - it is the floor under a via's land and a through-hole pad's, checked by `ViaDiameterRule` and `PadLandRule` - while the ring around that land is a separate number checked by `AnnularRingRule`. Both come from the same capability page and nothing had ever asked whether a via can satisfy both at once.
- **A via at the stated land, drilled at the stated minimum, has a ring of `(land - drill) / 2`.** Measured across all ten presets:

  | preset | land | drill | ring wanted | ring implied | short |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | JlcpcbStandard2Layer | 0.500 | 0.300 | 0.127 | 0.100 | **0.027** |
  | JlcpcbStandard4Layer | 0.450 | 0.200 | 0.100 | 0.125 | - |
  | JlcpcbAdvanced2Layer | 0.350 | 0.150 | 0.100 | 0.100 | - |
  | JlcpcbAdvanced4Layer | 0.350 | 0.150 | 0.100 | 0.100 | - |
  | PcbWayStandard | 0.500 | 0.200 | 0.127 | 0.150 | - |
  | OshPark2Layer | 0.508 | 0.254 | 0.127 | 0.127 | - |
  | OshPark4Layer | 0.450 | 0.254 | 0.100 | 0.098 | 0.002 |
  | IpcClass1 | 0.600 | 0.300 | 0.127 | 0.150 | - |
  | IpcClass2 | 0.500 | 0.250 | 0.127 | 0.125 | 0.002 |
  | IpcClass3 | 0.450 | 0.200 | 0.127 | 0.125 | 0.002 |

- **Three of the four shortfalls are 0.002mm, which is mils rounded into millimetres rather than a disagreement.** The fourth is `JlcpcbStandard2Layer` at **0.027mm**, and it is the preset this project ships as its default. A via built to that table - 0.5mm land on a 0.3mm drill - passes `PadLandRule` and fails `AnnularRingRule`, using nothing but the fab's own published numbers.
- **Not a bug fixed here, and saying which it is matters.** Two rows of a capability table are usually written about different processes, and the honest reading is that the land minimum pairs with a smaller drill than the minimum via drill. What was missing was anybody having looked. It is written down now, with the number, so a user who hits two rules disagreeing about a board built to spec finds it recorded rather than discovering it.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-rules --test two_numbers_from_the_same_table -- --nocapture` -> **2 passed**, printing the table above. The set of presets with a shortfall is pinned by name and by nanometre, so a table edit that changes which fabs carry the tension fails here rather than surfacing on somebody's board. A shortfall of a tenth of a millimetre or more fails outright, as that would be a mistyped table rather than two process rows. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **176 suites ok, zero failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-rules --all-targets` clean.
- NEXT-ACTION: **this is where D6's outstanding page-read starts, and now it has a target.** Five presets carry a `min_pad_size` nobody has checked against its own published source. The measurement says to begin with JLCPCB standard 2-layer, because it is the default and the only one whose two numbers are more than a rounding apart: find the row that pairs a hole with a diameter, and either the land belongs to a smaller drill - in which case the pairing should be written into the comment beside it - or one of the two figures is wrong.
- DONE: **the Workspace 0x0 finding is a measurement artefact, and the next action written for it last fire was aimed at the wrong half.** That action said to read the section's CSS against the `display:none` it starts with, on the theory that it was being un-hidden without a display mode its grid children need. `.pm-section` sets no `display` at all, so `style.display = ''` - which is what `updateProjectFiles` does - falls back to a div's default `block`. There was nothing there to fix.
- **What produces the symptom is an ancestor.** `#project-manager.hidden { display: none; }`, and every descendant of a hidden element measures zero on both axes while keeping its children. That is exactly "23 cards in a box measuring zero by zero", and `viewer/e2e/the-workspace-section-has-a-size.spec.ts` reproduces it directly rather than arguing it: 23 `.pm-project-item` children present, section box `0 x 0`, overlay hidden.
- **Measured, not reasoned.** Un-hidden the way the real code does it and given one card, the section computes `display: block`, its list computes `display: grid`, and both have real extent - over 100px wide and over 20px tall. Its starting state returns a null bounding box, which is the design and is asserted so a future reader does not read that zero as the fault either.
- **What this does not establish, stated rather than glossed:** which of the two states the app was actually in when the 23 files were seen. Nobody recorded it. What is settled is that the section's own CSS can produce extent and that a hidden overlay reproduces the symptom exactly, so the remaining reading is that the measurement was taken through a closed project manager. If it is seen again with the overlay open, this file is the place to add the case.
- Proof: `npx playwright test e2e/the-workspace-section-has-a-size.spec.ts` -> **3 passed**. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none. The item leaves the open list as measured rather than fixed, which is a different outcome from both "done" and "still broken" and is worth the distinction.
- DONE: **the F.Cu next action was run, and the answer is that the fixtures cannot answer it.** The open finding is the owner's board: the viewer draws copper on F.Cu that `kicad-cli pcb export svg --layers F.Cu` has no trace of. The recorded action was to count `(segment ...)` per layer in the file and compare with what the importer produces, because the difference names whether the importer or the renderer is at fault. `crates/cypcb-kicad/tests/which_layer_the_copper_lands_on.rs` does exactly that, over the six benchmark boards, counting the file's own text rather than going through this project's reader - a comparison made with the importer would be the importer compared with itself, which is the closed loop that let a class of KiCad faults ship until real KiCad was installed.
- **Every KiCad file this project ships holds five copper segments between them, and five of the nine have none at all.** `led_blink` has 1, `stm32_breakout`, `multi_ic`, `shift_driver`, `plane_board` and `qfp_fanout` have 0; `kicad10-slotted` has 2, `minimal` and `slotted` one each. The benchmark boards are placed and unrouted, which is what makes them benchmarks - and it means the layer comparison runs on a sample of **one segment**. A green tick there is not evidence about the importer, so `the_fixtures_carry_almost_no_copper_and_that_is_worth_saying` asserts the sample size outright. If somebody routes a fixture it fails, and the number gets raised deliberately rather than the coverage quietly changing underneath.
- **The sample the fixtures do not provide is now built rather than found.** `a_board_written_here_comes_back_on_the_layers_it_left_on` puts one trace on each copper layer through `write_board`, counts the layers in the **text**, then reads the file back and compares all three. Checking the reader against the writer alone would pass a pair that agreed on the wrong layer; the text in the middle is what stops that.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-kicad --test which_layer_the_copper_lands_on` -> **3 passed**. Mutation, replacing every `"B.Cu"` literal in `board_writer.rs` with `"F.Cu"` (5 occurrences): `one top trace was written; the file says {"F.Cu": 2}`, 2 passed 1 failed. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **174 suites ok, zero failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-kicad --all-targets` clean.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION on the F.Cu finding, narrowed: **the importer is not exonerated, it is untested**~~ - it is tested now, on 204 routed segments, and it passes. See the entry above. Two ways, in order of cost: route a benchmark fixture in-house, export it to KiCad, and run the same per-layer comparison on the result - which needs `cypcb-autoroute` where this test lives, so it belongs in `cypcb-cli`'s suite rather than here; or ask the owner to re-open the board that showed it and count the file's `(segment ...)` per layer directly, which is one `grep -c` and settles it without any new code.
- DONE: **a finding the tracker was told it had and did not.** Commit `af6b5e2`'s message says it recorded two open items here - the F.Cu one, and that the project manager's Workspace section renders 23 files into a box measuring zero by zero. Only the first is in this file. The second is written down now, below, and the lesson is the cheaper one: a commit message claiming a file was updated is not a check that it was.
- **OPEN, from the same session as the F.Cu finding: the Workspace section of the project manager lays 23 files into a box measuring 0 by 0.** `#pm-projects-section` is hidden by default and shown only when the dev server's WebSocket connects, and its grid is populated by `onFileList`. Measured on the running app, the section's own box came back zero on both axes with 23 cards inside it - so the files are in the DOM and none of them is reachable. NEXT-ACTION: ~~read the section's CSS against the `display:none` it starts with~~ - done, and the theory in it was wrong. See the entry above: the section's CSS is fine and a hidden overlay reproduces the symptom.
- DONE: **D6 turned into two changes, and one of them makes the checker less strict rather than more.** `min_pad_size` had been in every fab preset since the tables were written and read by nothing, held deliberately at D6 until the owner settled what it governs: the land around a **hole**, meaning a via's pad and a through-hole pad, not an SMD land.
- **A stated land minimum is now believed instead of derived from.** `min_via_diameter` was `min_via_drill + 2 * min_via_annular_ring` whenever a fab did not state one - which for JLCPCB is `0.3 + 2 x 0.127 = 0.554mm` against the 0.5mm it publishes. The derived figure is the land for the smallest hole the fab makes, not a floor under all of them, so it over-constrained every board by 0.054mm. Per-via ring width is `AnnularRingRule`'s question and is still asked of every via.
- **`PadLandRule` asks the same question of a drilled pad**, which nothing did. `ViaDiameterRule` has always measured a via's land; a through-hole pad is the same copper around the same kind of hole and was reported by nobody. New `ViolationKind::PadLand` with slug `pad-land`, a constructor naming the pin and the hole it is around, and the viewer entry so the error panel names it rather than falling back to the raw slug. Rule registry: 26.
- **It is not the annular ring, and a test holds the difference.** `AnnularRingRule` measures `(land - drill) / 2` against the ring that keeps copper attached when the hole wanders off centre. This measures the land against the smallest one the fab will image. A 0.4mm land on a 0.1mm drill has a comfortable 0.15mm ring and is still under anything JLCPCB will make - `a_generous_ring_does_not_excuse_a_small_land` asserts the ring passes before asserting the land fails.
- **One existing test encoded the old derived figure and was updated rather than worked around.** `drc_sandbox::via_diameter::multiple_vias_mixed` had a via at 0.3mm drill and 0.5mm land commented `Too small: min is 0.554mm`; it is legal now, the count moved 3 -> 2, and both that comment and `normal_via_ok`'s were rewritten to say what the number is and why, so the file does not carry a stale explanation of a rule it no longer describes.
- **The SMD test passed vacuously twice before it measured anything, and both causes are worth naming.** First it built a world with no footprint table, so `library.get` returned `None` for every part and the rule walked nothing. Then it used the built-in 0402, whose land is **not** under 0.5mm - so it would have passed whether or not the rule measured SMD pads. It now builds a pad 0.25mm across, which is JLCPCB's published SMD floor and the exact figure the D6 measurement turned on, and asserts that premise before asserting the result.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --lib rules::pad_land` -> **4 passed**. Mutation, letting SMD pads through the rule: `an_smd_pad_has_no_land_to_measure` fails, 3 passed 1 failed - and on the first two versions of that test the same mutation did **not** fail, which is how the vacuity was caught. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **173 suites ok, zero failed**. `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-drc --all-targets` clean, `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION, now with a target measured rather than a list to work through: **the other five presets state a land and none of them was checked against its own published page.** `min_pad_size` is now load-bearing for every board checked against OSHPark, PCBWay and the three IPC classes, and those figures were carried into the tables when nothing read them. The same page-read that settled D6 for JLCPCB - find the row that pairs a hole with a diameter - is what each of them needs before the number is trusted. Until then a board checked against a non-JLCPCB preset is being measured with a figure nobody has verified.
- DONE: **a slot on a turned part is measured against the side it actually points at.** The gap left open by the previous commit: `holes_of` rotates a slot's travel with the pad's position, and nothing exercised a non-zero rotation - the same shape of gap that produced `a_rotated_part_keeps_its_angle` in `cypcb-kicad` after it had already shipped there.
- **The rotation code was already right, so this fire bought coverage rather than a fix, and saying so is the point.** One 0402 sits 1.5mm above the slot's centre and is measured twice. Unturned, the slot runs left to right and reaches 0.5mm across its width, so the chip is clear at 0.7mm. Turned 90 degrees the same slot runs up and down and reaches 1.2mm along its length, so the same chip is 0.0mm away and JLCPCB's 0.3mm is gone. Same board, same copper, two answers.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --test copper_beside_a_milled_slot` -> **6 passed**. Mutation, turning the second board back to `Rotation::ZERO`: `turned 90 degrees the same slot points at the same chip: []`, 1 failed - so the assertion discriminates on rotation rather than on anything else. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **173 suites ok, zero failed**, `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-drc --all-targets` clean.
- The quality gate was not run for this one and the reason is written here rather than left to be guessed: the change is a single Rust test file, with no build script, no viewer bundle and no shell script touched. Skipping it also keeps `viewer/pkg` out of a commit that has nothing to do with it.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this rule. Copper, courtyard, containment and rotation are each held by a test that fails without the code that satisfies it.
- DONE: **the slot rule shipped with two faults and the second one is the one that mattered.** The recorded next action was the courtyard: a component sits in the spatial index as its assembly keepout, so a part whose body overhangs a slot while its copper stays clear would be reported. That was real, and `component_pads` - the collector `ClearanceRule` already had for exactly this - is now shared between the two rules rather than duplicated.
- **Writing the test for it found a worse one. `box_to_slot_gap` measured edge to edge, which is only the answer for two shapes that do not overlap.** A box that swallows the slot whole has every edge far from the axis, so copper lying **directly on top of a milled opening** read as several millimetres clear. Measured on a real courtyard rather than argued: a DIP-8 is 9.72 x 10.66mm, and a 2.4mm slot at its centre came back **3.9mm clear**. The rule's worst possible answer, on its worst possible input.
- **It was found by a mutation that refused to fail.** The courtyard test was written, it passed, and it passed just as happily against the code it was written to break - which is the signal that the test is vacuous or the fault is elsewhere. Dumping the actual spatial entries settled it in one run: the DIP-8 courtyard is `5.140,4.670 .. 14.860,15.330` and covers the slot at 10,10 completely, so containment was the case the geometry never handled. A guard for a slot end inside the box returns `-radius`, and a unit test now holds that boundary directly.
- **Both fixes are mutation-checked separately, because either alone leaves a hole.** Removing the containment guard: `a_box_that_swallows_the_slot_whole_does_not_read_as_clear` fails, 4 passed 1 failed. Removing the pad refinement: `a_body_over_the_slot_with_its_copper_clear_is_not_reported` fails with `U1 <-> J1: Copper is 0.000mm from a milled slot, 0.300mm required` - the courtyard reported as copper, in the checker's own words.
- **Measured and deliberately not changed: traces need no refinement of this kind.** `rebuild_spatial_index_with_traces` indexes copper **one entry per segment** with the half-width already in the box, so a trace's entry is already close to the copper it stands for. The over-report a whole-polyline box would cause does not exist, and that was checked in `world.rs` rather than assumed.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --lib rules::slot_clearance` -> **5 passed**; `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --test copper_beside_a_milled_slot` -> **5 passed**; `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **173 suites ok, zero failed**, twice in a row; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-drc --all-targets` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- **Observation recorded rather than swept: `cypcb-cli --test a_saved_design_is_checked_again` failed once under `-j 12`, taking 60.04s, and passes alone in 0.41s.** Two full workspace runs afterwards were clean. A 150x runtime under parallelism is contention, not this change - but it is written down here because a suite that fails once every few runs is how a real regression gets dismissed as flake. If it recurs, the thing to look for is a shared path or port between that test and its neighbours.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: none in this rule. It measures the copper rather than the courtyard, it does not report a part for its own annulus, and it does not call copper over an opening clear. What is untested is a slot on a **rotated** part - `holes_of` rotates the slot's travel and nothing here exercises a non-zero rotation, which is the shape of defect the `a_rotated_part_keeps_its_angle` test in `cypcb-kicad` was written for after the same gap shipped there.~~ Covered, see above.
- DONE: **D7 turned into a rule: `SlotClearanceRule` measures another net's copper against a milled opening.** The owner settled the meaning on 2026-08-09 - `min_slot_clearance` is the routed-edge question asked of a slot - and the number had been sitting in every fab preset since the tables were written with nothing reading it. Same shape as `min_paste_clearance` before `PasteClearanceRule` existed: thirteen appearances inside `cypcb-rules` and none anywhere else.
- **The objection that killed this rule the first time is what most of the test file is about.** A plated slot has its own annulus around it by construction, so a rule that measures every piece of copper against every slot fires on every slotted pad ever drawn. The rule skips the slot's own component and anything on a net that component connects to, which is what `HoleToHoleRule` and every clearance rule already do. Two of the four integration tests are that case and they are the ones a naive version fails.
- **A slot is a capsule, not a circle, and the gap is measured from its wall.** `box_to_slot_gap` runs each of the four sides of a spatial entry's box against the slot's axis and takes the bit's radius off, so a box level with the middle of a long slot is measured against its side rather than its nearest end. A 2.4 x 1.0mm slot has a 1.4mm axis and a 0.5mm radius: copper 1.6mm out along the length is 0.4mm from the wall, where measuring from the centre would report 1.6mm.
- **`DesignRules` gained `min_slot_clearance`**, mapped from the constraint set the presets already carried. `ViolationKind::SlotClearance`, its `Display` slug `slot-clearance`, a `DrcViolation::slot_clearance` constructor naming both sides through `other_entity`, and the viewer's `violation-kinds.ts` entry so the error panel names it rather than falling back to the raw slug.
- **The first version of the integration test passed vacuously and the guard against that is now in the file.** Nothing rebuilt the spatial index, so three of four tests were measuring an empty world; only the one asserting a violation failed, which is what exposed it. `slot_faults` rebuilds the index itself and asserts it is non-empty before running DRC, because a test that measures nothing is worse than no test.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --lib rules::slot_clearance` -> **4 passed**, `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --test copper_beside_a_milled_slot` -> **4 passed**. Mutation, removing the own-component skip: **all four fail**, and the report says `J1 <-> J1: Copper is 0.000mm from a milled slot, 0.300mm required` - the annulus reported against its own slot, which is the objection stated in the checker's own words. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` clean after the `DesignRules` doctest was given the new field, `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-drc --all-targets` clean, `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`. Rule registry: 25.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the rule measures a bounding box, and for a component that box is the courtyard.**~~ Fixed, and a worse fault found with it - see above. `ClearanceRule` already learned this the hard way and pre-collects per-pad copper through `component_pads`, because measuring a trace against a part's courtyard reads ordinary assembly as a dead short. This rule measures the courtyard, so a part whose body overhangs a slot while its copper does not will be reported. Nothing in the six benchmark boards has a slot, so no measurement moved - which is exactly why this is written down rather than assumed harmless. The fix is the same one `ClearanceRule` uses, and the test that would catch it is a part placed with its body over a slot and its pads clear.
- DONE: **the crates write down what they are doing and nobody was listening.** `cypcb-autoroute` alone carries **76** `tracing` calls - iterations run, whether the loop converged, which nets were abandoned, which variant failed and was dropped from the ranking - and no command installed a subscriber, so all of them went nowhere and `RUST_LOG` did nothing. The binary installs one now: warnings by default on stderr, `-v` for info, `-vv` debug, `-vvv` trace, and `RUST_LOG` overriding all of it for a reader who wants one crate. Proven: `cypcb route led_blink.kicad_pcb --fast -v` prints `All nets routed successfully segments=21 vias=4 iterations=1 converged=true`, and the same run without `-v` prints exactly what it printed before.
- `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/the_logs_reach_a_person.rs` holds all four halves: `-v` shows the router's own account, the default run carries no `INFO` and still prints `Wrote ...`, `RUST_LOG=cypcb_autoroute=info` reaches the router, and **stdout stays pure JSON at `-vvv`** - `score` and `parse` print their answer there and one log line in the middle makes it unparseable. Mutation-checked with `main.rs` reverted: three of the four fail, and the one that guards the quiet default passes both ways, which is what it is for.
- DONE: **`cypcb route` had two branches and only one of them read the flags.** A `.cypcb` board is routed eight ways and the best is kept unless `--fast` is given; `route_kicad` called `route_board` with `AutorouteConfig::default()` whatever was asked, so `--variants` and `--fast` were accepted and ignored on every KiCad board. Measured on `multi_ic`, release build, byte-identical output for all three invocations before the fix: `--fast` 22.09s, `--variants` 22.57s, no flag 22.47s, each **945 segments and 119 vias**.
- **What that cost the user, measured on the same board:** one default run gives **291 DRC violations, 187 shorts**, 119 vias in 5.88s; best of eight gives **165 violations, 86 shorts**, 167 vias in 86.03s, winner `PathFinder Pad Aware`. `PathFinder Default` - the setting every KiCad board was getting - ranked **sixth of the eight** on this board. Nearly twice the shorts, and no flag that could change it.
- `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/a_kicad_board_gets_the_router_the_flags_ask_for.rs` routes `led_blink.kicad_pcb` both ways and reads what came back: without `--fast` the run has to say it scored several variants and which it kept, with `--fast` it must not, and both have to write a board with copper on it. Mutation-checked with only `route.rs` reverted: `a KiCad board gets the same best-of-N a .cypcb board gets` and `best-of-N has to score more than one candidate and say so` both fail, and the `--fast` test still passes - which is the shape of the defect, one branch working and one not.
- The two test files that route a KiCad board only to have a routed board - the drill-pass split and the KiCad round trip - now pass `--fast` and say why in a comment, since best-of-eight would cost them eight runs of something they do not measure.
- Checked rather than assumed: the variant list prints in an order that is not sorted by the composite it shows, and that is correct - `generate_variants` sorts by unrouted, then shorts, then composite, with the reasoning written above the comparator.
- DONE: **a flag this run cannot honour is refused by name.** `--dry-run` says "export DSN only, don't run FreeRouting"; on a KiCad board it routed the whole thing and wrote it out - `Wrote led_blink.routed.kicad_pcb (21 segments, 4 vias) in 0.03s` - and the same on any `--in-house` run. `--freerouting` named a jar nothing started and `--max-passes` a number nothing read. All three are refused on both built-in-router paths now, named in the message, with the reason and what to do instead. `--timeout` cannot join them and the code says why: it carries a default, so a user who typed `--timeout 300` is indistinguishable from one who typed nothing.
- `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/a_flag_that_cannot_be_honoured_is_refused.rs`: the refusal names the flag, gives the reason, exits non-zero and leaves **no routed board behind**; two ignored flags are both named; and the runs that use no such flag still work on both paths. Mutation-checked with only `route.rs` reverted - three fail, and `a_run_without_those_flags_is_untouched` passes, which is what says the test is about the refusal and not about routing. The FreeRouting path itself is untouched: `cypcb route blink.cypcb --dry-run` still stops at `Dry run complete. DSN file ready for manual routing.` and writes `blink.dsn`.
- **The `--fast` doc comment was quoting a board that no longer exists.** It said the default setting ranks "fourth on every benchmark board" and quoted `multi_ic` at 248/106 against 317/166; today's measurement on that fixture is 291/187 against 165/86 with the default sixth of eight. The old figures predate the fixture repairs - the comma that put two parts 50mm off the board and the header that ran past its edge. Corrected, with the date and the reason the old numbers are gone.
- DONE: **the same audit run across the other commands, and `score` could not read a KiCad board at all.** Every flag was exercised on both kinds of file rather than read about: `check` honours `--no-drc` and `--preset` on both (`led_blink.kicad_pcb` 10 violations against jlcpcb_2layer, 10 against pcbway_standard, `OK ... (DRC skipped)`); `export` honours `--dry-run`, `--no-assembly` and `--force` on both (13 files written, 10 with `--no-assembly`, 0 with `--dry-run`, both kinds identical). `score` and `parse` were the two that did not go through `board_source`.
- **`cypcb score board.kicad_pcb` printed thirty-five parse diagnostics**, each of them wrong about a file that is not - `× Missing a net name` pointing at `(net 1 "VCC")`, which is what a KiCad net looks like. The benchmark fixtures this project measures its router with are all KiCad files, so the one command whose whole job is to put a number on a routed board could not read a single one of them. It goes through `board_source` now: `cypcb score led_blink.kicad_pcb` prints the eight metrics, and its `drc_violations` is **10**, the same number `cypcb check --preset jlcpcb` reports for that board - asserted in the test, not eyeballed.
- **`--weights` was a hidden option that did nothing.** Documented as "future-proofing - currently uses equal defaults", and the scoring call has always used `ScoreWeights::default()`. Removed; the inline test that asserted it parses now asserts the preset default instead, which is the field that decides the number.
- **`parse` gets the opposite treatment**, because there is a `parse-kicad`: one line naming it rather than a page of diagnostics. The first version of that line wrapped `parse-kicad` into `parse-` and `kicad` across two terminal rows - a suggested command that does not work when copied - because it printed the long path twice. The path goes last now and the comment says why.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test score_reads_a_kicad_board_too` -> 4 passed, and with `score.rs` and `parse.rs` reverted **all four fail**; workspace **123 suites, 1482 passed, 0 failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean.
- DONE: **the checker allowed a board OSHPark would have refused.** Auditing the CLI surface - which is clean: every flag on every command is read, and every command exits 1 on a bad file, a semantic error and a missing file - turned up something behind it. `DesignRules::from_constraints` builds the checker's rules from the same fab table the router is priced from, and it **threw one field away**: `min_hole_to_hole` was pinned at 0.5mm for every fab while the constraints carried the real number. OSHPark requires **0.635mm**, so a board with holes half a millimetre apart passed `cypcb check --preset oshpark` and would have come back from the fab; JLCPCB's advanced process allows 0.4mm and the checker demanded 0.5, failing boards that were fine. Measured on two parts 0.5mm apart, after: `jlcpcb -> 0 findings, jlcpcb_advanced -> 0, oshpark -> J1 ↔ J2: Hole-to-hole violation: 0.50mm actual, 0.64mm required`. Before, all three said nothing.
- `crates/cypcb-drc/tests/one_fab_one_set_of_numbers.rs` is what found it and what pins it: for every fab both tables know, the ten fields they share have to hold the same number. It failed on five presets before the fix - `min_hole_to_hole is 500000nm to the checker and 635000nm to the router` and four more - and the whole workspace passes with the fab's own value in place, ratchets included.
- **The two preset registries are still two.** `cypcb_drc::Preset` has 8 fabs including `prototype`; `cypcb_rules::RulesPreset` has 10 including `ipc1`, `ipc2` and `ipc3`, which every CLI command refuses although the rules crate documents them. They already share their numbers - the checker's presets call `RulesPreset::constraints()` - so what is left is the enum, the names (`jlcpcb_2layer` against `jlcpcb_standard_2layer`) and three fields the constraints do not carry: `min_via_diameter`, `min_silk_clearance` and `min_courtyard_clearance`, all three derived in `from_constraints` today. The second test in that file records the split so the day they merge it fails and says what changed.
- DONE: **one preset registry.** There were two: `cypcb_drc::Preset` with eight fabs and `cypcb_rules::RulesPreset` with ten, spelling the same fab differently and each missing what the other had - the checker owned `prototype`, the rules crate documented `ipc1`, `ipc2` and `ipc3` and every CLI command refused them. The numbers were already shared, so the second enum bought nothing but a way to drift, which it had already done once (the hole-spacing fix above). `cypcb_drc::Preset` is now a re-export of `RulesPreset`, and `.rules()` is an extension trait, because the presets belong to the crate that describes fabs and not to the one that checks boards.
- **`prototype` moved without changing what it checks.** It was thirteen hand-written numbers in the checker's table; it is a `DesignConstraints` now, with those thirteen kept exactly and everything a checker does not read - signal integrity, thermal, stackup - taken from IPC Class 1 and said so, rather than invented. `prototype_kept_every_number_it_had` asserts the whole table.
- To do that faithfully, `DesignConstraints` grew the three fields the checker needs and a routing table has no use for - `min_via_diameter`, `min_silk_clearance`, `min_courtyard_clearance` - as `Option<Nm>`. **A fab that does not state a rule is not a fab that requires whatever this project defaults to**: `None` means the checker derives one, which is what it always did, and `prototype` states all three because a via pad big enough to solder by hand is the point of it.
- Measured on the same board: `--preset ipc1`, `ipc3` and `prototype` all check `examples/blink.cypcb` now and report against `ipc_class1`, `ipc_class3` and `prototype`; before, the first two were refused with "Unknown preset". Every name either table ever accepted still resolves, which the merge test walks one by one.
- Two stale count assertions came out with it: `test_all_returns_10_presets` and `assert_eq!(all.len(), 8)` were numbers that had to be edited whenever a preset was added, and say nothing about whether the list is the enum. They walk the list now.
- DONE: **the report says whose number it is.** Three assembly-side rules have no counterpart in a fab's routing table, so when a preset does not state one the checker derives it - and `0.25mm required` reads exactly the same whether the fab published it or this project chose it. `cypcb check` now names the difference, for the rules that actually reported something: `jlcpcb_standard_2layer does not state a courtyard clearance. The 0.250mm above is this tool's own value, not the fab's.` Under `prototype`, which states 0.5mm of its own, the same violation gets no note.
- Printed only for rules that fired, because a note on every run about rules nothing broke is a line nobody reads. Three tests in `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/a_number_the_fab_never_stated_says_so.rs` hold all three halves: the derived rule says so, the stated one is left alone, and a rule that did not fire is not mentioned. Mutation-checked: with the provenance test dropped so every fired rule gets a note, `a_rule_the_preset_states_is_left_alone` fails.
- Observed once and not reproduced: one gate run reported `101 passed` and a failed Playwright stage without naming the spec; the standalone suite and two later gate runs are green at 102. Recorded rather than explained - if it comes back, the name is what to capture.
- DONE: **a four-layer board went to the fabricator claiming L1 twice.** Gerber X2 puts the layer in the file - `TF.FileFunction,Copper,L2,Inr` - and it is how a CAM system knows which foil goes where. Measured by exporting a four-layer board and reading the attribute out of each copper file: `F_Cu` said `Copper,L1,Top`, **`In1_Cu` said `Copper,L1,Inr`**, `In2_Cu` said `Copper,L2,Inr`, `B_Cu` said `Copper,L4,Bot`. Two files claimed L1 and **nothing claimed L3**. The same four files now read L1, L2, L3, L4.
- **Two conventions meeting, and neither was wrong on its own.** `Layer::Inner` is zero-based everywhere in this workspace - the DSL writes `inner1`, `sync.rs` reads it as `Inner(0)`, `to_copper_mask` shifts by `2 + n` - and the export computed the Gerber number as `n + 1`, which is the formula for a one-based index, with a comment saying "L2 for first inner". The comment described what it was supposed to do. `CopperSide::Inner` says which convention it takes now, and its fallback was wrong in the same way.
- **And the same directory of files described two projects.** `copper.rs` and `mask.rs` read the board's size and threw it away for the literal string `"board"` - `world.board_info().map(|(_size, _)| "board")` - while `silk.rs` and `outline.rs` used `world.board_name()`. Measured on one export: `G04 Board: board` in the copper and mask files, `G04 Board: stacked` in the silkscreen and outline. All six read `stacked` now.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-export --test every_copper_file_says_which_layer_it_is` -> 4 passed, and with `n + 1` put back three of the four fail with `left: [1, 1, 2, 4]` against `right: [1, 2, 3, 4]` and `got ["board", "board", "board", "flight_controller", ...]`. The whole crate was green before this test existed: **131 unit tests and six integration suites, and not one of them read a file function out of an exported file.**
- **The first version of the duplicate check passed on the defect.** It deduplicated whole attribute strings, and `Copper,L1,Top` and `Copper,L1,Inr` are different strings for the same layer - which is the fault itself. It compares layer numbers now, and fails on the mutant with `two files claim the same layer - [1, 2, 4]`.
- DONE: **the export writes a Gerber job file, and the stackup finally reaches the person who presses the board.** A directory of Gerbers says what to image on each layer and nothing about the board - how thick it is, what goes between the copper, which file is which. `<board>-job.gbrjob` is Ucamco's answer and this project wrote none: 15 files before, 16 now. Measured on a board declaring a seven-layer stackup: `BoardThickness 1.57`, `LayerNumber 4`, eleven `FilesAttributes` entries, and a `MaterialStackup` that names every layer the design stated.
- **The format was read before it was written.** `gerber-job-format-specification-revision-2020-08` off Ucamco, converted with `pdftotext` and grepped, rather than copied from memory - which caught two things a plausible implementation gets wrong. A file function spells it `Soldermask` and a material stackup entry spells it `SolderMask`; the first version matched the stackup spelling against a file function and called **every mask Positive**, which is a board that comes back with solder mask over its pads. The specification's own example says `"FileFunction": "Soldermask,Top"` with `"FilePolarity": "Negative"`, and this exporter images the openings, so Negative is right.
- **The other thing the spec is strict about**: *"If the Material Stackup is included, it must be complete - all layers of the PCB, must be present."* A design states its dielectrics and says nothing about mask or silkscreen, so writing the declaration straight through would claim a board with no solder mask in the same file that lists two solder mask Gerbers. The surfaces the export actually wrote are added around the declaration, with no thickness and no colour because neither is known - and a design that does describe its own mask or silk is passed through untouched.
- **What is deliberately absent, because there is no data for it**: `Finish`, `DesignRules`, and `Material` on any dielectric. FR4 is the usual answer and nobody has told this tool. `BoardThickness` appears only when every declared layer states a thickness - a sum missing a term is not a measurement - and `MaterialStackup` only when the design declares one.
- **Nothing in the file is recomputed from what the exporter meant to write.** Each entry's `FileFunction` is read back out of the Gerber it describes, so the job file cannot disagree with the files it ships beside. The same property would have shown last fire's four-layer numbering fault in a second place.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-export --test the_job_file_describes_the_board_it_ships_with` -> 8 passed, including the two controls (no stackup declared, partial thicknesses) and `every_gerber_written_is_named_in_the_job_file`, which compares the array against `read_dir` of the output; `cargo test -p cypcb-export --lib jobfile` -> 4 passed; `cargo run -p cypcb-cli -- export` -> `Export complete: 16 files`, `stk-job.gbrjob (2.8 KB)`, mask polarity `Negative` both sides.
- **One existing test had to be corrected rather than worked around.** `every_example_reaches_the_files` requires every file in the output whose extension starts with `g` to carry `M02*` and `%FSLAX` - and `gbrjob` starts with a g. The job file is JSON describing the Gerbers rather than a Gerber, so the filter was wrong, not the new file: 2 failed with `no end-of-file marker` across every example, 2 passed after excluding the extension by name.
- DONE: **the drill files say what they drill, and the job file names them.** Measured first, as the note below asked: the Excellon files carried no attributes at all - `M48`, a generator comment, coordinates. Every other file this exporter writes has stated a `TF.FileFunction` for as long as it has existed. So the job file described eleven Gerbers and no drill file, which reads as a board with no holes. The drill header now carries `TF.GenerationSoftware`, `TF.CreationDate` and `TF.FileFunction` as `; #@!` comments, which is where NC formats carry Gerber attributes, and the job file reads them back the same way it reads a Gerber - one mechanism, no second source of truth.
- **The span is the half that decides where the holes go.** A through file on a four-layer board states `Plated,1,4,PTH`; the non-plated file states `NonPlated,1,4,NPTH`; a via between the inner pair states `Plated,2,3,Buried` and one from the top to the first inner layer states `Plated,1,2,Blind`. A drill file with no stated span means "through everything" to every fabricator, which is what a buried via file used to look like.
- **The layer numbering now exists once.** `gerber::header::copper_layer_number` is the single place that turns a `Layer` into a Gerber layer number, called by the copper files and by the drill span. Two copies of that arithmetic is exactly how the first inner layer came to claim L1 alongside the top copper last fire.
- **Writing the drill entry exposed a flaw in what shipped last fire.** The job file sat in `gerber/` and named files by bare filename, which cannot resolve `drill/board-PTH.drl` from there. It sits at the root of the output now and names files relative to itself - `gerber/board-F_Cu.gbr`, `drill/board-PTH.drl` - and a test walks every path it claims and requires the file to be there. Every entry also carries `FileFormat` (`Gerber` or `NC`, the specification's word for Excellon), which the first version omitted entirely; a drill file described as a Gerber is one a CAM system tries to read as an image.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-export --test the_drill_files_say_what_they_drill` -> 6 passed; `--test the_job_file_describes_the_board_it_ships_with` -> 11 passed, including `every_path_it_names_resolves_from_where_the_job_file_sits`; `cargo run -p cypcb-cli -- export` -> 16 files, `drill/stk-PTH.drl` described as `Plated,1,4,PTH` in format `NC`, eleven Gerbers with polarity and the drill file without - a file that images nothing has none, and the specification's own drill entry omits it too.
- DONE: **a design can put a part on the back of the board, which it could not do at all.** `sync.rs` carried the note - "the DSL has no word for this yet" - and derived the side from the footprint's copper, so a part was on the bottom only if its footprint had no top-side pads. No footprint in the library is built that way, so **no design could place a part on the bottom**: half of every two-sided assembly was unsayable. `side bottom` says it.
- **The flip is registered once, in the footprint library.** `mirrored_to_bottom` reverses the local x axis - a part turned over, seen from above, which is how every coordinate here is written - and moves every layer it touches to the matching layer on the other face: copper, mask, paste and silk together. `sync_ast_to_world` registers the flipped copy as `<name>@bottom` and points the instance at it, so the checker, the four Gerber writers, the drill file, the renderer and the pick-and-place list all place the mirrored pads without one of them knowing about sides. A mirror written six times is a board whose copper and solder mask disagree about which face a part is on.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test a_part_can_be_put_on_the_other_side` -> 6 passed. A bottom 0402 puts **0 flashes on F_Cu and 2 on B_Cu**, and its mask and paste move with it (0 on F_Mask/F_Paste, 2 on B_Mask/B_Paste); the CPL row says `Bottom`. Two controls: a part that states nothing and a part that states `top` produce byte-identical copper.
- **Mutation-checked, and the failure is the interesting part.** With the instance pointed back at the unflipped footprint, three tests fail with `left: 2, right: 0` on the top copper - but `the_assembly_list_tells_the_machine_which_side_to_place_it_on` still passes, because the CPL reads the `Side` component rather than the copper. That is precisely the shipping hazard this design avoids: a placement machine told `Bottom` while every pad is imaged on the top.
- **The tree-sitter grammar is updated and its generated parser is not.** `grammar.js` gains `side_property` and `parser.rs` gains the arm that reads it, so the second parser is ready; `grammar/src/parser.c` is generated from the grammar and this container has no tree-sitter CLI - `command -v tree-sitter` -> nothing, `npx tree-sitter --version` fails. The optional `tree-sitter-parser` feature is off by default (`default = ["rust-parser"]`), so this changes no build; it does mean **that parser will reject `side` until somebody regenerates it**, and the differential test - which only runs with both features on - would say so. The gate did catch the half of this that matters: `cargo test` compiles the crate with all features, so a `ComponentDef` missing the field failed the build rather than drifting quietly.
- **That NEXT-ACTION was written from a stale fact and the measurement corrected it.** It said the browser builds its own snapshot with `parseSource` in JavaScript, which stopped being true on 2026-08-07 when that reader was deleted and `WasmPcbEngineAdapter.load_source` started calling the engine. So most of the viewer needed nothing: `crates/cypcb-render/tests/a_bottom_part_reaches_the_browser_flipped.rs` drives the engine the way the browser does and a bottom part arrives with **bottom-copper layer bits and pad 1 at +0.5mm instead of -0.5mm**, and `getPadColor` already refuses to draw a bottom pad when the bottom copper is hidden.
- DONE: **the half that really was wrong is the ink.** Silkscreen and the body outline come from a footprint, and a footprint has no side of its own - the part does. The renderer drew both in the top silkscreen colour whatever side the part was on, so a bottom part printed its legend on the top of the board and looked identical to a top-side part whose pads had gone to the wrong layer. `ComponentInfo.side` now rides in the snapshot and `componentSilkColor` decides the colour; a bottom part's legend is drawn in `bottom_silk`.
- **One of the four new browser tests guards the other three.** They compare against `LAYER_COLORS.bottom_silk`, so a palette that gave both sides the same ink would leave all three passing while the screen told nobody anything; the fourth asserts the two colours differ.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-render --features native --test a_bottom_part_reaches_the_browser_flipped` -> 3 passed; `npx vitest run src/__tests__/a-bottom-part-is-drawn-as-one.test.ts` -> 4 passed.
- **The first version of the flip test was wrong about the units and said so loudly.** It subtracted the part's position from a pad coordinate that is already relative to the part, and compared -9.5mm against 10.5mm. The engine was right; the reader was not. Corrected to compare the pad against the part's own centre, with the mistake written down beside it.
- **One question a bottom part raises that this project cannot answer for itself, and will not guess.** The placement file writes the design's rotation unchanged for both faces. Our own geometry is the footprint mirrored in x and then rotated, seen from the top; viewed from the bottom - which is how a machine sees a board it is placing on the underside - that is the unmirrored footprint rotated by **minus** the same angle. So whether a fabricator's CPL wants the angle or its negative for a bottom part is a convention of theirs, not arithmetic of ours, and getting it wrong rotates every bottom part on the board. Recorded for the owner rather than picked: no board here has a rotated bottom part, so nothing is currently wrong.
- DONE: **the KiCad export described a bottom part as a front-side part with its pads on the back.** Measured by writing a board with one part on each face: `(footprint "cypcb:0402@bottom" (layer "F.Cu"))` with its reference on `F.SilkS`, its value on `F.Fab` and its pads on `B.Cu`. Three separate faults in one line - the face, the legend, and the geometry, which came from the mirrored copy this project keeps internally, so KiCad would have flipped it a second time on load. The derived name `0402@bottom` leaked into the file as well, naming a library entry nobody has.
- **The convention was not guessed.** This project's own KiCad reader relies on it and its fixture states it: a back-side footprint in a real board file carries `(layer "B.Cu")` and pad coordinates **identical** to a front-side one, because KiCad mirrors the geometry itself from the layer. So a bottom part is written from the footprint the design named, with `B.Cu`/`B.SilkS`/`B.Fab`, and `footprint::base_name` keeps the internal suffix out of the file.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-kicad --test a_bottom_part_survives_the_round_trip` -> 5 passed, the strongest of which writes the board and reads it back with this project's own parser: `[("R1", Top), ("R2", Bottom)]`. With the layer choice reverted three of the five fail, and the round trip returns `[("R1", Top), ("R2", Top)]` - the part changed faces on the way through a file.
- DONE: **the KiCad export walked past every zone, and a ground plane is close to every board anybody makes.** Measured on the bundled example: `cargo run -p cypcb-cli -- to-kicad examples/pour-island.cypcb` produced a file with **0** occurrences of `zone` for a design whose whole point is a ground pour, and 8 lines of other geometry. The board opened in KiCad without its ground - the same silence the Gerber writer had before the pour was implemented, one file over. It writes them now: 1 zone for that example, on `F.Cu`, net `GND`, over the rectangle the design states.
- **A pour is written per layer, because that is how KiCad stores one.** `(layers ...)` on a zone is for rule areas rather than for copper, so a design pouring both faces produces two `(zone ...)` blocks; a keepout is written as a zone with nothing allowed in it rather than as filled copper. The shape came from this project's own KiCad reader and its fixture, the same authority the footprint side used.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-kicad --test a_ground_plane_survives_the_round_trip` -> 5 passed, four of them a round trip through our own parser - the plane comes back on `GND`, over `2,2` to `38,28`, and a two-face pour comes back as two. With the writer unwired four of the five fail with `one pour went in: []`. The fifth is the control: a board with no zones still writes none.
- DONE: **the two writers disagreed about the silkscreen, and the measurement is the whole finding.** The same design through both: **38 stroked segments** in `pour-island-F_SilkS.gbr` against **0** `fp_line` in the `.kicad_pcb`. Three parts, each an outline in the fabrication files and nothing at all on screen in KiCad - so a board round-tripped through KiCad lost the marks that tell a person where its parts are. The KiCad file carries 12 now, four per part.
- **The rule is shared rather than reinvented.** `gerber/silk.rs` prints a footprint's own artwork when it has any and its courtyard outline when it does not, and `write_legend` follows the same line: artwork if present, otherwise the courtyard as four `fp_line` segments. Drawing both would put a box on the board the footprint never asked for, which is what that comment in the Gerber writer says.
- **Not checked by a round trip, and that is stated rather than papered over.** This project's KiCad reader does not read `fp_line` at all, so there is nothing to read it back with - unlike the pours and the part sides, where the reader made the round trip the strongest available proof. What is checked is the text: four lines per part, on `F.SilkS`, meeting end to end and closing; a footprint with artwork writing its artwork and no courtyard; and a board with no parts writing nothing.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-kicad --test the_board_arrives_with_its_legend` -> 4 passed; with the call unwired three fail with `left: 0, right: 4`.
- **The silkscreen import was measured and dropped rather than built.** The next step recorded last fire was to read `fp_line` back, so the legend could be round-tripped like the pours and the part sides. Counted first: the boards this project actually imports - the six routing benchmarks under `viewer/kicad-tools/boards/` - carry **0** `fp_line` and **0** `fp_circle` between them, because a script generated them. One unrelated third-party file has 25 and 3. So the reader would be tested only against a fixture written to satisfy it, which proves that it can read what I wrote and nothing else. Recorded as measured and not built.
- DONE: **two files out of the same export disagreed about how thick the board is.** `(general (thickness 1.6))` was a literal. A design declaring a stackup that sums to 1.57mm got `1.570mm of material` from the checker, `"BoardThickness": 1.57` in the Gerber job file, and **1.6** in the KiCad board - which is worse than either number being wrong on its own, because whoever notices has to work out which to believe. The KiCad file reads `Stackup::total_thickness` now, the same function the other two use.
- **1.6 stays where it is a default rather than a measurement**: a design that states no stackup, or one whose layers do not all state a thickness, keeps it, because a partial sum is not a thickness. Every example in this repository is in that case and none of them moved.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-kicad --test the_board_is_as_thick_as_the_design_says` -> 4 passed; with the literal restored two fail with `left: "1.6", right: "1.57"`. Two of the four are the controls.
- DONE: **the exported board carried no design rules at all, so KiCad checked it against KiCad's defaults.** Measured: **0** `(setup ...)` blocks in the written file. A designer opening the board and running KiCad's DRC got a different answer than `cypcb check` on the source, with neither tool saying why - the most visible form of the disagreement this export keeps producing. The file now states `min_clearance`, `min_track_width`, `min_via_diameter` and `min_through_hole`, and for `--preset jlcpcb` they read 0.127, 0.127, 0.554 and 0.3, which are the same numbers that preset gives the checker.
- **`to-kicad` was the last command in the CLI that never asked which fabricator a board is for.** `check`, `route`, `score` and `export` all take `--preset`; this one wrote a board for nobody in particular. It takes one now, and this file's older claim that "no command in the CLI guesses a fabricator" is true for the first time in the stronger sense: none of them writes fab-dependent output without being told the fab.
- **Without a preset it still writes no rules, deliberately.** Rules nobody chose are worse than none, because KiCad believes them. `cypcb-kicad` takes the four numbers as a `KicadDesignRules` argument rather than reaching for a preset table: which fab a board is for is the caller's knowledge, and the crate has no opinion about design rules.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test the_kicad_board_carries_the_rules_it_was_checked_with` -> 4 passed - the fab's own numbers, a different fab giving different numbers, no preset giving no `(setup`, and a typo refused by name. With the rules unwired two of the four fail with `no min_clearance in:`.
- DONE: **the solder mask opening was a constant in the file that goes to the fab.** Both mask sites in `job.rs` called `MaskPasteConfig::default()`, whose expansion is a hardcoded 0.05mm, so a house publishing anything else got its openings drawn to the constant - JLCPCB's four-layer rules state 0.04mm and IPC Class 2 states 0.075mm. Silently, because a mask opening a hundredth of a millimetre off looks like every other mask opening. `ExportPreset` carries `mask_expansion` now, the same discipline `silk_clearance` already had.
- **No board in this repository changes today**, and that is the honest headline: both export presets are for houses publishing 0.05mm, which is what the constant happened to be. Same shape as the copper weight - the value was right and the reason it was right was luck. A test in `cypcb-cli` now holds the export preset's number against that house's design rules, two tables in two crates that had no way to disagree loudly.
- **And the fault I wrote last fire is fixed in the same commit.** `(pad_to_mask_clearance 0)` was a literal, written one line under a comment about numbers KiCad believes. It is the fab's expansion now: `--preset jlcpcb` gives `(pad_to_mask_clearance 0.05)`.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-export --test the_mask_opening_is_the_fabs_own` -> 3 passed, including the geometry both ways - a 0402 pad at 0.05mm gives a 0.7mm opening, at 0.04mm a 0.68mm one and no 0.7mm one; `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test the_kicad_board_carries_the_rules_it_was_checked_with` -> 5 passed.
- DONE: **every fab published a paste clearance and nothing read it.** `min_paste_clearance` appeared **thirteen times inside `cypcb-rules` and nowhere else in the workspace** - no checker, no exporter. So a board could put two SMD pads close enough that the steel between their stencil apertures tears, the openings merge and the parts bridge with solder on reflow, and every check passed. `PasteClearanceRule` is the twin of the solder mask bridge rule: same geometry, a different sheet.
- **A paste aperture is the pad itself**, because `paste_reduction` is 0.0 and a reduction is a stencil design decision rather than a number a fabricator publishes. The rule invents nothing: it reads the fab's own figure, 0.127mm for JLCPCB. A through-hole pad has no aperture at all and is skipped.
- **Measured against every bundled board before believing it**: it fires on `drc-test.cypcb` - **3 violations**, on the same overlapping pair the mask bridge rule reports - and on none of the other examples. A rule that fires on every chip resistor is a rule people turn off; an 0402's own pads sit 0.4mm apart, three times the published minimum.
- **The count was wrong the first time I read it.** `grep -c paste-clearance` said 4 because it counted the summary line `paste-clearance: 3` as well as the three violations. Corrected by listing them.
- **The new rule broke a claim the CLI makes about the board, which is the useful part.** `cypcb check` prints `copper touching copper at 0.00mm: N`, counted by filtering on `actual == 0` **regardless of kind** - so the first rule to report a different zero-width thing was counted as a short. A paste stencil web of 0.000mm is a torn stencil, not copper touching copper. The filter selects `ViolationKind::Clearance` now, and `cli_integration` went from 20 passed, 1 failed back to 21 passed.
- The registry is 22 rules - `grep -c 'Box::new(rules::' crates/cypcb-drc/src/lib.rs`. Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --test the_stencil_has_to_survive_being_made` -> 5 passed, including the two controls (a web the fab allows, and a plain 0402 board) and the through-hole case. The viewer guard caught the new kind before the table was touched: `these arrive in the panel as a raw slug: paste-clearance`.
- **Scanned every published constraint for a reader, and fifteen have none.** `blind_vias_allowed`, `board_thickness`, `buried_vias_allowed`, `castellated_holes_allowed`, `diff_pair_gap`, `diff_pair_tolerance`, `max_copper_layers`, `max_drill_aspect_ratio`, `max_stub_length`, `max_vias_per_high_speed_net`, `min_acid_trap`, `min_copper_pour_clearance`, `min_hole_to_edge`, `min_pad_size` and `min_slot_clearance` appear only inside `cypcb-rules`. Command: `grep -rn "\bFIELD\b" --include=*.rs crates/ | grep -v "^crates/cypcb-rules/"` per field.
- DONE: **the first of those is closed, and it was worse than unread - it was contradicted by its own documentation.** `export_copper_layer`'s doc comment states that `ExportJob` passes the fab's clearance through `export_copper_layer_with`. **No caller ever did.** Every exported pour was filled with `PourOptions::default()`, whose 0.3mm is deliberately generous rather than published, so on a JLCPCB board - which publishes 0.254mm - every plane shipped 0.046mm smaller on every edge than the house allows.
- Before and after on `examples/pour-island.cypcb` through the real command: same 7 regions and same 29 draws, with a region edge moving `Y19600000` -> `Y19646000`. Exactly the 0.046mm, and in the direction that gives the designer the plane they asked for.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-export --test the_pour_keeps_the_fabs_distance` -> 3 passed - every coordinate that moves, moves by 46000nm; the plane still clears a foreign pad by the published distance; both presets state what their fab publishes. The cross-crate check sits in `cypcb-cli` beside the mask one, against the constraints table rather than `DesignRules`, because the checker's own view carries no pour clearance - no rule reads one.
- DONE: **the second of the fifteen unread numbers: a hole too near the routed edge.** The board is milled out of a panel and a drilled hole whose wall sits closer to that cut than the fab allows comes out open on one side - a mounting hole with a notch, or a plated hole with no barrel. `min_edge_clearance` cannot answer it: that rule measures **copper**, and a mounting hole has none at all, so a drill with no copper is invisible to it by construction. `HoleToEdgeRule` measures the hole wall - centre plus the drill radius - against the same outline the copper rule uses, through the same `distance_to_outline` rather than a second copy of it.
- **Silent on everything in the repository**, checked before believing it: every `examples/*.cypcb` and all three routed KiCad benchmarks report 0. The rule exists for the board somebody has not written yet, which is what the test fixtures stand in for.
- **The first version of the test could not have shown anything.** It put a 3.2mm hole inside a 3.6mm pad and expected the copper rule to pass while the hole failed - impossible, because a concentric hole is always further from the edge than the copper around it, so the copper rule always fires first. The case that matters is the mounting hole: no copper anywhere, invisible to the copper rule, 0.1mm from the cut.
- The registry is 23 rules. Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --test a_hole_the_router_would_break_into` -> 5 passed, including the boundary either side of the published 0.3mm - a via wall at exactly 0.3mm passes, at 0.29mm it does not - and the control well inside the board.
- **`min_pad_size` was implemented, measured, and thrown away - which is the result.** The third of the fifteen unread numbers reads as "the smallest copper pad the fab will etch", and JLCPCB publishes 0.5mm. Written as a rule over every copper pad's narrow side it was silent on all twenty-odd examples and on two of the three routed benchmarks, and then reported **16 pads on `usb_joystick_routed.kicad_pcb`**: `Pad A1 is 0.250mm across its narrow side, 0.500mm required` and fifteen more, all of them J1 - a USB-C connector. JLCPCB builds USB-C boards every day, and its published trace and space is 0.127mm.
- **So the plain reading is refuted by a board that fab manufactures routinely.** `min_pad_size` is not a limit on SMD land width; it is most likely a minimum via land or through-hole pad diameter, where 0.5mm on a 0.3mm drill is the ordinary figure. That is an inference, not a fact this project holds, and a checker that flags every fine-pitch connector is a checker people turn off. The rule and its field were reverted: registry back to 23, `grep -c "pad-size" ...` on that board back to 0.
- **What this cost and what it bought.** One fire, and the discipline held where it matters: the rule was measured against a real board before it shipped rather than after somebody trusted it. The remaining twelve unread numbers are not all like this - `min_hole_to_edge` and `min_paste_clearance` each mapped to exactly one geometry - but the ones whose meaning is ambiguous now have a worked example of how to find out.
- **The gate caught a third flake, and this one had a stated cause.** `a_saved_design_is_checked_again` passes on its own in **0.41s** and failed inside the full gate at **20.02s** - exactly its own deadline, with the rest of the workspace compiling and testing alongside it. Its docstring says it waits for output rather than sleeping "because a test that fails when the machine is busy teaches people to ignore it", and then it did precisely that. The deadline is 60s now, sized for a loaded machine, and the panic says how long it actually waited. A generous deadline costs nothing when the watcher works: the loop returns the moment the log says what it is waiting for.
- **D6, for the owner**: what does `min_pad_size` govern - via lands, through-hole pads, or something else? Until that is answered the number stays unread, deliberately.
- DONE: **two more of the fifteen closed by one rule - `max_drill_aspect_ratio` and `board_thickness`.** Plating a through hole is chemistry rather than machining: copper is pulled down the barrel out of solution, and past some depth-to-width ratio the solution stops refreshing in the middle. The board comes back with a barrel that is thin or open somewhere nobody can see. Every fab publishes the ratio it still plates - 8:1 on JLCPCB's standard process, 12:1 on its advanced one - and nothing read the number.
- **It could not be written before this week.** Aspect ratio is thickness divided by drill, and the checker had no thickness: `stackup` was parsed and dropped. Now that a declared stackup reaches the model, `DrillAspectRatioRule` takes the depth from the design when the design states one and from the fab's own `board_thickness` when it does not - not from a constant typed into the checker.
- **This is the distinction the rule exists for, and a test pins it:** a 0.3mm via is JLCPCB's own minimum drill, so every rule about the drill alone passes it on any board. Through 1.6mm it is 5.3:1 and ordinary; through 3.2mm it is 10.7:1 and unbuildable. `the_drill_rule_says_nothing_about_this_board` asserts 0 `ViaDrill`/`DrillSize` violations on that board and 1 from this rule.
- **Non-plated holes are not asked to plate.** A mounting hole is drilled and left bare, so there is no copper in it to come out thin; the rule skips `PadDef::is_non_plated`, the same question the drill file asks when it decides which file a hole belongs in. A rule without that skip fires on every screw hole in every thick board - which is what the mutation check showed when the skip was removed.
- **Measured across everything in the repository before believing it**: all 21 `examples/*.cypcb`, the three routed KiCad benchmarks and the six benchmark fixtures report **0**. The smallest drill anywhere in the corpus is 0.3mm through 1.6mm, which is 5.3:1. The rule exists for the board nobody has written yet.
