What was missing is the guard - `cli_integration` asserts the inner files are *named* in the export's output, which an empty file satisfies, and `routed_board_reaches_the_files` only ever covered top and bottom.
- `an_inner_layer_carries_its_own_copper_and_nobody_else_s` routes one trace per layer of a four-layer board at four different heights, so a layer carrying somebody else's copper shows in the coordinates rather than only in a count. **Proven to bite**: making the layer filter treat every inner layer as one - `trace.layer != layer && !matches!(layer, Layer::Inner(_))` - fails it with `In1 carries the trace that belongs to top`, while the old top-and-bottom test passes throughout.
- A four-layer board is where losing a layer is hardest to notice and most expensive: the fabricator builds the stack from what arrives.
- Workspace 113 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **a drilled pad did not exist on the layers it is drilled through, and two readers disagreed about which layer is which.** Pulling on the four-layer example's short found both.
- `sync.rs` gave every through-hole DSL pad exactly `[TopCopper, BottomCopper]` whatever the board declared, so on a four-layer board it was absent from In1 and In2: an inner trace could not reach it, and the checker could not see its copper there. `examples/four-layer.cypcb` - whose whole subject is inner copper - shipped a trace on Inner1 joining two pads it could not touch, and `cypcb check` had been saying so all along with **four pins unreached**.
- **The numbering split was the second half.** `job.rs` writes `Layer::Inner(n)` as `In{n + 1}` and the DSL reads `Inner1` as `Inner(0)`, so the project is zero-based inside - but the KiCad reader read `In1.Cu` as `Inner(1)` and the KiCad writer wrote `Inner(n)` as `In{n}`. An imported inner trace landed one layer deeper than the file said and would have exported under the next layer's name. Both are aligned on zero-based now, and `In0.Cu` is refused rather than read as `Inner(-1)`.
- Measured end to end on the example: **4 unreached pins -> 2**, the two left being the `CROSS` net which has no copper in the file by design. Its routed board went from `1 violations, 1 of them copper touching copper` to `0 violations` with a via, so it exports without `--force` now.
- The pads were also given a drill in the example, which is what the file always meant: a pad with no drill is surface copper on one layer and cannot be joined by an inner trace.
- Every ratchet unmoved, workspace 113 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the chain a four-layer board takes is now checked from the file a person writes to the file a fabricator reads.** Both defects fixed last fire could hide because every test sat on one side or the other: the unit tests check `export_copper_layer` directly, and `cli_integration` checks that the inner files are named. Nothing ran a four-layer `.cypcb` through routing and export and looked at which Gerber the copper landed in.
- `a_four_layer_board_reaches_its_own_layers` does, with no `--force` - a board that needs it is a board that cannot be sent. Measured: `F_Cu` 1 draw, `In1_Cu` 2 draws, `In2_Cu` 0, `B_Cu` 0, and four pad flashes on every one of them.
- **Both defects are caught by it.** Reverting the drilled pad to the outer two layers gives `In1_Cu carries 1 flashes, and the board has four drilled pads that every copper layer passes through`; reading `Inner1` one layer deeper would put the trace in `In2_Cu`, which the third case forbids.
- The first assertion said "4 flashes exactly" and read a legitimate via ring as a defect. It asks for at least four now, which is what it means: a via is free to flash beside the pads.
- **The same shared-scratch-directory race caught me a third time** - three tests, one output directory, deleting each other's files, all of it looking like an export failure. Per-test directories, and the helper now says so in its own doc.
- Workspace 114 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`In2_Cu` carries copper on a board in the repository for the first time.** `examples/four-layer.cypcb` gained a second pair of pads joined on Inner2, so both inner layers now hold a trace the file names: `F_Cu` 1 draw, `In1_Cu` 2, `In2_Cu` 2, `B_Cu` 0, with six pad flashes on every layer. The board still routes at **0 violations** and exports without `--force`.
- **The guard is by coordinate, not by count.** `SIG` sits at y=10mm on Inner1 and `SIG2` at y=5mm on Inner2, so a layer holding the other one's copper shows in the Gerber's own numbers. A draw count would have looked plausible either way - which is exactly how the off-by-one survived.
- Proven: shifting the DSL's `inner1` from `Layer::Inner(0)` to `Inner(1)` fails it with `In1 is missing the trace the file puts at y=10mm`, while the two structural cases beside it pass throughout.
- Workspace 114 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **The router does produce blind and buried vias, which nobody had measured.** On `multi_ic`: 119 vias, **46 of them blind or buried** across four layer pairs - 17 Top..In1, 14 In1..Top, 8 Top..In2, 6 In1..Bottom and one buried **In2..In1** that touches neither face. The claim that no board in the repository has one was wrong; no *example* does, and nothing checked the export.
- DONE: **the drill split is checked on a board the router actually produced.** The only coverage was `a_buried_via_is_not_drilled_from_the_outside`, which builds one via by hand. Routed and exported through the release binary, `multi_ic` writes five drill files - the through pass plus `Top-In1`, `Top-In2`, `In1-Bottom` and `In2-In1` - and the hits sum to **149 = 30 drilled pads + 119 vias**.
- **The first version of the second test asked the wrong question.** "Is any coordinate in two files" cannot tell a via written twice from two different vias the router stacked at one point: two coordinates appear in both the `Top-In1` and through passes, and `cypcb check` names one of them `USB_DP <-> OSC_IN` at 0.00mm - a genuine two-net short through one point, already reported as hole-to-hole. Counting is decisive where matching is not, so the test compares the total hits against the board's own vias and drilled pads.
- Fixed counts are deliberately not asserted: they move whenever routing moves, and a test that fails for that reason teaches people to update it without reading it.
- **The shared-scratch-directory race caught me a fourth time**, and again presented as a failure of the thing under test - the two cases passed alone and failed together. Per-test directories, with the reason in the helper's doc.
- Workspace 115 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **The stacked vias were chased and the obvious fix was measured and dropped.** After routing: `stm32_breakout` 4 hole-to-hole, `multi_ic` 7, `qfp_fanout` 15, most of them reading as a net against itself at 0.00mm.
- The plausible cause - a net's paths are converted to vias separately, so two paths changing layer at one point each ask for one - is **not** the cause. The drill files settle it: at (56.134, 41.910) one via goes into the `Top-In1` pass and the other into the through pass, so they have different layer spans and are repeats of nothing.
- Measured anyway, since the filter is four lines: deduplicating by position and span moved `qfp_fanout` from 309 violations to 291 and left **every hole-to-hole count identical**, 4, 7 and 15 before and after. It removes something, and that something is not what it was written for. Reverted - a change whose stated reason does not hold is one nobody can maintain, and 18 violations are not worth carrying a false explanation.
- Written up in `docs/routing.md` as the eighteenth instrument measured and dropped, with what a real fix would have to do: stop the search putting a via where the board already has one at any depth, which is a cost-model question rather than a filter on the output.
- DONE: **the cost model already has a knob for the stacked holes, and it is the wrong lever.** `CongestionMap` tracks the cells each via's ring covers and charges `ring_penalty` per ring; the default is **0.0**, so the search knows where every hole is and pays nothing to put another on top. That looked like the whole answer.
- Swept at 0, 1, 3 and 8 on the three fixtures that stack holes, with violations and hole-to-hole measured together in one run. **No price wins on both counts, and none wins on either consistently**: `stm32_breakout` improves at 8 (180 -> 158 violations, 4 -> 1 holes), `multi_ic` gets worse at stacking at every price tried (7 -> 13, 8, 12), and `qfp_fanout` halves its stacked holes at 1 while paying 67 violations, outside its band of 57.
- **The reason is what the penalty charges for.** A ring price makes the search avoid *crossing* copper around an existing via; it says nothing about *placing* a via on a cell that already has one. On a crowded board it pushes routes away from rings and creates the congestion that makes another layer change attractive somewhere else. Nineteenth instrument measured and dropped; the sweep stays as a diagnostic.
- Recorded with it: the 27 stacked holes this sweep reports for `qfp_fanout` and the 15 from `drc_report` are the same board through different harnesses - one checks against `DesignRules::jlcpcb_2layer`, the other against the `jlcpcb` preset - which do not share a hole-to-hole minimum. Rows compare within a table, never across.
- DONE: **`CELL_VIA` was never set because it cannot be**, and that is a hard constraint on the cost-model work rather than an oversight. `is_free` reads `self.layers[layer][idx] == CELL_FREE` - an equality against zero - so any bit set in a cell's byte is a hard veto. A flag written purely to record something blocks the cell that carries it.
- Measured rather than reasoned: recording each via on the layers it spans, with **nothing reading the flag**, moved every board. `qfp_fanout` 309 violations -> **383** and `plane_board` 28 -> 29, both past their ratchets; `multi_ic` 291 -> 222 and `shift_driver` 65 -> 49 the other way. A change that only writes a note should not have a before and after at all, and the ratchets caught the claim that it did not.
- Reverted; all six ratchets back at their committed values. Written up in `docs/routing.md` beside the two instruments already dropped chasing these holes.
- **Where the memory has to live instead:** beside `CongestionMap`'s ring array, which already carries per-cell counts the search reads as *cost* rather than as *permission*. That is the difference between the two structures, and it is the whole reason one of them can hold this and the other cannot.
- DONE and reverted: **the fourth attempt, built exactly where the third said it had to go.** `CongestionMap` gained a `holes` count beside `ring`, marked when a net's path is committed and unmarked on rip-up, read by the search as `via_stack_penalty` per hole at the one moment a via is *placed*. At 0.0 it reproduced every fixture byte for byte, which is the right way to add a knob.
- **At 5, 20 and 100 it also reproduced every fixture byte for byte** - identical violations, shorts, stacked holes, vias and routes. A term that costs a hundred and changes nothing is never charged, and I did not establish why before the budget ran out. The likely reading, recorded for whoever picks it up: a net's holes are marked only once all its paths are committed, so same-net stacking - `VCC_3V3` against `VCC_3V3`, most of these violations - cannot see itself.
- Reverted rather than shipped. A knob that provably does nothing is worse than no knob: it reads like a lever, moves nothing, and costs the next person a fire to discover that.
- Four attempts now, each narrowing the next, all in `docs/routing.md` with numbers: not the output filter, not the ring price, not the occupancy byte, and not this shape of hole count until the marking moment is understood.
- **One of the two candidates for the dead knob is eliminated, by reading rather than by rebuilding.** `variant.rs` copies seven `AutorouteConfig` fields and ends `..AutorouteConfig::default()`, which looked like the answer - anything a caller sets outside those seven is replaced. It is not: `via_price_sweep` sets `via_foreign_copper_penalty`, which is not among the seven, calls `route_board` directly, and gets a real spread of 33 to 42. The sweep that found nothing took the same route, so the plumbing carried the value.
- The seven-field copy is **not a defect either**, which is worth recording so nobody else chases it: what it drops falls back to the shipped defaults, and those are the measured values. The CLI has only two paths - `--fast` with plain defaults, or variants - and neither loses a tuned setting.
- So the remaining candidate is the marking moment: a net's rings, and the holes marked beside them, are recorded only once all of that net's paths are committed. That would explain same-net stacking not seeing itself; it does not explain the cross-net pairs, so it may not be the whole answer.
- **The cause is still not established, and this fire says so rather than guessing again.** My previous commit published the marking-time reading as "the most likely"; that was one candidate of two presented as if it were the finding, and the doc now says what is known and what is not.
- DONE: **instrumented instead of rebuilding, and the second candidate falls too.** Counting how often the via-transition branch prices a layer change at a cell already covered by a via ring: `multi_ic` **46,181 of 2,836,140** evaluations (1.63%), `qfp_fanout` **49,376 of 1,783,840** (2.77%).
- Tens of thousands of times per board the search evaluates putting a via where one already is, **with that information in front of it**. The congestion map holds it at the right moment, so the marking is not too late and the idea is not unworkable.
- **The fourth attempt therefore failed on its own wiring, not on principle.** A price at that decision point can work; what it needs first is a check that the hole array is actually populated - one assertion after routing one fixture, which the fourth attempt never made before trusting its sweep.
- It also explains the ring price: it charges all 46,181 of those evaluations for *crossing* copper, which is why it moved every board, and nothing extra for stacking, which is why it moved no stacked hole.
- Probe reverted; no code change. Four attempts, two eliminated causes, and the question is now narrow enough to be worth a fifth.
- DONE: **the fifth attempt found the bug, measured the price, and the price does not pay.**
- **The fourth attempt's bug was one line.** `congestion_map.set_stack_penalty(..)` was never added beside `set_ring_penalty` - my edit had matched no text and I never checked - so the map's penalty stayed 0.0 and `stacking_cost` returned zero however high the knob went. A price of 100 producing byte-identical boards was exactly that.
- Rebuilt with the check first, which is the lesson: routing `multi_ic` leaves **364 holes recorded for 119 vias**, about three layers each, so the map is populated *before* a price is swept against it.

| stack price | stm32_breakout | multi_ic | qfp_fanout |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (shipped) | 180 / 4 | 291 / 7 | 309 / 27 |
| 5 | 208 / **0** | 245 / 15 | 368 / 31 |
| 20 | 184 / 2 | **237** / 9 | 366 / 31 |
| 100 | 203 / 1 | 237 / 9 | 371 / 30 |

- Violations then stacked holes, read against the bands 59, 65 and 57: `stm32_breakout` clears its stacking at 5 and gives back 28 violations; `multi_ic` is 54 violations better at 20 and *worse* at stacking; `qfp_fanout` is worse on both at every price. **No price wins.**
- **Kept at 0.0 rather than reverted**, unlike the fourth attempt, and the difference is that this one demonstrably works - every nonzero price moves the boards, the shipped default reproduces the router exactly, and a future sweep is one command. Same basis `via_ring_penalty` is kept on.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. Five attempts, and the honest summary is that stacked holes are not reachable by pricing the placement either - the two boards that improve and the one that worsens disagree in a way no single constant resolves, the same shape as the pad-zone margin. The next real move is a rule that varies with the board, and nothing here has established what it would key on.

### V3 - `cypcb-desktop`, the workspace member nothing compiles
- DONE: **the AppImage failure has a root cause now, it is nine lines of a vendored script, and it is not this project's code.** Tauri reports `failed to run linuxdeploy` and nothing else; every step of this came from running the tool by hand and then reading the plugin it runs.
- **Where it stops.** `/config/.cache/tauri/linuxdeploy-plugin-gtk.sh`, lines 300-310. After setting rpaths it walks three module directories - GTK's immodules and printbackends, and gdk-pixbuf's loaders - and symlinks every `.so` in them flat into `$APPDIR/usr/lib`:

  ```
  for directory in "${PATCH_ARRAY[@]}"; do
      while IFS= read -r -d '' file; do
          ln $verbose -s "${file/\/usr\/lib\//}" "$APPDIR/usr/lib"
      done < <(find "$directory" -name '*.so' -print0)
  done
  ```

  `ln -s` without `-f` fails when the name is taken, the script exits 1, and linuxdeploy reports `Failed to run plugin: gtk`.
- **Measured, on an AppDir the same build had just created from nothing.** The three directories hold 13, 2 and 12 `.so` files and share **no basename between them** - `uniq -d` over all 27 is empty, so they are not colliding with each other. `$APPDIR/usr/lib/` already contains `im-am-et.so`, `im-broadway.so`, `im-cedilla.so` and the rest of the immodules by the time the loop runs, put there by linuxdeploy's own dependency copying earlier in the same invocation. The plugin then tries to link the same names in again.
- **The earlier guess is refuted twice over.** `: File exists` looked like a stale AppDir from a failed first attempt; deleting `bundle/appimage` and `bundle/appimage_deb` and building again failed identically, and this measurement says why - the collision is created inside a single run, not carried between them.
- **Nothing is changed here on the strength of it.** The one-character fix is `ln -sf` in a file that lives in a download cache and is fetched again whenever it is missing, so patching it would work once and mislead afterwards. It belongs upstream, in `linuxdeploy-plugin-gtk`.
- **The practical position is comfortable**, which is why this is recorded rather than worked around: `target/release/bundle/deb/CodeYourPCB_0.1.0-beta_amd64.deb` and `.../rpm/CodeYourPCB-0.1.0-beta-1.x86_64.rpm` are real, 7.3MB each, and a Linux user installs either. Only the AppImage is missing.
- Proof: `find` over the three module directories -> 13, 2 and 12 files, and `uniq -d` on their basenames -> nothing. `ls $APPDIR/usr/lib` -> the immodules are already present on a freshly built AppDir. `grep -n "Manually setting rpath" -A 12` on the plugin -> the loop above. `./build-linux.sh` after deleting the AppDir -> `BUILD_EXIT=1`, `.deb` and `.rpm` still produced.
- NEXT-ACTION: **none in this vector worth its cost right now.** The cause is named, the workaround belongs to somebody else's repository, and two of the three Linux artefacts build. What is worth doing if the AppImage becomes necessary is testing on a host with `/dev/fuse`, where linuxdeploy mounts rather than extracts and may not take the same path through the plugin - which is a different environment rather than a different fix, and this container cannot answer it.
- DONE: **`tauri build` was run for the first time, and the path this project has been printing for years is now observed rather than argued.** The previous commit corrected `src-tauri/target/release/bundle/` to `target/release/bundle/` from `cargo metadata` and the workspace layout, and recorded plainly that nobody had looked. Looked now: `target/release/bundle/deb/CodeYourPCB_0.1.0-beta_amd64.deb` and `target/release/bundle/rpm/CodeYourPCB-0.1.0-beta-1.x86_64.rpm`, **7.3MB each**, and `src-tauri/target` still does not exist. These are the first installers this project has ever produced.
- **The corrected script behaved the way it was corrected to.** It exited non-zero because the AppImage was missing, instead of printing `Build complete!` over three `(not created)` lines of which two would have been false.
- **The AppImage failure had a cause Tauri does not report, and it is now removed.** Tauri says only `failed to bundle project 'failed to run linuxdeploy'`. `linuxdeploy` is itself an AppImage, and an AppImage mounts itself with FUSE; this container has `fusermount` but no `/dev/fuse`. Measured on the same binary: without the variable it answers `dlopen(): error loading libfuse.so.2` and `AppImages require FUSE to run.`, with `APPIMAGE_EXTRACT_AND_RUN=1` it answers `linuxdeploy version 1-alpha (git commit 659c9db)`. `build-linux.sh` sets it **only when `/dev/fuse` is absent**, so a machine that has FUSE keeps the faster mounting path. After the change the libfuse error is gone from the build log.
- **OPEN: the AppImage is still not produced, and the remaining cause is unknown.** Running `linuxdeploy` by hand against the AppDir - which is the only way to see its output at all, because Tauri swallows it - gave `Manually setting rpath for GTK modules / : File exists / ERROR: Failed to run plugin: gtk (exit code: 1)`. That reads like a stale AppDir from the failed first attempt, and **it is not**: deleting `bundle/appimage` and `bundle/appimage_deb` and building again fails identically. So `: File exists` is what the second run of the plugin says, not what stops the first, and the first cause is still unnamed. Recorded rather than guessed at further.
- Proof: `./build-linux.sh` -> `BUILD_EXIT=1`, with the `.deb` and `.rpm` present and listed. `ls -d src-tauri/target` -> does not exist. `grep -c "dev/fuse is missing" /tmp/b2.log` -> 1, and `grep -i "libfuse" ` on the same log -> nothing, so the workaround reached the bundler. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **make linuxdeploy's own output visible, then read it.**~~ Done, and it named the cause - see the entry above. Tauri reports six words for a plugin that printed a page; every diagnosis in this entry came from running the tool by hand, and the next one will too. The specific thing to try is `linuxdeploy --appdir ... --plugin gtk` on a **fresh** AppDir with the plugin's verbosity raised, since the run that produced the `: File exists` line was a second pass over an AppDir a failed attempt had already filled. Worth knowing before that: the `.deb` and the `.rpm` are real, installable artefacts, so a Linux user is not blocked on the AppImage.
- DONE: **the question "who builds `viewer/dist`" had an answer already, and reading it first turned up a different defect.** `tauri.conf.json` declares `beforeBuildCommand` as `npm run build` in `../viewer`, so the Tauri CLI does build the frontend - and `viewer/package.json`'s `build:desktop` is `tauri build`, which honours it. Nothing was missing. What is missing is only missing when somebody runs `cargo build -p cypcb-desktop` directly, which bypasses the CLI, which is exactly what the smoke test does. No fourth place needed to build it.
- **What the reading found instead: all three build scripts and two documents name a path that cannot exist.** `cargo metadata --no-deps` reports `target_directory: /workspace/codeyourpcb/target`, and `cypcb-desktop` is listed as a workspace member - members share the workspace root's target directory. `src-tauri/target` does not exist and never did.
- **The consequence is a script that contradicts itself.** `build-linux.sh` announced `src-tauri/target/release/bundle/`, then looked there for the AppImage and the `.deb`, with `|| echo "  (not created)"` on both. So a build that produced two installers printed **`Build complete!`** followed by `(not created)` twice. `build-macos.sh` and `build-windows.bat` carried the same path.
- **Fixed at every site rather than at the one that was noticed**: `build-linux.sh`, `build-macos.sh`, `build-windows.bat`, `DESKTOP-SETUP.md` (four lines) and `CONTRIBUTING.md`. `.planning/phases/12-tauri-desktop-foundation/` keeps its copy, because a planning record is what was planned rather than what is true. The Polish in `DESKTOP-SETUP.md` came through with its diacritics intact - 21 of them, counted, because a sed over a Polish file is how they get eaten.
- **And a missing installer is a failure now, not a line of text.** `build-linux.sh` exits non-zero when Tauri returns success and leaves nothing in the bundle directory. A build that exits 0 and produces no artefact is not a build that worked, and printing that under a "Build complete!" banner is how nobody notices.
- Proof: `cargo metadata --no-deps --format-version 1` -> `target_directory: /workspace/codeyourpcb/target`, `members with src-tauri: ['cypcb-desktop']`. `ls -d src-tauri/target` -> does not exist. `grep -rn "src-tauri/target"` across the scripts and docs -> **1 occurrence left, and it is the comment explaining the fix**. `bash -n` clean on both shell scripts, `npx tauri --version` -> `tauri-cli 2.9.6`, so the scripts do reach the bundler. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **nobody has run `tauri build` here, so the corrected path is reasoned rather than seen.**~~ Run, and seen: the .deb and .rpm are in `target/release/bundle/`. See above. It follows from `cargo metadata` and the workspace layout, which is strong, but the honest statement is that no `.AppImage` or `.deb` has been produced in this container to look at. A release build plus bundling is a long job and needs the AppImage tooling; it is the one thing that would turn this from an argument into an observation, and `./build-linux.sh` is now the command that would say so either way.
- DONE: **the desktop app has been run, for the first time, and it opens a window with content in it.** Compiling was settled two commits ago; whether it starts was not, and nothing in this project had ever asked. `scripts/desktop-smoke.sh` asks it the cheapest honest way: start the binary on a virtual display, wait, photograph the screen, and look at what is there.
- **Measured, on a 1280x900 Xvfb display**: the process was still alive after 12 seconds, the screen carries **351 distinct colours**, and the largest regions are the empty root at 58.3% and white at **39.8%** - a window with a page in it, against a root that is one colour when nothing has drawn. The only output the app produces is two `libEGL warning` lines about the container having no hardware acceleration, which is the truth about the container rather than a fault in the app.
- **A missing piece surfaced before the app could even be tried: `viewer/dist` did not exist.** `tauri.conf.json` sets `frontendDist` to `../viewer/dist`, and nothing in this project builds it - the gate builds `viewer/pkg`, the wasm module, which is a different artefact. So the window would have opened onto nothing. `npm run build` produces it (117 files), and the smoke script treats its absence as an error rather than letting it pass as a white screen, because an app that opens onto nothing is a passing smoke test and a broken program.
- **What the script proves and what it refuses to claim.** It proves the binary starts, survives, and draws. It does not prove the content is the right content, that the native file picker works, or that a menu click reaches the frontend - those need a person or a UI driver, and the script says so in its own closing line rather than letting a green tick imply them.
- **Its first version called a running application dead**, and the cause is the same mistake this session had already caught once: `xvfb-run ... | grep -v "libEGL warning"` reports **grep's** status, and grep exits 1 when it filters everything out - which is exactly what happens when the only output is those two warnings. The exit status is captured directly now, with the reason written above the line so the next person does not reintroduce it.
- Proof: `./scripts/desktop-smoke.sh` -> `[OK] it was still running after 12s`, `[OK] a window with content is on the screen`. Measured separately without a pipe first: `timeout 20 xvfb-run -a ./target/debug/cypcb-desktop; echo EXIT=$?` -> **`EXIT=124`**, which is timeout killing a process that was still running rather than a process that had exited. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **`viewer/dist` is built by nobody**~~ - it is built by `beforeBuildCommand`, which only the Tauri CLI runs. See the entry above, and the defect that reading it turned up. The smoke script errors on its absence, which turns a silent white screen into a message, but that is a guard rather than a fix: a fresh checkout still cannot start the desktop app without somebody knowing to run `npm run build` in `viewer/`. Either `setup-dev.sh` builds it, or `build-linux.sh` does, or `tauri.conf.json`'s `beforeBuildCommand` is what should have been doing it all along - reading which of those three is already meant to is the first move, not adding a fourth.
- DONE: **`cypcb-desktop` has its first test, and it is the check the crate most needed.** Zero `#[test]` in any of its four files - `commands.rs`, `lib.rs`, `main.rs`, `menu.rs` - which follows from a crate nobody could compile. `src-tauri/tests/the_menu_handles_what_it_declares.rs` asks the one question the menu can be asked without a display: does every id `handle_menu_event` acts on exist in the menu `create_app_menu()` builds.
- **Three assertions, and the third is the defect it exists for.** A match arm naming an id the menu does not declare is dead code that looks like a feature; a menu item whose id the handler misspells is a control that does nothing when clicked. Also asserted: no two items share an id, since the frontend switches on it and could not tell which was clicked, and every id is shaped `group.action`, which the handler's arms and `desktop.ts`'s switch both assume.
- `mod menu` became `pub mod menu` for it. That is the whole reason the data model was unreadable from a test: `create_app_menu()` was public inside a private module, so the menu could be checked by regex and by nothing else.
- **A claim my own last commit made false is corrected in the same breath.** `viewer/src/__tests__/every-native-menu-item-does-something.test.ts` opens by saying the desktop crate cannot be compiled in this container, which is why nothing else in the project reads `menu.rs`. True when written, false since the crate started compiling. The comment now says what changed and why both halves are kept: the TypeScript one is the only half that can see `desktop.ts`'s switch, and the Rust one is the only half that can see an id built rather than written as a literal.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-desktop --test the_menu_handles_what_it_declares` -> **3 passed**. Mutation, misspelling the handler's arm as `view.full_screen`: `handle_menu_event acts on 'view.full_screen', which no menu item offers`, followed by all thirteen ids the menu does offer - 2 passed 1 failed. `npx vitest run src/__tests__/every-native-menu-item-does-something.test.ts` -> 4 passed. `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-desktop --all-targets` clean, `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION unchanged and now narrower: **the crate has never been run.** This test says the menu's wiring is consistent; it says nothing about a window opening, a file arriving through the native picker, or a click reaching the frontend. Those need a display - `xvfb-run` is the cheap way to find out whether the app even starts headless, and `dev-linux.sh` is the entry point. The three commands in `commands.rs` are the other untested half, and they need an `AppHandle`, which means a running app or a harness that fakes one.
- DONE: **D2 started, and `cypcb-desktop` compiles for the first time. It was excluded from every run in this project long enough to rot.** The gate skipped it in two stages with the reason "they need system GTK/webkit", nothing installed those, and so nobody ever read what the compiler had to say about the crate. Three layers, each under the last.
- **Layer one: the container had no `pkg-config`**, let alone GTK. `cargo check -p cypcb-desktop` stopped at `The pkg-config command could not be found` before it looked for a single GTK symbol. Ubuntu 24.04, `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev 2.52.3-0ubuntu0.24.04.1`.
- **Layer two: nine compile errors, all of them the Tauri v1 to v2 move.** `emit` twice and `get_webview_window` once are methods that now live on the `Emitter` and `Manager` traits - the compiler says so in as many words, `trait Emitter which provides emit is implemented but not in scope` - and four are `FilePath::path()`, which Tauri 2 does not have. The fix for those is `into_path()` rather than a cast: it fails loudly on a platform where the picker's answer is not a path at all, which is Android returning a content URI, instead of manufacturing a path that is not one.
- **Layer three, and not an API question: `generate_context!` refused the icon.** `icon /workspace/codeyourpcb/src-tauri/icons/32x32.png is not RGBA`. Measured rather than assumed, all five files: `128x128.png`, `128x128@2x.png` and `32x32.png` are PNG in **RGB** mode, `icon.ico` is a 32x32 ICO in RGB, and **`icon.icns` is a PNG** - a 128x128 RGB PNG carrying the name of an Apple icon format. The three PNGs are RGBA now, same pixels with an opaque alpha channel.
- **`icon.icns` is left alone and recorded instead.** A macOS bundle built from it would be given a file whose contents are not the format its name claims. That is the macOS half of D2, which D2 itself puts after Linux - "then ask the owner for a Mac and a Windows machine" - and it cannot be checked here. `icon.ico` being RGB may be the same shape of problem for Windows and is equally untested.
- **The gate stopped excluding the crate, and the dependencies went into `scripts/setup-dev.sh` first.** Removing the exclusion without that would have made the gate pass here and fail on any machine that had not had this session's apt run by hand - which is the exact failure `setup-dev.sh` was written for, in its own words: "the answer used to be somebody ran a command once". Stage `[5/6]` checks `pkg-config --exists gtk+-3.0 webkit2gtk-4.1` before installing anything.
- **What is not measured, said rather than glossed:** the package list is proven sufficient and not proven minimal. `setup-linux.sh` already carried the same set minus `libxdo-dev` and `libssl-dev`; both were installed in one go, so whether those two were needed is unknown. The comment in `setup-dev.sh` says so where somebody would look.
- Proof: `cargo check -p cypcb-desktop` -> `Finished dev profile`, where the same command opened this fire with 9 errors. `cargo test --workspace` **with no `--exclude`** -> **180 suites ok, zero failed**, against 177 with the crate skipped. `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-desktop --all-targets` clean. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`, now covering the crate in stages 2 and 3.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the desktop crate compiles and has never been run.**~~ It has a test, and as of 2026-08-12 it has been run: it opens a window with content. See above. A window that opens, a file that opens through the native picker and a menu item that reaches the frontend are three things no test here touches, and the crate has exactly the shape - trait imports, picker return types, menu event ids - where compiling and working are different questions. `dev-linux.sh` is the entry point. After that, D2's own next step: ask the owner for a Mac and a Windows machine, and take `icon.icns` and `icon.ico` with the question.

- **Measured, and the exclusion is justified.** `scripts/quality-gate.sh` excludes `cypcb-desktop` from clippy and from the test run, and there is no CI in the repository at all - no `.github/workflows` - so the crate is compiled nowhere. Building it in the container fails on `pkg-config` and GTK, which is an environment limit rather than a defect, and the gate says so in its own comment.
- **What is covered, measured rather than assumed:** `cargo fmt --check` parses `src-tauri` and reports errors in it - a deliberate syntax error there fails stage 1 - so the sources cannot rot into unparseable code unnoticed. The logic the desktop wraps lives in `cypcb-platform`, which is in the tested workspace and passes 9 tests.
- **What is not:** type drift across those 251 lines. `src-tauri` imports exactly one project crate, `cypcb_platform`, so a change to that crate's `Menu`, `MenuBar` or `MenuItem` would break the desktop build and nothing in the repository would notice.
- Checked for this session specifically: nothing changed here touches `KicadPcbParseResult`, `PadDef`, `AutorouteConfig` or the layer naming, so none of the struct changes made over the last several fires can have broken it.
- NEXT-ACTION: none pulled. The desktop gap is now measured and bounded rather than unknown, and closing it properly means either a CI runner with GTK or a headless build of the crate - both are owner-scale decisions about infrastructure rather than fires.
- DONE: **components say which face they are on.** A `Side` component with the three questions a face answers - which copper its SMD pads take, which silkscreen it prints to, and its layer-mask bit. The KiCad importer reads `(layer "B.Cu")` from a footprint, which is the only place in the codebase where the side is data rather than inference; sync derives it from the footprint's copper and stores the answer so every rule reads the same one. The silkscreen rule takes it and falls back to the old guess only when nothing states it. Proven on a two-footprint board where one is `F.Cu` and one `B.Cu`: `[("R1", Top), ("R2", Bottom)]`.

### V2 - Autorouter and routing quality

- DONE: **the same defect was alive in the viewer, in four places, and the fix for it came with a test that proved nothing until it was rewritten.** `f7c1b28` wired `ruleset_for_world` into `cypcb route` and `cypcb score` and recorded that `cypcb-render` builds `PresetRuleSet::new` four times. All four are production - `auto_route`, `auto_route_with_params`, `auto_route_debug`, `auto_route_variants` - so the editor's own router ignored the constraints the command line had just started obeying. Same board, same design, two behaviours depending on which of the two you routed from.
- **The first test written for it was vacuous, and the mutation is what said so.** It stated `net VCC [width 0.4mm]`, routed through `PcbEngine`, and asserted the emitted width. It passed. Reverting all four sites to `PresetRuleSet::new` -> **46 passed**, unchanged. The reason is the one this vector already recorded: `pathfinder_v2.rs:174` reads `world.net_constraints(net).width` directly and never consults the rule set, so a width assertion cannot tell the two apart. `net_width_override.rs` has always passed for the same reason.
- **Rewritten to measure clearance, which has no second path.** Two engines, one board, the watched net saying nothing in the first and `[clearance 4mm]` in the second; route both, count traces and total copper, assert the two results differ. Reverting the four sites -> `assertion left != right failed: a net demanding 4mm of space routed identically to one that said nothing`, **1 failed**. The doc comment says which of the two it measures and why, so the next person does not re-write the width version.
- **A third instance of the same family, fixed in passing.** `auto_route_variants` scored every variant with `DesignRules::default()` - which is `jlcpcb_2layer`, and matched the preset above it only because that preset is hard-coded to `jlcpcb`. Now `DesignRules::from_constraints(&preset.constraints())`. Two coincidences agreeing is not one source.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-render --features native --lib` -> **46 passed**. Mutation, all four sites back to `PresetRuleSet::new` -> **1 failed**, named above. `cargo clippy -p cypcb-render --all-targets` clean. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector beyond what `f7c1b28` already left - `current` is still not a width, for the layer reason recorded there. What this run adds to that entry is a warning about how to test it: an assertion about **width** cannot distinguish a wired rule set from an unwired one, because width reaches the trace by a second route. Any future test of a constraint reaching the router has to pick a constraint the rule set alone carries.

- DONE: **the router never received the clearance the design asked for, and nothing had noticed because nothing outside a unit test ever built a rule set with overrides in it.** `PresetRuleSet` has carried `with_overrides` and `set_net_override` since the presets were written; `grep -rn "set_net_override" crates/ --include=*.rs` finds three callers and all three are its own tests. Every real caller - `route.rs` twice, `score.rs` once, `cypcb-render` four times - built `PresetRuleSet::new(preset)`, which is the fab preset and nothing else. So the router planned against one set of numbers and `cypcb check` graded the result against another, the design's, and the gap surfaced as violations on a board the router thought it had finished.
- **Width was already carried and clearance was not, which is why this looked fine.** `pathfinder_v2.rs:174` reads `world.net_constraints(net.net_id)?.width?` directly, so `net VCC [width 0.4mm]` really was emitted at 0.4mm and `net_width_override.rs` really did pass. Nothing anywhere read `.clearance`. The width path also stops short of the grid: `grid.rs:196` bloats obstacles by `min_clearance + min_trace_width / 2` from the **rule set**, so a wide net was drawn wide and planned as if it were minimum width.
- **New `cypcb_drc::ruleset_for_world(preset, world)`**, in the one crate that has the world, the rules and the calculator together. It reads each net's stated width and clearance out of the registry and raises the preset's two figures to them. Raises only: a design asking for copper thinner than the fabricator images is a fault `MinTraceWidthRule` reports, not licence to plan below what can be made. Nets the design says nothing about are absent from the map and route exactly as before.
- **Measured on `examples/mains-sequencer.cypcb`, which is the board the owner raised this from** - `netclass Mains [current 10A clearance 3mm]`. Before: 768 segments, 55 vias, and `cypcb check` reporting **462 clearance violations**, 58 trace-current, 5 shorts, 0 unrouted pins. After: 319 segments, 29 vias, **199 clearance**, 62 trace-current, **122 shorts, 84 unrouted pins**. That is not an improvement and is not written as one. The board is not manufacturable either way; what changed is that the router now attempts the 3mm the design states instead of routing to 0.2mm and being graded at 3mm. The mains rule on this board, at this board size, is beyond the router.
- **A second fault the change exposed rather than caused.** `route_variants` scored every variant with `DesignRules::from_constraints(constraints_for_net(rules, 0))` - net **0**, whichever net that happens to be, used as a stand-in for the preset. That was harmless only while no net had an override. On this board a mains net interns first, so every variant would have been scored against 3mm. Now `rules.preset().constraints()`. Evidence it was live: the composites moved uniformly the moment it was fixed, `PathFinder Default` 320053.0 -> 300053.0 and `Guarded Pads` 219052.8 -> 204052.8, with the same routes underneath.
- **The six benchmark boards do not move, and the reason is structural rather than lucky.** `set_net_constraints` is called from `sync.rs` only - the DSL path - so a KiCad-imported board has no stated constraints, the override map is empty, and `with_overrides(preset, {})` is `new(preset)`. Confirmed by running them: `cargo test -p cypcb-autoroute --release --test benchmark_validation -- --ignored` -> **2 passed**, every ratchet held, stm32_breakout 187 against 239, multi_ic 304 against 356, shift_driver 65 against 82, qfp_fanout 318 against 366, plane_board 28 against 28, all with 0 unrouted.
- **One-sided, and the module says so rather than leaving it to be found.** `ClearanceRule` folds a pair of nets with `.max` (`clearance.rs:150`), so mains-to-signal is 3mm measured from either end. A rule set is consulted per net being routed, so the mains net is planned at 3mm and a signal approaching it afterwards is still planned at the preset.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc --lib net_rules` -> **4 passed**. Mutation, making `wider` return the floor unconditionally -> **2 failed**. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **`current` is still not a width, and the reason is the layer.** `netclass Mains [current 10A]` needs IPC-2221, which `cypcb-calc` has and `TraceCurrentRule` already uses - but the answer depends on whether the copper lands on an outer layer, which sheds heat into the air, or an inner one, which does not. The rule set is built before the router picks a layer. `current_alone_is_not_yet_a_width` pins the present behaviour so the choice is made deliberately: plan every current-bearing net at the inner-layer width and over-build the outer ones, or make the rule set answer per layer. The second is the honest one and is a change to `RoutingRuleSet`, not to this module.
- DONE: **a claim this tracker made last commit was false on two counts, and correcting it refuted the candidate it was written about.** `879dceb` recorded that "via_price_sweep measured that price on every board once already and the shipped default came out of it". Wrong test and wrong coverage: `via_price_sweep` measures `via_foreign_copper_penalty` - what a via pays for foreign copper inside its keepout - while `via_ring_penalty` is a different knob, what a via's **own** ring costs so the next one does not land on it. The test that does measure it, `what_a_via_ring_should_cost`, ran on three boards of six and `plane_board` was one of the three it skipped.
- **Widened to all six and run, which is what should have happened before the claim was made.** `via_ring_penalty` at 0, 1, 3, 8:

  | board | ring 0 | ring 1 | ring 3 | ring 8 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `led_blink` | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 |
  | `stm32_breakout` | 187 / 99 | 198 / 104 | 252 / 149 | 159 / 88 |
  | `multi_ic` | 304 / 200 | 257 / 159 | 309 / 203 | 298 / 196 |
  | `shift_driver` | 65 / 34 | 64 / 34 | 66 / 35 | 66 / 35 |
  | `plane_board` | **28 / 13** | **28 / 13** | 34 / 18 | 34 / 18 |
  | `qfp_fanout` | 318 / 149 | 385 / 203 | 374 / 183 | 384 / 207 |

- **`plane_board` at ring 0 and ring 1 is identical to the violation.** The knob does nothing there on the shipped default - which is the board whose neighbourhood said `[via_ring 1]` was a real improvement, outside a band of zero. Both measurements are correct and they are not about the same thing: the neighbourhood changes one knob **on top of `Eager Pads`**, and what it found is a combination effect rather than a property of the ring price.
- **That is a limit of one-knob neighbourhood search worth stating where somebody will read it.** A neighbourhood measures a knob on a particular base, and a win there is a claim about the pair, not about the knob. `is_the_best_variant_a_local_optimum` is still the right instrument for "is this point a local optimum"; it is the wrong one for "is this value good", and the two questions read identically in its output.
- **No board wants a ring price on the evidence of this sweep.** `stm32_breakout` at 8 is 159 / 88 against 187 / 99, which is -28 / -11 inside a band of 59 / 61 - noise. `multi_ic` at 1 is -47 / -41 inside 65 / 56 - noise. `qfp_fanout` is worse at every non-zero value. The shipped default of 0.0 stands, now measured on six boards instead of three.
- Proof: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test what_a_via_ring_should_cost -- --ignored --nocapture` -> the table above, 8.9s, `1 passed`. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **none pulled in this vector.** The twelve variants are four-of-six local optima, the one candidate a neighbourhood produced does not survive being measured on its own, and the ring price now has six boards behind its default rather than three. What remains recorded and unfinished is the allocator - 41% of instructions in malloc and free - which is a rewrite of `route_with_blockers` so the search owns its scratch space, and a project rather than a fire.
- DONE: **all six neighbourhoods measured, four of the six shipped picks are local optima, and the diagnostic was corrected before its own output misled anybody.** The remaining four boards were run in two pairs, because the first attempt at all six at once was killed before it printed anything.

  | board | winner | verdict |
  |---|---|---|
  | `led_blink` | `High-Density` | local optimum |
  | `stm32_breakout` | `Eager` | local optimum |
  | `multi_ic` | `Clearance Priced` | **local optimum** |
  | `qfp_fanout` | `Default` | local optimum |
  | `shift_driver` | `Eager Light` | ranking prefers `[margin 2]`, -2 / -8 against a band of 17 / 8 - **inside the band** |
  | `plane_board` | `Eager Pads` | ranking prefers `[via_ring 1]`, -2 / -1 against a band of **0 / 0** - outside it |

- **The twelfth variant defends itself.** `PathFinder Clearance Priced` is a local optimum on `multi_ic` against all eleven of its own one-knob neighbours, which is a stronger statement about it than the sweep that put it there.
- **The instrument said "BEATEN" for two boards and only one of them is an improvement, which is a defect in the instrument rather than in the boards.** It ranks with the router's own rule - complete, then shorts, then composite - and that is not the noise-band test. On `shift_driver` the ranking prefers a neighbour by 8 shorts against a band of exactly 8. Printing only the preference would have read as two free improvements. The verdict now carries both figures and says which question each answers: `the ranking prefers X (-2 violations, -8 shorts, band 17 / 8) but the move is inside the band`.
- **That is the same class of mistake this vector already recorded**, when the weighted-heuristic sweep looked like a 96-violation win on the total and was noise on five of six boards plus one real regression. An instrument that reports a preference without its band invites exactly that reading.
- Proof: `CYPCB_BOARDS=stm32,multi` -> both local optima, 53.7s; `CYPCB_BOARDS=shift,qfp` -> `qfp_fanout` a local optimum and `shift_driver` preferred-but-inside-band, 22.0s; `CYPCB_BOARDS=plane` -> `and the move is OUTSIDE the band: a real improvement`. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `GATE_EXIT=0`, `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **one candidate, and it has to be measured on the five boards it was not found on.** `via_ring 1` on `plane_board` is the only move outside a band across all six neighbourhoods. ~~`via_price_sweep` measured that price on every board once already~~ **- false, and corrected in the entry above: that sweep measures a different knob, and the one that measures this covered three boards of six.** A thirteenth variant carrying 1.0 would be a claim that one board wants a value nobody had measured on it - which is exactly what a variant is for, and exactly why it needs the other five measured before it ships rather than after.
- DONE: **the variant space is not fully explored, and a one-knob neighbour beats the shipped pick on `plane_board`.** `docs/routing.md` has recorded for several fires that each variant is a point somebody measured and that nothing has ever looked between them. `is_the_best_variant_a_local_optimum` looks, the cheapest way: take the variant a board's own ranking picks, change one knob at a time, and hand the winner and its neighbours to `generate_variants` together so the same rule ranks them - complete first, then fewest shorts.
- **Two boards run, and they answer differently:**

  | board | winner | verdict |
  |---|---|---|
  | `led_blink` | `PathFinder High-Density` | local optimum on this neighbourhood |
  | `plane_board` | `PathFinder Eager Pads` | **beaten** by `[via_ring 1]`, 10 (4 shorts) against 12 (5) |

  `plane_board`'s band is **zero on both columns**, so -2 violations and -1 short is outside it in the right direction. That is the board where movement of any size is signal, and it moved.
- **One knob at a time, deliberately.** A neighbourhood that moves two cannot say which move mattered, and this vector has spent enough fires on instruments whose result could not be attributed.
- **Four of the six boards were not run, and the diagnostic says so in its own output.** The full sweep is six boards times a dozen points, and `multi_ic`'s winner now carries the clearance barrier so every point in its neighbourhood builds the field - the first attempt was killed before it printed anything. `CYPCB_BOARDS=plane,led` runs a subset; unset runs all six and takes minutes. A diagnostic that quietly measured two boards and printed a verdict would read as a verdict about six, so it names what it skipped.
- Proof: `CYPCB_BOARDS=plane,led cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test is_the_best_variant_a_local_optimum -- --ignored --nocapture` -> the table above, 11 points per board, 1.35s, `1 passed`, and the closing line `Not run this time: stm32_breakout.kicad_pcb, multi_ic.kicad_pcb, shift_driver.kicad_pcb, qfp_fanout.kicad_pcb`. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **run the other four, then decide whether `via_ring 1` earns a thirteenth point.**~~ The other four are run - see above. A neighbour that beats the pick on one board is not a variant yet - `via_price_sweep` measured the via ring price across all six once already, and a value that helps `plane_board` has to be checked against the five it was not measured on before it ships. The order matters: measure the remaining four neighbourhoods first, because a search that adopts the first improvement it finds is hill-climbing on one board rather than on the fixture set.
- DONE: **the clearance barrier is an eleventh variant, and the ranking picks it on the board that wanted it.** The sweep had left two boards disagreeing about one term - `multi_ic` far better at `k = 10`, `qfp_fanout` worse by the same margin - which the plan's rules refuse as a default and which is exactly what `generate_variants` exists to hold. `PathFinder Clearance Priced` carries `clearance_barrier: 10.0`; the other nine carry zero.
- **Where each board's ranking puts it**, from `variant_picks_per_board`, ranked complete-first then fewest shorts:

  | board | rank of the new variant | winner |
  |---|---|---|
  | `multi_ic` | **1 of 12** | **PathFinder Clearance Priced**, 164 (91 shorts) |
  | `led_blink` | 5 | High-Density |
  | `plane_board` | 5 | Eager Pads |
  | `shift_driver` | 7 | Eager Light |
  | `stm32_breakout` | 9 | Eager |
  | `qfp_fanout` | 9 | Default |

- **On `multi_ic` that is 304 (200 shorts) at the default down to 164 (91)**, with nothing abandoned. The ranking is lexicographic, so it won on shorts rather than on the composite - which is the axis this project puts first and the one a user notices without reading anything.
- **The regression the sweep found is still there and can no longer reach anybody.** `qfp_fanout` ranks it ninth of twelve and the ranking will not hand it over. That is the whole argument for a variant over a default: a term two boards disagree about does not need one answer, it needs the per-board pick that `route --in-house` already makes.
- **The cost is on the board that chose it.** `multi_ic` now routes twelve variants in 16.0s, because the twelfth is the only one that builds the clearance field. Worth stating beside the win rather than under it.
- Proof: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test variant_picks_per_board -- --ignored --nocapture` -> the table above, 12 variants per board, 38.1s total, `1 passed`. That test also asserts nothing the ranking picks may be beaten on shorts **and** violations at once by a variant that routed everything, so the `multi_ic` pick is not a dominated board. `benchmark_validation` is untouched by this: it measures the default, which still carries `clearance_barrier: 0.0`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the eleven variants are still eleven guesses, and this is the twelfth.**~~ Two neighbourhoods measured; one of them is not a local optimum. See above. `docs/routing.md` has recorded for several fires that nothing has ever searched the space between them - each is a point somebody measured, and with a routing run under a second on most boards a small hill-climb from the best known point per board is affordable. That is now a twelve-point grid rather than an eleven-point one, and the argument for searching it is one board stronger: `multi_ic` just moved 140 violations by adding a single point nobody had tried.
- DONE: **step 4 is implemented and swept, no value earns the default, and this is not the null result step 2 was.** The barrier from section 2.2 is in `pathfinder_v2.rs`, gated exactly as the design resolution said: outside the routing net's own pad zones it applies to everything, inside them only where `grid.pad_owner` names a **different** net. `AutorouteConfig::clearance_barrier` defaults to **0.0**, which also switches off building the field, so nothing shipped moved - `benchmark_validation` reads `led_blink 2/0, stm32_breakout 187/99, multi_ic 304/200, shift_driver 65/34, qfp_fanout 318/149, plane_board 28/13`, unchanged to the digit.
- **The sweep, read against each board's own band rather than against a total.** Introduced violations / shorts, `k` in 0, 1, 10, 100:

  | board | band | k=0 | k=1 | k=10 | k=100 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `led_blink` | 0/0 | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 | 2 / 0 |
  | `stm32_breakout` | 59/61 | 175 / 95 | 184 / 104 | 228 / 148 | **301 / 182** |
  | `multi_ic` | 65/56 | 262 / 194 | 228 / 160 | **123 / 85** | 335 / 224 |
  | `shift_driver` | 17/8 | 65 / 34 | 67 / 34 | 67 / 34 | 67 / 34 |
  | `plane_board` | 0/0 | 28 / 13 | 21 / 15 | 20 / 14 | 22 / 15 |
  | `qfp_fanout` | 57/44 | 318 / 149 | 343 / 187 | **424 / 269** | 537 / 291 |

- **`k = 10` produces the largest single improvement this vector has ever measured, and a regression of the same size on another board.** `multi_ic` goes **-139 violations and -109 shorts** against a band of 65/56 - far outside it, on the board with the most copper. `qfp_fanout` goes +106 / +120 against a band of 57/44, also far outside, the wrong way. The plan's rule 2 refuses a change that moves any board outside its band the wrong way whatever the totals say, so the default stays 0.0.
- **`k = 1` is refused on a different rule.** Every board is inside its band except `plane_board`, which moves -7 violations and **+2 shorts** on a band of zero. Rule 4 puts shorts ahead of violations, and a board whose band is zero moving the wrong way on shorts is exactly the signal the zero-band boards exist to give.
- **The cost is real and belongs beside the result.** `multi_ic` at `k = 10` takes **6.9s against 0.5s** - fourteen times - and at 100 it takes 10.2s. A term evaluated per neighbour that changes the rip-up order changes how much work there is to do.
- **Kept as a knob at zero rather than reverted, and the precedent is in the same struct.** `via_foreign_pad_penalty` ships at 0.0 for the same reason: a measured instrument that one board wants and another refuses is a point on a Pareto front, not a failed experiment. Step 2 was worse everywhere that mattered and went in the dropped table; this is not that.
- Proof: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test clearance_barrier_sweep -- --ignored --nocapture` -> the table above, 27.3s for 24 routing runs. `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test benchmark_validation -- --ignored` -> **2 passed**, six boards unchanged. `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-autoroute --all-targets` clean, `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the two boards disagree about the same term**~~ - settled: it is a variant, and the ranking picks it on `multi_ic`. See above. `multi_ic` is large and SMD-dominant; `qfp_fanout` is a fine-pitch escape on two layers, and `docs/routing.md` already records it as the board where negotiated congestion is least stable. Either the barrier is a good idea that a two-layer escape cannot afford - in which case it belongs in `generate_variants` as an eleventh point rather than in the default - or the regression is the rip-up order moving and would show on any perturbation of that size. `variant_picks_per_board` is the instrument that separates those, because it already routes every board eight ways and ranks them.
- DONE: **step 4 of `docs/router-plan.md` is unblocked, by reading rather than by writing an instrument.** The barrier term had been held back for several fires by a question the plan did not answer: the clearance field knows distances and not nets, so a barrier over it charges a route for approaching **its own** pad - the one approach every route has to make. Step 3 was deferred behind the same gap.
- **The search already carries the missing half.** `pad_zones_per_net[net_idx]` is a list of `PadZone { cx, cy, radius }` - the discs belonging to the net being routed - built once per net and passed into the search as `&[PadZone]`, which already consults them. Outside the routing net's own zones the barrier applies, because that is where foreign copper is; inside them it does not, because reaching your own pin is not a fault. No new structure, no per-net field, one lookup the search makes anyway.
- **And the half that suppression creates is written down with it, rather than discovered later.** Going quiet inside the zone re-creates the defect the cross-tab named: more than half of every introduced violation is `part <-> trace` on a cell the grid marked as a pad - 109 of stm32_breakout's 206 and 112 of multi_ic's 215 - because a net's own zone covers a **neighbouring part's** pad and switches it off with the rest.
- **The resolution is to use each structure for what it knows.** The field gives a distance and no net, and is right outside the zone. `pad_owner` gives a net and no distance, is already per-cell, and is right inside it. So inside a pad zone the barrier is narrowed rather than switched off: it applies to cells whose `pad_owner` is a **different** net and to nothing else. A route stays free to reach its own pin and keeps paying for a stranger's pad it is sitting on.
- **Written so it can fail**, which is what the rest of this plan is for: if that narrowing does not move the cross-tab's `part <-> trace on a pad cell` figure, the diagnosis behind the whole plan is wrong about where the violations come from, and step 4 dies with it.
- Proof: `grep -n "pub struct PadZone" -A 12 crates/cypcb-autoroute/src/pathfinder.rs` -> centre and radius in grid cells; `grep -n "pad_zones" crates/cypcb-autoroute/src/pathfinder_v2.rs` -> built at line 492 as `pad_zones_per_net`, selected per net at 601, handed to the search at 644 and consulted at 1123. `docs/router-plan.md` 422 -> 497 lines, md5 identical on both sides of the copy.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **step 4, now that it has a design.**~~ Implemented and swept - see the entry above. The barrier from section 2.2, narrowed by `pad_owner` inside the routing net's own pad zones, with `k` swept per board against each board's own noise band. Read `led_blink` and `plane_board` first - their band is zero, which is where step 2 showed its result and where every earlier experiment in this vector hid. The acceptance bar is unchanged and stricter than a total: a change counts only when it moves a board outside its own band, and totals across boards are not a criterion.
- DONE: **step 2 of `docs/router-plan.md` ran, the falsifier fired, and it fired harder than the plan predicted.** The plan said that if pointing the via keepout at real distances moved no board outside its own band, the premise was weaker than claimed. What happened is not "no movement" - it is movement the wrong way. Measured in nanometres instead of ceilinged cells the disc drops from 13 cells to 9, and **three boards get worse outside their own bands while none gets better outside its own**: `led_blink` 2/0 -> 3/1 and `plane_board` 28/13 -> 38/19, both on a measured band of zero, and `qfp_fanout` shorts 149 -> 199 against a band of 44. `stm32_breakout` 187/99 -> 213/113, `multi_ic` 304/200 -> 251/149 and `shift_driver` 65/34 -> 67/39 all stayed inside their bands, which is to say they said nothing.
- **`plane_board` is what settles it.** It routes identically at every via price in the sweep range - that is why its band is zero - and it moved by ten violations and six shorts. That is not the negotiation going differently; it is the board getting worse.
- **The obvious rescue was measured and lost too.** The shipped price of 0.25 was tuned against a 13-cell disc, so a 9-cell disc collects 1.44 times less crowding for the same geometry and `0.25 x 13/9 = 0.36` charges the same total. At 0.36: `plane_board` 45/26 and `qfp_fanout` 393/226, worse than both the shipped disc and the exact one, with `led_blink` still at 3/1. A coefficient does not replace what the extra ring of cells was doing.
- **What it was doing, and this is the finding worth carrying: the over-block is load-bearing.** A via priced only where its copper actually reaches sits one cell closer to foreign copper, and one cell at this resolution is 0.254mm against the 0.127mm the fab requires. The quantisation was buying a safety margin nobody wrote down, and taking it away left the price doing a job it was never measured doing.
- **The plan's central claim is narrowed rather than abandoned, and both documents now say so.** It is not "every rounding removed is a violation saved". It is: a grid of whole cells cannot hold that quantity, **and a consumer that takes the rounding away must put a margin back deliberately**. Step 1 is unaffected - the field measures what it says it measures, and its numbers stand. What is refuted is the assumption that a consumer can take the rounding away and keep everything else.
- Reverted in full, and the revert re-measured back to baseline exactly: `led_blink 2/0, stm32_breakout 187/99, multi_ic 304/200, shift_driver 65/34, qfp_fanout 318/149, plane_board 28/13`, `2 passed`. Nothing of this experiment is in the code; what it produced is a row in the dropped-instruments table, a section of `docs/routing.md` naming the mechanism, and a step in `docs/router-plan.md` that records its own falsification instead of quietly moving on.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **step 4 next, not a retry of step 2 or step 3.**~~ Its open design question is answered - see the entry above. The barrier term from section 2.2 is the one instrument in the plan that carries a margin by construction: it is non-zero *before* contact, so it does not depend on a rounding to keep copper apart. Step 3 - the pad zone reading the field - has the same shape as the instrument that just failed and should not be attempted until step 4 has said whether an explicit margin recovers what an accidental one was providing. Measure `k` per board against each board's own band, and read `led_blink` and `plane_board` first, because a band of zero is where this experiment showed its result and every earlier one hid.
- DONE: **step 1 of `docs/router-plan.md` - the clearance field exists, and nothing reads it.** `crates/cypcb-autoroute/src/clearance_field.rs`: one distance per cell per layer, in nanometres, from a two-pass 3-4 chamfer over copper seeded with **no clearance bloat at all**. The grid marks `pad_radius + clearance_cells`; the field marks `pad_radius`, so a reader gets the distance to the metal and can compare it against whatever a particular pair actually requires, rather than against a number baked in when the grid was built.
- **Built as a separate walk of the world, not by threading state through `RoutingGrid`.** `populate_pads`, `populate_zones` and `populate_locked_traces` are untouched. That is the whole reason the falsifier below could be as sharp as it is: a change that cannot reach the router cannot move the router.
- **The falsifier held.** `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test benchmark_validation -- --ignored` -> `2 passed`, and every board is identical to the digit: `led_blink 2/0, stm32_breakout 187/99, multi_ic 304/200, shift_driver 65/34, qfp_fanout 318/149, plane_board 28/13`. The two boards whose noise band is zero did not move, which is where a perturbation would show first.
- **The chamfer's error is measured, not quoted, and the module's first claim about it was wrong.** The doc comment said the transform always over-estimates along the diagonals. It does the opposite: 3-4 normalised by 3 charges `4/3 = 1.3333` for a step whose true length is `sqrt(2) = 1.4142`, so a diagonal reads **5.7% low**. Caught by the test, which brute-forces the Euclidean answer for every non-copper cell of led_blink against every copper cell: **18481 cells, worst over 1.0541 at (133, 1), worst under 0.9428 at (45, 22)** - both inside 6%, and both bounded by the test so neither can drift unnoticed. The comment now states the direction the measurement found, and says why reading low is the safe direction for a router: a cost term built on this believes copper is nearer than it is.
- **What the transform costs, which was the other thing step 1 had to show.** `--ignored --nocapture`, against grid construction on the same board:

  | board | cells | seeds | grid | field |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | led_blink | 37604 | 419 | 0.10ms | 0.20ms |
  | stm32_breakout | 151552 | 4185 | 0.44ms | 0.80ms |
  | multi_ic | 496440 | 5947 | 1.25ms | 2.61ms |
  | shift_driver | 101304 | 8250 | 0.26ms | 0.54ms |
  | plane_board | 59100 | 2548 | 0.06ms | 0.27ms |
  | qfp_fanout | 66248 | 5923 | 0.12ms | 0.30ms |

  Roughly twice grid construction, and grid construction is not where the time goes: the whole six-board benchmark routes in 2.73s, so the worst board's transform is about a thousandth of its own routing run. **The plan named this as its largest performance risk and it is not one at this rebuild rate.** What is still unmeasured is rebuilding it per net per iteration rather than once, which is what step 2 will actually ask for.
- **Found by writing the test, and worth knowing before step 2 reads the field: `multi_ic` is a four-layer board whose two inner layers carry no copper at all.** Every cell on them answers `i64::MAX` - nothing is near, at any distance - which is a real answer and not a missing one, but it is not comparable against a bloat radius, and the first version of the agreement test overflowed `i64` adding a tolerance to it. Any reader of this field has to handle the bare-layer case explicitly.
- Proof: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test the_field_agrees_with_the_grid` -> **3 passed, 1 ignored**. The agreement test walks every cell of all six boards in both directions: a cell the grid calls blocked is within the bloat radius of copper, a cell it calls free is not inside it, both carried by a tolerance of one and a half cells for the grid's round-up and its disc rasterisation. `cargo fmt --check` clean, `cargo clippy -p cypcb-autoroute --all-targets` clean, `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **step 2 - point the via keepout at the field, and nothing else.**~~ Run, refuted, see above. The narrowest possible first consumer and the one whose arithmetic is already written down: `0.15 + 0.127 + 0.127 + 0.0635 = 0.4675mm` becomes a 2-cell disc of 0.508mm against 0.277mm of real copper. The falsifier is the one the plan states: if no board moves **outside its own band**, quantisation was not what was costing those violations and the premise of the whole plan is weaker than it claims - which is exactly what step 2 exists to find out. It also forces the first real performance question, because a via keepout is asked per candidate rather than once per board.
- DONE: **D1 has a written plan, and it is `docs/router-plan.md`.** The owner closed D1 on 2026-08-09 - in-house router, FreeRouting out, roughly a month, approach mine to propose - and a month of work does not start from a heartbeat fire. The plan is 422 lines and is derived entirely from measurements this project already holds; it estimates nothing and schedules nothing.
- **What it bets, stated so it can be wrong: the seventeen dropped instruments did not fail because their ideas were bad, they failed because a grid of whole cells cannot hold the quantity they were all reaching for.** The arithmetic that makes the case is already in this file: a via keepout is `0.15 + 0.127 + 0.127 + 0.0635 = 0.4675mm`, which at 0.254mm per cell rounds up to a 2-cell disc of 0.508mm against 0.277mm of real copper - an 83% over-block the grid cannot avoid at any resolution it can afford. The finer grid was measured and took stm32_breakout from 15.88 to 39.46 violations per 100mm of copper at double the time, so halving the cell halves the quantisation and doubles the work.
- **What to build, in one sentence: replace cell occupancy with a clearance field - one nanometre distance per cell per layer - and give the search a barrier term that predicts the violation the checker will report.** The barrier is an f64 and not a bool, which is the rule this vector paid seven vetoes to learn. The cost model's `required(pair)` is the same number `cypcb-drc` uses, which is the point: today A* minimises length, vias and congestion history while the board is graded on clearance and shorts, and nothing connects the two.
- **Seven steps, each with its own falsifier written before any number is seen.** Step 1 builds the field with nothing reading it and must leave every board byte-identical. Step 2 points the via keepout at it - the narrowest first consumer, and the one whose arithmetic is already written down - and if no board moves outside its own band there, the premise is weaker than the plan claims and that is worth knowing at step 2 rather than step 5. Step 3 is the pad zone, where the cross-tab puts 52-53% of introduced faults. Steps 4 to 7 are the cost term, the loop that keeps the best board rather than the last, the re-ask of the dropped instruments whose mechanism was arithmetic, and the variants as a search.
- **The acceptance bar is in the plan and it is stricter than a total.** A change counts only when it moves a board outside that board's own measured band; totals across boards are not a criterion, because the weighted-heuristic sweep looked like a 96-violation win on the total and was noise on five of six boards plus one real regression. `led_blink` and `plane_board` have a band of zero, so any movement on either is signal in both directions.
- **Named as deliberately out of scope**, so the next fire does not read the plan as licence: a new search algorithm (negotiated congestion is not what has been measured as failing - its termination and its prices are), a finer grid, retuning the composite while two of its six terms are known not to measure what they are named after, and rewriting the variants.
- Proof that the numbers the plan quotes are the numbers the code produces today: `cargo test --release -p cypcb-autoroute --test benchmark_validation -- --ignored --nocapture` -> `2 passed`, with `led_blink 2/0, stm32_breakout 187/99, multi_ic 304/200, shift_driver 65/34, qfp_fanout 318/149, plane_board 28/13` and every ratchet held. The same run also confirms one of the plan's claims directly rather than by citation: **`Smoothness 1.000` on all six boards and both strategies**, which is the term that scores perfectly without examining a corner because a 45-degree turn costs nothing.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **step 1 of the plan - the clearance field, with nothing reading it.** A distance transform over the obstacle set, one `i32` of nanometres per cell per layer, populated beside the occupancy grid and consumed by nobody. Two things have to be shown: that the field agrees with the grid wherever the grid can answer (a blocked cell has a field below the bloat radius, a free one does not), and what the transform costs per board. The falsifier is unusually sharp for a first step - **any** movement in any board's violation count means the transform perturbed something it must not touch, because nothing reads it yet.
- **The repair pass wins by emptying the board, and its own log now says so.** `repair_routes` is the machinery meant to clean up exactly the faults a user sees - a trace crossing a pad it does not belong to - and it is off by default because the benchmark numbers never moved. Turned on and asked to narrate, on `multi_ic`:

  ```
  repair off: Complete, 945 routes, 291 violations, 0.5s
  Repair attempt measured radius=0 pass=1 violations=40 best=256 routes=108 vias=22
  Repair complete violations_before=256 violations_after=40 accepted_attempts=1
  repair on : Complete, 108 routes, 275 violations, 2.8s
  ```

  The attempt it accepted carries **108 routes against 945**. It did not repair the board, it emptied it: `measure` applies the previous result to the world before the next attempt is routed, so the connectivity analysis sees most pins already joined, the ratsnest is nearly empty, `route_with_blockers` routes the handful that is left, and `is_complete` says `Complete` because nothing was abandoned - there was nothing to abandon. Forty violations on a board with an eighth of its copper beats 256 on a full one, so the pass takes it.
- The `routes` and `vias` counts in that log are new. Without them the line reads as a repair that worked, which is how this survived being looked at twice.
- DONE: **`route` and `check` graded the same board differently, and the checker was the one at fault.** `cypcb route --in-house examples/blink.cypcb` reported 4 violations with 2 shorts about the board it was writing; `cypcb check` on that file reported 5 with 3. It is not the writer - routing the board and checking the world, then writing it out and checking what comes back, gives identical lists both before and after this change, printed side by side by `which_violations_the_file_adds`.
- The clearance rule reported one closest approach per segment of one side of a pair, so the count depended on which entity the spatial index handed over first, and that depended on how the copper happened to be grouped - which the DSL writer changes, because it splits a net into one block per branch. On the routed blink the router lays a GND run straight up x=22.479 from y=20.955 to y=26.035, one segment, and VCC crosses it twice: a horizontal segment at y=21.971 and a diagonal cutting x=22.479 at y=24.765. Two real shorts. Per segment of GND that is one segment and one report; per segment of VCC it is two.
- Both directions are measured now and merged, with contacts closer together than the copper is wide treated as one - the scale the measurement is made at, rather than a tolerance chosen to make this case come out right.
- Re-measured on all six benchmark boards against each board's own noise band: stm32_breakout 180/93 -> 187/99 (band 59/61), multi_ic 291/187 -> 304/200 (65/56), shift_driver unchanged, qfp_fanout 309/147 -> 318/149 (57/44), plane_board and led_blink unchanged. Every movement inside its band, no ratchet moved, and **the two boards whose band is zero did not move at all** - on a board the router is deterministic on, a checker that had started inventing contacts would show it there.
