- DONE: **the gate could have been testing a different build, and now cannot.** Chasing the flaky Playwright run turned up something worse than a flake: `playwright.config.ts` sets `reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI`, and the gate did not set `CI`, so stage 6 attached to whatever was already listening on port 4321. Proven rather than reasoned about - a bare `python3 -m http.server` on that port was enough for the default run to execute the entire suite against it without a word; had it been an older build of the app, the tests could have **passed against stale code**. `CI=1` on the stage makes Playwright start its own server from this tree, and refuse with `already used` when it cannot. Three standalone runs before the change were clean at 93 passed / 17 skipped, so the one 92-passed run is most likely this: a dev server left over from an earlier manual run.
- DONE: **audited for tests that cannot fail, and the audit found a missing rule.** Fourteen Rust tests run code and assert nothing; eleven are legitimate - `#[should_panic]` carries the assertion, or the test exists to prove a trait is object-safe and fails at compile time. Three were not: `small_via_drill_violation`, `normal_via_drill_ok` and `component_with_unconnected_pin` in `drc_sandbox.rs`, each ending in `let _ = result;`. Asserting what those actually do turned up a hole: **a via with a 0.1mm drill against a 0.3mm minimum produced no drill violation at all.** `ViolationKind::ViaDrill` and `DesignRules::min_via_drill` both existed with nothing enforcing them - the diameter rule checks the copper ring, and nothing checked the hole. `ViaDrillRule` does now, and the three sandbox tests assert their outcomes: one drill finding, none, and one unconnected pin.
- QUEUED: three `benchmark-screenshots` failures that route boards through the hidden Route UI (blocked on D5).
- DONE: the recent-projects list had two different limits. `addRecentFile` capped storage at 10, `populateRecentFiles` rendered whatever was stored, so a list seeded by an older build or a test showed rows the app would silently drop on the next save. One `RECENT_FILES_SHOWN` now serves both. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `npx eslint src/project-manager.ts` clean, `npx vitest run` 149 of 149.
- ~~QUEUED: put the browser system deps somewhere durable~~ **Done:** `scripts/setup-dev.sh`.
- **Owner decision, 2026-08-05: no GitHub Actions.** Tests run locally, in the `code-server` container, and that is the whole CI story. Nothing is pushed - `fix/cli-check-drc` has no upstream and 42 commits sit ahead of `origin/main`. `.github/workflows/` is empty and stays that way. `scripts/quality-gate.sh` is the gate; run it by hand.
- DONE: **a fresh clone builds.** `crates/cypcb-parser/grammar/src/` was gitignored, so a clone arrived without `parser.c` and `build.rs` panicked before anything compiled - the project could not be built by anyone who had not generated the grammar first, which meant node, npm and the tree-sitter CLI just to compile a Rust workspace. The generated parser is committed now, which is what every tree-sitter grammar repository upstream does. Verified the only way that means anything: cloned the branch into a directory that has never seen node, `cargo build --release -p cypcb-cli` finished in 35s, and the binary checked two examples including the one that imports a module library.

### V4 - Architecture and deduplication
- DONE: **the IPC-2221 trace width is computed in one place, and the phase map now says what is measured rather than what was planned in March.** The viewer held its own copy of the formula in `ipc2221MinWidthNm` - k, both exponents and the thickness of an ounce of copper written out again - beside `cypcb-calc`, which the router, `cypcb check` and the language server all go through. Measured against each other before deleting anything: 100mA **12542** viewer / **12541** calc, 250mA 44385 / 44385, 500mA 115465 / 115465, 1A 300376 / 300376, 2A **781411** / **781410**, 5A 2765426 / 2765426. Agreement to a nanometre, which is what a duplicate looks like until somebody edits one side - and this exact rule had already drifted once, when the language server used 1.37 mils for an ounce against 1.378 and quoted widths 0.58% off what the router draws.
- `PcbEngine::min_trace_width_for_current_ma` was already exported to wasm and reached nobody: the TypeScript `PcbEngine` interface did not declare it. Plumbed through the raw wasm interface, the interface, the adapter and the fallback engine, and `interaction.ts` asks the engine at both places it picks a routing width. The fallback engine returns 0 rather than a second copy of the arithmetic - it is the build that cannot read a board either, and the caller keeps its default width.
- `viewer/src/__tests__/one-width-formula.test.ts` holds it: no viewer source may carry two of the three constants in code, `interaction.ts` has to name the engine call, and the interface plus both implementations have to declare it. Mutation-checked against the pre-fix tree - all three fail, the first naming the offender: `expected [ 'wasm.ts: 0.048, 0.725, 1.378' ] to deeply equal []`.
- **Two comments outliving their code came out with it**: a "shared parsing utilities" heading with nothing under it, and the doc comment of `parseSource`, deleted a day earlier.
- Proof: `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint src/` silent; `npx vitest run` -> **28 files, 234 passed**; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`. The phase map at the top of this file was re-measured in the same commit: a fresh `git clone` plus `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` gives 119 suites, 1467 passed, 0 failed in 1m18s from cold, which closes P1, and the gate closes P2. Both had been done for days while the map still said P1 was current.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector that is not owner-blocked. The remaining duplication is D3's orphan crates.
- DONE: **the copper pour is computed in one place.** `cypcb_world::copper::fill_zone` is the only code that turns a zone into copper; the Gerber writer and the viewer's snapshot both call it. It was in `cypcb-export`, one crate away from the renderer that needed it, which is how a second implementation starts. See V5 for the measurements.
- DONE: manufacturer rules had two copies that disagreed - the router routed JLCPCB to 0.127mm clearance while the checker demanded 0.15mm, so a correctly routed board failed its own DRC. `cypcb-drc` already depended on `cypcb-rules` without using it; the seven fab presets are now one line each on top of `DesignRules::from_constraints`, and `drc_presets_do_not_diverge_from_routing_constraints` walks every pair so they cannot drift again. Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-drc` -> 113 + 31 + 23 passed, consumers green, gate clean.
- DONE: settled the question the second parser rests on. `cypcb-parser`'s manifest says tree-sitter is "not WASM compatible"; it is - `cargo build -p cypcb-parser --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` succeeds, and so does the whole render crate with `--features native`. `PcbEngine::load_source` now lives in the wasm-bindgen impl, so the browser can reach it. Measured cost, wasm-pack release: 702,357 bytes with the parser absent against 804,520 with it exported, about 100KB.
- **Why the JS parser could not just be deleted, found by trying.** It is not only a parser. `getFootprintPads()` consults `dynamicFootprintRegistry`, a map the viewer fills at runtime from EasyEDA fetches: a `.cypcb` file names a package like `LQFP-48` without describing its pads, and for anything outside the built-in library the geometry only exists because the JLCPCB insert flow went and got it. The Rust engine had no way to be told about such a footprint, so switching to `load_source` would have rendered every fetched part as an empty outline. Proven rather than assumed - the engine answers `unknown footprint: 'XKCD-2'` for a package nobody handed it.
- DONE: `PcbEngine::register_footprint` closes that gap, on both targets - `JsValue` for the browser, a JSON string natively so it is testable without a browser - and lands in the same `FootprintLibrary` the parser resolves against. Three tests: an unknown package is a hard parse error until the footprint is registered and then parses clean with both pads in the snapshot; a registration survives three re-parses, because the editor re-parses on every keystroke while the fetch happens once; malformed pads come back as an error rather than silence. Verified in the generated bindings too: `wasm-pack build --features native` exports it.
- **Measured, so the size question is settled:** the wasm module with the tree-sitter parser compiled in is **841,166 bytes against 702,332** without, +139KB for +20%. That buys deleting ~600 lines of hand-written regex parser that has no unit tests at all - no vitest file imports `parseSource`, its only coverage is the Playwright suite.
- DONE: `PcbEngine::register_3d_model`, the second runtime registry the viewer keeps. Same story as a fetched footprint - nothing in a `.cypcb` file says which 3D model a package uses, and the viewer learns it from a supplier. Plain strings, so one method serves both targets; exported in the generated bindings. Tested three ways: no model before registration, the right model after, and a package nobody registered does not borrow someone else's.
- **"Retire the JS parser in one step" was wrong, and reading the adapter is what showed it.** `parseSource` is not the only thing in the way. The JS snapshot is the viewer's *board model* - traces, vias, drag, undo all mutate it - and `get_snapshot()` merges WASM's violations with two checks that exist only in JavaScript, `checkSilkClearance` and `checkTraceCurrentViolations`. `MockPcbEngine` is a second full implementation of that model for when WASM fails to load. Deleting the parser without those is not a smaller change, it is a broken one.
- **One claim in the adapter was already stale.** It caches the JS snapshot because the engine's "would have empty traces since we only populated components/board" - true of `load_snapshot`, where JavaScript hands over components and nothing else, and not true of `load_source`. Verified: `uat-routing-locked.cypcb` through `load_source` yields a snapshot carrying its `trace VCC` block. A regression test now says so.
- DONE: **the IPC-2221 trace-width formula was in five places, not four, and they disagreed.** `cypcb-calc` had the real implementation and zero consumers; `cypcb-lsp/hover.rs`, `viewer/src/wasm.ts` and *two* inline copies in `viewer/src/interaction.ts` each did the arithmetic again. The language server took 1 oz copper as **1.37 mils** where every other copy uses **1.378**, so its hover quoted a width 0.58% off what the router draws - 0.3021mm against 0.3004mm at 1A. The language server now calls `cypcb-calc`, which un-orphans that crate; `PcbEngine::min_trace_width_for_current_ma` exposes the same one implementation to the browser; the three JavaScript copies are one exported helper, which dies with the parser swap. Five down to two, one per language, and a test pins the engine to the calculator rather than to a number.
- DONE: **M1 closed, and the check it was blocking ships.** `NetRegistry` now carries a design's per-net constraints - width, clearance, current - as a `Vec` indexed by `NetId`, so a lookup is one bounds-checked load rather than a hash of a net name, and a board that declares nothing costs one `None` per net. `sync_ast_to_world` fills it from the `net X { ... }` block, which the DSL has always parsed and the board model has always thrown away. `TraceCurrentRule` reads it: a net that says `current 3A` and is drawn 0.2mm wide now fails, with the layer taken into account because buried copper cannot shed heat. Proof end to end, parser through CLI: `net VCC [current 3A]` on `uat-routing-locked.cypcb` reports `trace 'VCC' is 0.200mm wide for 3.0A: IPC-2221 wants 1.367mm on an outer layer`.
- **Same defect as K012, in the half nobody had checked.** `apply_routes` learned to spawn `NetId` as its own component so DRC's same-net exemption could see it. Traces written in the DSL never did: `sync_ast_to_world` spawned `(trace, span)`. So a hand-written trace collided with the pad it connects to, and every message about it read `trace '?'`. The same file went from 3 violations to 1 - the two clearance findings were false and the remaining one is real.
- **The swap was attempted and reverted, and the reason is not what the tracker said.** Every viewer capability the engine lacked is now in the engine, so the code side went fine: the adapter parsed through `load_source`, `parseSource` and the JS-only checks and `MockPcbEngine` came out - 1615 lines down to 695 - and `tsc`, `eslint` and vitest 149/149 were all clean. The application then failed to load. **`wasm-pack build --features native` produces a module with 25 unresolved imports from `env`**, and Vite cannot resolve them: `Failed to resolve import "env" from "pkg/cypcb_render.js"`.
- **What those 25 symbols are settles the question.** Not libc - they are tree-sitter's own runtime plus our grammar: `ts_parser_new`, `ts_tree_root_node`, `ts_tree_cursor_goto_first_child`, `tree_sitter_cypcb` and 21 more.
- **Correction to the line above, which this tracker got wrong for one commit: the C *is* compiled, for the wrong architecture.** `target/wasm32-unknown-unknown/.../libtree-sitter.a` exists and its members are ELF machine 62 - x86-64. The container has `/usr/bin/gcc` and no clang, so `cc` was handed `TARGET=wasm32-unknown-unknown`, fell back to the host compiler and archived host objects. **The wasm linker skips wrong-architecture archive members in silence**, which is why `cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` reported success and why the earlier measurement in this file - "the parser does build for wasm32" - was true about the command and worthless about the artifact.
- DONE: **the build says so now instead of shipping it.** `cypcb-parser/build.rs` reads the object `cc` produced and refuses a wasm32 build whose first object does not start with `\0asm`, naming both ways out. Verified in both directions: `cargo build -p cypcb-parser` native still finishes, and the wasm32 build stops with `the C parser was compiled for the wrong architecture ... starts with [127, 69, 76, 70]`. The shipping build is untouched - `--features wasm` still produces 0 imports from `env`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: decide how the engine gets a parser it can carry into a browser~~ **Decided and done, and this line outlived it by a dozen fires.** Route (b) shipped: the Rust reader is `parse` everywhere, `cargo tree --workspace -i tree-sitter` finds no such package, and the TypeScript reader is deleted. See `docs/one-parser.md` for the costing and the measured drift that settled it.
- DONE: **the browser had a second copy of two design rules, and both were dead.** `silk-clearance` and `trace-current` are Rust rules the engine already runs; `viewer/src/wasm.ts` implemented them again in TypeScript and the adapter appended the results to what the engine found. A vitest against the adapter with a stub engine shows the append doubling a violation - `expected [ ...(2) ] to have a length of 1 but got 2` - and the two copies had drifted badly: the Rust silk rule learned about printed designators and about clipping the legend off copper, and the TypeScript one knew about neither. They even used different names for the same defect: `trace-current` from the rule, `trace-width-current` from the module.
- **Worse than duplication: on a real board the TypeScript copies could not fire at all.** Probed in the running app, the engine's snapshot for a board with `net VCC [current 3A]` carries `nets: [{"name":"VCC","id":0,"connections":[...]}]` - **no `current_ma`** - and the TypeScript check reads exactly that field. The silk copy had the matching problem recorded two vectors ago: it only sees artwork an EasyEDA fetch supplied. Two rules that looked like a safety net and caught nothing.
- Deleted: `checkSilkClearance`, `checkTraceCurrentViolations`, their four call sites and the dead helper `segmentNearPoint`. `viewer/src/wasm.ts` **1210 -> 1035 lines**. The fallback `MockPcbEngine` lost its copy too - a fallback that answers differently from the engine is worse than one that answers less. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint src` clean, `npx vitest run` 173 passed, `npx playwright test` 93 passed 17 skipped.
- Proven end to end rather than by reading the code: `viewer/e2e/one-checker.spec.ts` loads a board with a silkscreen fault and one with a trace too thin for its current, and asks the running app what it found. The silk violation comes back in the Rust rule's own wording, `silkscreen over`, and `trace-width-current` appears nowhere.
- DONE: **what a design states about a net now reaches the browser, and one of the two holes was a single word.** `build_snapshot` read a side map that `load_source` filled by walking the AST, behind `#[cfg(feature = "native")]` - the browser filled the same map and then hardcoded `(None, None, None)`. So the viewer saw a net's name, id and connections and nothing else, and `interaction.ts` picked a routing width from `ipc2221MinWidthNm(net.current_ma)` on a field that was always absent: drawing on a 3A net gave it the default width. It reads `world.net_constraints(id)` now, the map and its `NetConstraintCache` are deleted, and the adapter's `cachedNetConstraints` workaround has nothing left to work around.
- **The second hole was bigger than the cfg and only the model could close it.** The AST walk saw constraints written on the `net` block and nothing else, so a net that gets its width from a `netclass` had none - in *both* builds. `sync_ast_to_world` merges the class into the net, which is why the fix is to ask the world rather than to flip a `cfg`. Mutation-checked: `a_net_that_gets_its_constraints_from_a_class_carries_them_too` fails on the previous commit with `left: None, right: Some(500000)`.
- **Proved in the browser, and doing so caught a gate that was lying.** `viewer/pkg` is a committed artifact and nothing regenerated it, so `npx playwright test` had been running against an engine compiled three fires ago. The E2E test written last fire to prove the silk rule reaches the browser passed against that stale bundle and **failed the moment the wasm was rebuilt** - correctly, because the exporter clips the legend now and there is no ink to report. Stage 6 of `scripts/quality-gate.sh` rebuilds `viewer/pkg` before it runs, and the E2E test asserts what is true: a Rust-only rule (`courtyard-clearance`) reaches the screen, the deleted TypeScript wording never appears, and `VCC` arrives carrying `current_ma: 3000`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. The engine is the model, the checker and the parser for the browser, and the snapshot now carries what the design says rather than what a cache remembered. The next fire should look at V5 - `interface` is the last v2 construct that parses and does nothing, and it is the owner's call; everything else in the roadmap is either done or waiting on D1.
- ~~QUEUED: IPC-2221 duplicated in 4 places; orphan crates `cypcb-calc`, `cypcb-kicad`, `cypcb-watcher`, `cypcb-platform`~~ **Both halves are now false, counted rather than remembered.** The width formula lives only in `cypcb-calc`; the viewer's copy was deleted and a test fails if it returns. And of those four crates only **`cypcb-watcher` has zero consumers** - `cypcb-calc` is depended on by 3, `cypcb-kicad` by 3, `cypcb-platform` by 1 (`grep -rl <crate> crates/*/Cargo.toml src-tauri/Cargo.toml`). D3 is about one crate, not four.

### V5 - Features from the roadmap

- DONE: **the language server fell back on an unknown fab and said nothing about it - on the surface where somebody is most likely to be typing the name.** `f506df8` taught the LSP to read `fab` and left this open in the same entry: the CLI refuses an unknown name outright, the viewer draws the board anyway with a diagnostic on the word, and the server checked against JLCPCB and underlined nothing at all.
- **A fourth channel, because the three it had are all errors.** `DocumentState::fab_fallback` carries the name as the document wrote it and the byte range it occupies; `run_diagnostics` turns it into a **warning** with the code `cypcb::unknown-fab`, because the board really is still checked. Cleared in `update` beside the other four, so a corrected name stops being reported on the next keystroke rather than on the next reopen.
- **Placed on the word, not at the top of the file.** The span comes from the resolved AST's board block, so the squiggle sits under `jlpcb` rather than under `fab` or on line 1. `an_unknown_fab_is_underlined_where_it_was_written` asserts the line **and** the column, the column computed from the text rather than written down as a number, and a mutation forcing the span to `(0, 0)` fails it with `left: 0, right: 5`.
- **`SourceFile::board()` instead of a second copy of the same walk.** The viewer's `fab_span` and the server both need the first board block; `cypcb-parser` now answers that once and each caller reads `.fab` off it. `fab_span` is two lines rather than eight, which is the smaller of the two reasons - the other is that the gate runs a copy-paste detector and duplicated walks are what it is for.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-lsp --lib` -> **46 passed**, two of them new. Mutation, dropping the diagnostic -> `left: 0, right: 1`, 1 failed. Mutation, forcing the span to the start of the file -> `left: 0, right: 5`, 1 failed. `cargo clippy -p cypcb-lsp -p cypcb-render -p cypcb-parser --all-targets` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none on `fab`. The word is read by the language, the model, the writer, the checker, the router, the browser and the server, and every surface that cannot refuse an unknown name now says which table it used instead. What is still open from `b26ed2d` is unrelated to the fab itself: **`cypcb export`'s `--preset` names a file-naming convention rather than a design-rule table**, the two flags are spelled identically on two subcommands of one binary, and only the source of one of them says so.

- DONE: **the editor and the language server were both still grading every board against JLCPCB, one commit after the command line stopped.** `b26ed2d` gave the language `fab` and taught `check`, `route`, `score` and `watch` to read it. `cypcb-render` reached for `RulesPreset::from_name("jlcpcb")` by name in four routing entry points and built `DesignRules::default()` in `run_drc_internal`, which is the same table under another name; `cypcb-lsp` did the same in `build_world`. A board written `fab oshpark` was checked against OSHPark on the command line, against JLCPCB in the browser, and against JLCPCB again in the editor's underlines - one design, three surfaces, two answers.
- **One method per surface answers it now.** `PcbEngine::preset()` reads `world.fab()` and falls back to JLCPCB; six callers, the four routing entry points, the checker, and `get_min_clearance_nm` - which is the number the browser's interactive router enforces while a person drags a trace, and which had been JLCPCB's 0.127mm on every board ever opened. The LSP resolves the same way in `build_world`.
- **In the viewer the fallback is not silent.** The CLI refuses a fab it does not know; the editor cannot, because it still has to draw the board. `load_source` reports the unknown name as a diagnostic underlined at the `fab` token - `fab_span` finds it in the parsed AST - naming the failed word, the table used instead, and the alternatives. The LSP falls back the same way and says nothing yet; that is the next action rather than a claim.
- **The LSP test was vacuous on its first run and the failure is what said so.** `DocumentState::new` stores the text and nothing else - `test_document_state_new` asserts `ast.is_none()` right after it - so without `parse()` the world is empty, every count is zero, and the two zero-expecting cases pass while proving nothing. The OSHPark case is what caught it by expecting a number. The expected count was wrong too: `grep -c clearance` on the CLI output counts the summary line as well as the violation, so the discriminator is **one** clearance violation, not two.
- Proof: two traces 0.14mm apart, a gap JLCPCB images and OSHPark does not. `cargo test -p cypcb-lsp --lib` -> **44 passed**; `fab oshpark` -> 1 clearance violation, no fab -> 0, an unknown name -> 0 by falling back. `cargo test -p cypcb-render --features native --lib` -> **48 passed**; `get_min_clearance_nm` is **150000** under `fab oshpark` and **127000** without one. Mutation, making `preset()` ignore the world -> `assertion left == right failed: the board asked for OSHPark`, 1 failed; mutation, dropping the viewer's unknown-name check -> `the fallback has to be visible: []`, 1 failed; mutation, making the LSP ignore the world -> `left: 0, right: 1`, 1 failed. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **the language server falls back on an unknown fab and does not say so.** The viewer publishes a diagnostic at the `fab` token and the CLI refuses outright; the LSP checks against JLCPCB and underlines nothing about the word that caused it, which is the surface where a person is most likely to be typing the name. It has `parse_errors`, `sync_errors` and `import_errors` feeding its diagnostics and needs a fourth channel or a place in one of those three.

- DONE: **D4 - a board says which fabricator it is for, and four commands stop guessing.** `--preset` carried `default_value = "jlcpcb"` on `check`, `route`, `score` and `watch`, which means a caller who asked for JLCPCB and a caller who asked for nothing arrived at the command identical. A board drawn for OSHPark was measured against JLCPCB's table unless whoever ran it remembered to say, and nothing in the output looked wrong. `board b { fab oshpark }` puts the answer where the rest of the board's facts live.
- **Both readers, because the gate compares them.** `fab_property` in `grammar.js`, regenerated with `node_modules/.bin/tree-sitter generate` (`grammar.json`, `node-types.json`, `parser.c`), read by `parser.rs` through `node_kinds::FAB_PROPERTY` and by `reader.rs` - the shipping one - as a board property beside `size`, `layers` and `stackup`. `BoardDef.fab` is an `Identifier` and not a validated preset: `cypcb-parser` reads the language and has no table of fabs to check a name against.
- **`Fab(String)` on the board entity**, with `set_fab`/`fab()` mirroring `set_stackup`/`stackup()` for the same reason - most designs say nothing, and a board that named none has to stay distinguishable from one that named this project's default out loud. `board_as_dsl` writes it back when there is one and invents nothing when there is not.
- **`preset_choice::resolve(flag, world)` is the one place the question is answered**: the flag, then the design, then JLCPCB. The flag still wins, so a caller asking about a specific fab is not overridden by the file. An unknown name is refused with the full list either way, and the message says **where the name was written** - `The board asks for fab 'jlpcb'` against `Unknown preset 'jlpcb'` - because a typo in a file and a typo on a command line are fixed in different places.
- **`watch` resolves per pass rather than once at startup.** The file being watched is the file that names the fab, so an edit to it takes effect on the next save like every other edit. Resolution moved into `check_once`, and a bad name prints and keeps watching instead of killing a watch nobody would leave running.
- **A stale test was rewritten rather than worked around.** `score`'s CLI test asserted `cli.score.preset == "jlcpcb"` with a comment explaining that a command without a default scores against a fabricator nobody named. That is now `None`, and the comment says where the default moved to.
- Proof, read off the line `cypcb check` already prints: a board with `fab oshpark` and no flag -> `2 DRC violation(s) against oshpark_2layer`; the same board with `--preset pcbway` -> `against pcbway_standard`; a board naming no fab -> `against jlcpcb_standard_2layer`. `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --test the_board_says_which_fab_it_is_for` -> **4 passed**; `cargo test -p cypcb-world --test a_board_says_which_fab_it_is_for` -> **4 passed**; `cargo test -p cypcb-cli --bin cypcb preset_choice` -> **4 passed**. Mutation, dropping the fab in `board_as_dsl` -> `the writer dropped the fab`, 1 failed; mutation, dropping the design branch in `resolve` -> **4 failed** across the two suites. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: **`cypcb export` takes a `--preset` that is a different kind of preset, and nothing says so at the point of use.** Its flag names a **file-naming convention** - what a house wants the Gerbers called - not a design-rule table, and it kept its `default_value = "jlcpcb"` here deliberately for that reason. The doc comment on `resolve_preset` explains the difference; the flag's help text does not, and the two flags are spelled identically on two subcommands of one binary. Either rename one or make both help strings say which question they answer.
- DONE: **a click on a buried pad no longer starts a route on copper that pad does not have.** The gap the picker left open one commit ago: `layerForPad` read `LAYER_BIT.TOP` and `LAYER_BIT.BOTTOM` and answered the active layer for everything else, so a pad living only on `Inner1` fell through to whatever the user was holding. `layerBit` had been written for exactly this comparison and had no caller.
- **It asks the board's stack instead of two bits.** Every copper layer the pad actually has, filtered out of `copperLayerNames(layerCount)`; one layer settles it, several let the user's choice stand **as long as the pad is on it**, and a pad on `Inner1` and `Inner2` with `Top` active now answers `Inner1` rather than `Top`. `startRoute` reads the count from `snapshot.board.layer_count`, so a four-layer board is read against four layers.
- **The last branch is the old behaviour and says so.** A mask naming nothing this board has keeps the active layer - it is the only answer left, and it is at least a layer that exists.
- Proof: `npx vitest run src/__tests__/the-layer-you-draw-on.test.ts` -> **18 passed**, three of them new: a pad on `Inner1` only, a pad on `Inner2` only, a buried pad whose active layer is one of its own, and the defect stated directly - `Top` active with a buried pad used to answer `Top`. Mutation, dropping the one-layer case and the membership test: **4 failed**, including `expected 'Top' to be 'Inner1'`. `npx vitest run` -> **41 files, 311 passed**; `npx tsc --noEmit` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector that is worth its cost. The picker names the board's layers, the router starts on a layer the pad has, and `L` walks the stack. What is untested is a **route** that changes to an inner layer mid-way - `flipLayer` is top-to-bottom by design and nothing offers Inner1 during routing, which is a via-placement question rather than a picker one and belongs with the router rather than the toolbar.
- DONE: **the layer picker reads the board's own stack instead of stating two options for every board.** A four-layer board could be drawn since inner layers shipped - `inner-layers.test.ts` and the View menu's own checkbox say so - and the picker offered Top and Bottom while `setActiveLayer` refused `Inner1` by name. `snapshot.board.layer_count` was in the type and read by exactly one caller, the 3D renderer.
- **Three helpers carry it, and each answers one question.** `copperLayerNames(n)` gives the stack in order - `Top, Inner1, Inner2, Bottom` for four - counting inner layers from 1 the way KiCad and `Layer::Inner(n)` both do. `layerBit(name)` gives the mask bit, which for `Inner1` is `1 << 2`. `nextLayer(current, n)` walks down and wraps.
- **`setActiveLayer` validates against the board rather than against two literals**, so the same name is right on one board and wrong on another: `Inner1` is accepted on a four-layer board, refused on a two-layer one, and `Inner3` is refused on four. The refusal names what the board does have rather than only what it does not.
- **`L` cycles the stack; `F` still flips.** They are different questions and were the same key's answer only because the stack was two deep. `F` during routing means the far side of the board, which is what a KiCad user reaches for; `L` means the next layer down.
- **The picker builds its own buttons from the board and rebinds them**, keyed on the stack so a redraw does not throw away a button mid-click. The two in `index.html` are what a blank editor shows until a board says how many layers it has. Inner copper takes the colour the renderer draws it in rather than a swatch invented for the picker.
- Proof: `npx vitest run src/__tests__/the-layer-you-draw-on.test.ts` -> **15 passed**, four of them new: the stack for 2, 4 and 6 layers; an inner layer accepted where it exists; `Inner1` refused on a two-layer board and `Inner3` on a four-layer one; and the cycle wrapping in both stack depths. `npx vitest run` -> **41 files, 308 passed**. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the picker can now name an inner layer and the router cannot start a route on one.**~~ It can now - see above. `layerForPad` reads `LAYER_BIT.TOP` and `LAYER_BIT.BOTTOM` and answers the active layer for anything else, which is right for a through-hole pad and untested for a pad that lives only on `Inner1` - a board with buried copper has those. `layerBit` exists for exactly that comparison and nothing calls it yet; wiring it into `layerForPad` is the next small thing, and a pad on one inner layer is the test that would fail first.
- DONE: **the sixteen swallowed catches read against the owner's own rule - recovered from with a stated fallback, or surfaced, never swallowed.** Read rather than counted, which is what the recorded action asked for. Most were already honest; three were not, and the three are the ones where silence hides something a user needs.
- **Already surfaced, left alone:** `jlcpcb.ts` 244, 266, 347 and 448 each log the error and return null; `settings.ts:148` warns and falls back to the defaults; `settings.ts:172` names what it could not write and how big it was; `wasm.ts:1058` logs and drops to the mock engine; `main.ts:1595` warns on invalid drop data; `main.ts:2588` puts `Routing error: invalid JSON response` in the status bar and logs the payload.
- **Recovered with a fallback the code states, left alone:** `main.ts:110` - a WebSocket constructor throws on an invalid URL, and the fallback is no dev server; `jlcpcb.ts:208` - caches an empty category so the failure is not retried; `jlcpcb.ts:392` - falls through to the EasyEDA path; `jlcpcb.ts:493` - a malformed attribute blob becomes an empty one; `routing.ts:37` and `wasm.ts:33` - `deepSanitize`'s JSON round trip returns the object unchanged.
- **Three were genuinely swallowed and now say something.**
  - `main.ts` `readDiagnostics`: `JSON.parse` of the engine's diagnostics, `catch { return [] }`. An empty list is **how the error panel goes quiet**, and it went quiet exactly when the engine had faults to report and could not spell them. The fallback is still an empty list - there is nothing better - but it is no longer indistinguishable from a clean board.
  - `routing.ts` line 488: `catch (_) { /* ignore */ }` around `deepSanitize` of the snapshot. If it throws, the snapshot keeps its BigInts and every number read out of it afterwards is a BigInt in arithmetic that expects a number - which throws somewhere else, or worse, does not. Ignoring it meant the second failure had no connection to the first.
  - `imports.ts` `readerForBaseUrl`: a failed fetch returns null and the design reports the import as missing, so there **is** a downstream surface - what was lost is why. A 404 and a dead network read identically to somebody staring at a board with no library. Both are named now, the HTTP status separately from the thrown error.
- **Nothing was turned into a thrown error or a dialog.** Each of the three keeps the fallback it had; what changed is that the fallback is stated out loud. A catch that recovers is not the defect - a catch that recovers silently is.
- Proof: `npx tsc --noEmit` clean; `npx vitest run` -> **41 files, 304 passed**; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. The audit the owner asked for is done and its result is that thirteen of sixteen were already right, which is worth knowing in itself - the instruction was written after one bad catch was found in `saveToStorage`, and the fear that there were fifteen more like it was not borne out.
- DONE: **the writer stopped producing a file its own parser rejects.** Found last commit: the language's `identifier` is `[a-zA-Z_][a-zA-Z0-9_]*` with no quoted form, so a net called `VBUS+`, `D+` or `D-` cannot be named at all - and `from-kicad` interns whatever a KiCad file carries, so a world can hold one even though a designer could never have typed it. `board_as_dsl` wrote the name anyway. On `from-kicad` that is caught, because it re-reads what it wrote; on the viewer's save path there is no such re-read, and the result is work the user cannot reopen.
- **The omission is stated in the file rather than made silently, which is the choice already made for copper pours a few lines away in the same writer.** A board carrying such a net now produces a comment naming every one of them - `// 1 net(s) on the source board are not written, because the language has no way to name them: VBUS+` - and leaves their pins and copper out. The file parses; the gap is legible; nobody loses a save.
- **Strictly better than what it replaced on every axis worth having.** Before: the whole file was unreadable, so every net's copper and the board itself were lost together, and nothing said why. After: one net's copper is missing and the file says which and why.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-world --test which_trace_does_not_survive_being_written_down` -> **6 passed**, the sixth now asserting that the written file parses, that it names `VBUS+`, that it carries the phrase `no way to name`, and that the copper is genuinely absent rather than mangled onto another net. Mutation, removing both `is_writable_identifier` guards: `the writer produced a file its own parser rejects`, 5 passed 1 failed. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **175 suites ok, zero failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-world --all-targets` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- NEXT-ACTION unchanged, and this does not replace it: **a quoted name in the grammar, for nets and pads together.** This commit makes the failure honest, not absent - a USB board still cannot round trip, it now says so instead of breaking. The real fix is one grammar change covering both gaps, because they are the same defect: `net_definition` takes `$.identifier` and `pad_definition` takes a number, while a schematic names `VBUS+` and a USB-C receptacle names its pads A1, B4, S1. It needs `npx tree-sitter generate` and the generated `parser.c` rsynced back from the container, both readers, the AST, `sync`, the footprint model and both writers - which is a session, not a heartbeat fire, and is written here as such rather than being started and left half done.
- DONE: **the round trip was broken on purpose, and five of six shapes survive.** With both halves of the journey counted, the tracker's action was to drive the shapes a hand-drawn trace can take through the writer and the reader and see which does not come back. Six shapes, each a different way for the language to fall short: two traces on one net, three, one per layer, one carrying a via, one whose segments do not chain, and one on a net whose name needs quoting.
- **Two traces on one net both come back, and so do three.** That is the owner's report in its simplest form - draw one, draw the next - and at the level of the DSL writer and the parser it does not reproduce. Layers survive. Segments that do not chain survive: `contiguous_runs` splits one entity into two blocks where a segment does not begin where the last one ended, and the test asserts no segment is lost in the split, which a count alone would hide.
- **The via survives and its diameter does not, which is what `dsl.rs` already said.** A via written with a deliberate 0.9mm ring on a 0.3mm drill comes back at 0.6mm - twice the drill, rebuilt because the language has no syntax for the ring. The test pins that number in both directions, so if the comment at the top of that file ever stops being true it says so rather than the comment quietly rotting.
- **The sixth shape is worse than a round-trip loss: the language cannot name the net at all.** `net "VBUS+" { ... }` is refused by the parser - `Missing { expected: "a net name" }` at the quoted token - and a bare `VBUS+` is not an identifier either. So a design carrying a USB net cannot be written by hand, and a world that holds one - which an import produces, because `from-kicad` interns whatever names a KiCad file carries - is serialised by `board_as_dsl` into a file that does not parse. Measured, not argued: the test writes such a world out and asserts the result is rejected.
- **Severity is bounded by one line, and it is worth naming.** `crates/cypcb-cli/src/commands/from_kicad.rs:71` re-reads what it wrote, so on that path the fault is reported rather than silent. The viewer's save path has no such re-read, which is where this would bite without warning.
- Proof: `cargo test -p cypcb-world --test which_trace_does_not_survive_being_written_down` -> **6 passed**. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> **175 suites ok, zero failed**; `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy -p cypcb-world --all-targets` clean.
- NEXT-ACTION: **a net name that needs quoting, in the grammar.** `VBUS+`, `D+` and `D-` are on every USB design there is, and the same gap has already been recorded for pads - `pad_definition` takes a number while a USB-C receptacle's pads are called A1, B4, S1. Both are the same shape of defect and both block the same boards, so they are one change to plan rather than two: a quoted-string form wherever the language names something a schematic named first. Until then the honest statement is that this project cannot round-trip a USB board, and the tests now say so out loud instead of the absence being discovered by a user.
- **Also recorded: the owner's disappearing trace is not reproduced by this.** Six shapes through the writer and the reader, five clean and the sixth a syntax gap rather than a loss. Whatever drops the trace is elsewhere - in the interactive path, the editor's debounce, or the engine's own state - and the census wired into both halves is what will name it when it happens live, because it now prints on `console.error` with the net and both counts.
- DONE: **both halves of the disappearing-trace round trip are counted now.** The engine-to-editor half landed last commit; this adds the other one. `loadDesign` compares the source text against `export_traces_as_dsl()` immediately after the engine parses it, which is where a trace the parser cannot rebuild goes missing. Between the two, the whole journey a hand-drawn trace makes is instrumented: world -> DSL -> editor -> DSL -> world.
- **Counted per net, and only a fall is reported.** The DSL writer splits a net into one block per branch, so a count that rises is that rather than a fault, and reporting it would make the instrument noisy on every ordinary board.
- **An instrument that cries wolf is worse than none, so that is what the new e2e checks first.** `viewer/e2e/the-round-trip-keeps-its-copper.spec.ts` loads every bundled example that declares a trace - discovered by scanning `examples/` rather than pinned to a list, and asserting the list is not empty, because two tests in this project have already passed by measuring nothing - and fails on any `[trace-census]` line the console produces. Five files, silence.
- **The second test is the control, and it is the one that matters.** A test that only ever asserts silence passes just as well when the instrument is disconnected. It feeds the loader a design whose trace names a net the board never declares - copper the parser has nowhere to put - and requires the census to name `GHOST_NET`. Without the wiring in `loadDesign` that test fails.
- Proof: `npx playwright test e2e/the-round-trip-keeps-its-copper.spec.ts` -> **2 passed**; `npx tsc --noEmit` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **break it on purpose, now that both halves answer.**~~ Done - six shapes, five clean, one syntax gap. See the entry above. Drive the interactive router from the end-to-end suite and watch the census: two traces, three traces, one on each layer, one with a via, one whose segments do not chain, one on a net whose name needs quoting. The via case is the one with a written suspect already - `dsl.rs` documents an outer diameter rebuilt from a default rather than read - so it is the one to run first. The owner's report is two occurrences with no pattern; a counter on both halves is what turns that into a cause, and it is now in place to do it.
- DONE: **the disappearing-trace round trip is countable now, which is what the tracker asked for before the hunt.** The owner has twice seen a trace vanish when the next one was drawn, and the reason it has survived two sightings is that the journey is silent: `engine.add_trace` puts copper in the world, `syncTracesToEditor` replaces the managed section with `export_traces_as_dsl()`, the editor's debounce parses that back, and nothing anywhere says how many traces went in against how many came out.
- `viewer/src/trace-census.ts` counts `trace` blocks per net out of the text, diffs two stages, and says what was lost on `console.error` naming the net and both counts. Wired into `syncEditorTraces`, comparing what the engine exported against what the editor holds immediately afterwards.
- **The counter is a scanner over the text and not a call into the parser, deliberately.** The question is whether what the writer produced survives; asking the parser to establish what the writer produced compares the pair with itself, which is the closed loop this project has already been caught by once with KiCad.
- **Writing its tests found a fault in the counter itself.** The first scanner counted the word wherever it appeared, so the line `// a trace to nowhere` interned a net called `to` - a counter that invents copper is worse than none, because the report it prints is noise a reader learns to skip. It strips line comments and requires the block's own bracket now, and the test asserts the absence of that phantom net by name rather than only the total.
- **What this does not instrument, said rather than implied: the parse back.** The comparison covers engine-to-editor. The editor-to-world half needs a hook in the reload path, and a green reading here is not an all-clear - the tracker's own suspicion points at the writer's expressiveness (`dsl.rs` documents a via's outer diameter being rebuilt from a default rather than read), which is on the far side of the half now covered.
- Proof: `npx vitest run src/__tests__/a-lost-trace-is-loud.test.ts` -> **10 passed**, covering quoted net names (`"VBUS+"`, `"D-"`), a net that vanished entirely, a trace that appeared (which stays quiet, because that is a different fault), and that the report goes to `console.error` rather than `console.log` - the silence is the defect's hiding place. `npx vitest run` -> **41 files, 304 passed**; `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean; `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **instrument the other half, then break it on purpose.**~~ The other half is instrumented; see the entry above for what remains. A census taken after `loadDesign` re-parses the text closes the loop; with both halves counted, drive the interactive router from the end-to-end suite - two traces, three traces, one per layer, one with a via, one whose segments do not chain, one on a net whose name needs quoting - and see which of them does not survive being written down and read back. The owner's report is two occurrences with no pattern; a counter on both halves is what turns that into a cause.
- DONE: **the editor could not say which copper layer you were drawing on, and it was drawing on the wrong one.** Reported by the owner while wiring by hand - "nie mamy zadnego warstw pickera i nie wiemy na jakiej warstwie rysujemy sciezki" - and it turned out to be two faults wearing one coat. The layer was never *shown*: `startRoute` and `flipLayer` each wrote a `console.log` and that was the whole surface. And it was never *chosen*: `startRoute` inferred it from the start pad with `(padHit.pad.layer_mask & 0x02) ? 'Bottom' : 'Top'`.
- **That expression tests the bottom bit first, and a through-hole pad has both.** `Pad::through_hole` in `crates/cypcb-world/src/components/physical.rs` sets `layer_mask: Layer::TopCopper.to_copper_mask() | Layer::BottomCopper.to_copper_mask()`, so `0x03 & 0x02` is truthy and **every route started from a through-hole pad began on the bottom of the board** with nothing on screen saying so. On a board of headers, DIP parts and screw terminals that is every route.
- `layerForPad(pad, activeLayer)` replaces it and splits the question the old expression conflated. A pad with copper on one side settles it - there is nowhere else to attach, so a bottom SMD pad starts a bottom trace even when the picker says Top. A pad with copper on both sides settles nothing, and the user's choice is the only thing that can.
- **The layer is editor state now, not route state.** `flipLayer` used to return the state untouched unless a route was in progress, which is precisely what made the layer unpickable: there was no way to decide where the *next* trace would go, and nothing for a picker to show. It flips while idle, `setActiveLayer` sets it outright, and the choice is remembered between sessions (`activeLayer` in settings) because somebody wiring the underside of a board should not be put back on top by a reload.
- **The picker is a segmented control in the toolbar, not another line in the View menu.** The View dropdown already carries "Top layer" and "Bottom layer" checkboxes and they answer a different question - what is *drawn*, not what is drawn *on*. Both readings of the word are now visible at once, and picking a layer that View has hidden says so in the button's own tooltip rather than silently drawing where the user cannot see. The swatches take their colour from `renderConfig.layerColors`, so recolouring a layer in preferences moves the picker with it.
- `L` switches the active layer when idle. It is not `F`, because `F` is "fit board to view" when idle and "flip layer" while routing - the flip during routing stays where a KiCad user reaches for it. Registered in `CLAUDE.md` and in the help modal, which `shortcut-registry.test.ts` enforces.
- Proof: `npx vitest run src/__tests__/the-layer-you-draw-on.test.ts` -> **11 passed**, covering the pad rule in both directions, the idle flip, the refusal of a layer name a board cannot carry (`currentLayer` is written straight into every trace, so an unknown name becomes an unparseable file the user has already saved), and that the picker is in the toolbar rather than nested in the dropdown. Mutation, restoring the old expression: `AssertionError: expected 'Bottom' to be 'Top'`, 1 failed 10 passed. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. `routing.test.ts`, `settings.test.ts` and `shortcut-registry.test.ts` alongside it: 39 passed.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **the same question on a board with more than two copper layers.**~~ The picker reads the board now - see above. `LAYER_BIT` names two bits and `setActiveLayer` accepts two names, while `inner-layers.test.ts` and the View menu's "Inner layers" checkbox say the renderer already handles four. A four-layer board can be drawn but its inner copper cannot be reached by hand, and the picker states two options where the board has four. The board's own layer count has to reach the picker before that is a control rather than a guess.
- **Measured what a copper pour exports today: nothing, silently.** A board declaring `zone GND_POUR { bounds 2mm, 2mm to 28mm, 18mm layer top net GND }` produces a top copper layer with **4 pad flashes and 0 draw commands** - the pour is absent from the manufacturing files entirely. The design reads as finished, because a zone carries a net and the ratsnest treats a pad inside it as connected, so someone who draws a ground plane and sends these files gets a board without one. Filling a zone is polygon subtraction against every piece of foreign copper and its clearance, which is a feature rather than a fire; what shipped is the export saying so: `1 copper pour(s) are declared and not exported`. A board with no zones is silent.- **Measured what a copper pour exports today: nothing, silently.** A board declaring `zone GND_POUR { bounds 2mm, 2mm to 28mm, 18mm layer top net GND }` produces a top copper layer with **4 pad flashes and 0 draw commands** - the pour is absent from the manufacturing files entirely. The design reads as finished, because a zone carries a net and the ratsnest treats a pad inside it as connected, so someone who draws a ground plane and sends these files gets a board without one. Filling a zone is polygon subtraction against every piece of foreign copper and its clearance, which is a feature rather than a fire; what shipped is the export saying so: `1 copper pour(s) are declared and not exported`. A board with no zones is silent.
- DONE: **the pour's geometry, which is the half that decides whether it shorts the board.** `cypcb_export::pour::fill` takes a zone, the copper already on that layer and the fab's clearance, and returns what is left: the zone minus each obstacle grown by the clearance. Both sides are axis-aligned, so a subtraction splits a rectangle into at most four and the answer stays a set of rectangles - exact, cheap, and checkable by area. Curved or rotated copper is boxed first, which pulls the pour back further than it needs, the safe direction. Hand-computed cases pin it: a 2x2mm obstacle in a 10x10mm zone leaves 96 square millimetres in four pieces, and 0.5mm of clearance costs exactly 5 more. Two structural properties are asserted rather than assumed - no two pieces overlap, and no piece reaches into any keepout. Mutation-checked by making clearance grow nothing, which fails the case that measures it.
- DONE: **the pour is copper on the board now.** `export_copper_layer` fills every zone on its layer after the pads, traces and vias are placed, so the regions are cut around copper that is already there. Obstacles are chosen by net: copper on another net is cut out with the fab's clearance around it, copper on the **pour's own net is not** - a plane that keeps clear of the thing it grounds is grounded to nothing. The clearance comes from `ExportJob` because a fab decides it and this crate does not know the fab; a caller who says nothing gets 0.3mm, which is the generous direction. Measured on a board with one ground pour: the top copper layer goes from **0 draw commands to 5 regions and 20 draws**.
- The test reads the regions back out of the Gerber and asks the two questions that matter: no region overlaps the keepout around a foreign pad, and some region reaches a pad on the pour's own net. The first version compared a single coordinate and failed against correct output - a band running under an obstacle legitimately spans the same x - which is a reminder that an assertion written from the shape of the answer rather than from the question is its own kind of wrong.
- **The warning changed rather than went away.** It said pours are declared and not exported, which is no longer true; it now says they are filled without thermal relief - a pad on the pour's own net is flooded solid, which connects it and makes it hard to solder by hand.
- DONE: **a pad on the pour's own net gets a thermal relief instead of being flooded.** Solid copper carries heat away from a pin faster than an iron can put it in, which is why a hand-soldered ground pin on a plane lifts the pad rather than wetting. The pad's keepout is cut with `thermal_relief_gap` and bridged by two crossing bars of `thermal_relief_spoke_width` - four spokes - and the three `DesignConstraints` fields that had carried those numbers with nothing reading them finally mean something, through `PourOptions`.
- **The test caught a short the reasoning missed.** A spoke reaches a quarter of a millimetre past its pad, and on two 0402s a millimetre apart the horizontal bar ran to 8.054mm while a foreign pad's keepout started at 7.9mm - the relief bridging into a neighbour's clearance. Spokes are cut against the same obstacles the pour was cut against now, and clipped to the zone. The test asserts both halves: a spoke crosses the pad's centre so it is connected, and a corner of the gap ring stays open so it is not flooded.
- DONE: **the checker sees a pour that hangs off the board.** A zone is copper, and one reaching past the edge is copper the fabricator's router cuts through - reported by nothing, because zones are not in the spatial index. They cannot be, either: a zone covers every pad inside it, so indexing one would have the clearance rule fire on each. The edge is the question a zone's own outline can answer on its own, so `EdgeClearanceRule` walks the zones beside the index. A pour inside the board says nothing; one over the edge is one violation. The test fails on the previous commit with an empty list.
- **What the clearance rule still cannot see is deliberate rather than forgotten.** The fill keeps the fab's clearance from foreign copper by construction, so a rule measuring the filled rectangles would pass by definition and prove nothing. The checks worth having on a pour are the ones the geometry cannot guarantee: the edge, which now exists, and two zones on different nets overlapping each other, which is the next one.
- DONE: **two pours on different nets over the same copper are reported.** A pour is cut against pads, traces and vias and not against another pour, so two planes declared over the same area both fill it and the board has a ground plane shorted to a supply plane across their whole overlap. `ZoneOverlapRule` reports it, and the exporter deliberately does not pick a winner: which plane owns the contested copper is a decision only the designer can make, and a tool that chooses quietly makes a board nobody asked for. Three things it does not report, each with a test: the same net twice (one plane drawn twice), planes on different layers, and planes whose bounds meet exactly at an edge, which is a designer being precise.
- The registry is 16 rules now - `grep -c "Box::new(rules::" crates/cypcb-drc/src/lib.rs`.
- DONE: **the engine now hands out the copper a pour becomes, and the renderer draws it.** The snapshot carried no zones at all - `grep -n zone crates/cypcb-render/src/snapshot.rs` and `viewer/src/types.ts` both returned nothing, and `wasm.ts` matched `zone` only to skip it - so a board with a ground plane drew as a bare board. `BoardSnapshot.pours` now carries each zone as the rectangles it becomes, filled per layer because what obstructs a pour differs on each, and `drawPour` paints them under the traces at 0.35 alpha. Drawing the outline instead would hide exactly the mistakes a pour causes: a plane swallowing a pad, an island cut off from the net it is meant to be.
- **The fill moved once so it cannot be computed twice.** It lived in `cypcb-export`, which `cypcb-render` does not depend on and should not - so wiring the viewer meant either a second implementation or a shared one. `cypcb_core::pour` holds the geometry and `cypcb_world::copper::fill_zone` holds the board-aware part; the exporter's `export_pours` is now 20 lines that call it and emit regions. This is the silkscreen gap's shape, refused rather than repeated.
- The binding test is what makes that hold: `the_viewer_and_the_gerber_are_given_the_same_rectangles` counts the pieces in the snapshot against `G36*` in the top copper Gerber for the same source and requires them equal. On the previous commit the snapshot has no pours at all, so it reads 0 against the fabricator's regions. A second test asserts no drawn rectangle covers a pad on a foreign net.
- Proof: `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> 67 ok targets, 1363 passed, 0 failed; `npx vitest run` -> 153 passed; `npx tsc --noEmit` silent; `cargo clippy --all-targets` on the four touched crates clean; `cargo check -p cypcb-render --no-default-features --features wasm` builds, so the browser gets it too.
- **Nothing is on screen yet, and saying otherwise would be the claim this tracker exists to stop.** `WasmPcbEngineAdapter.load_source` at `viewer/src/wasm.ts:1181` calls the JavaScript `parseSource`, not the engine, so the snapshot the renderer receives is the one JavaScript built - and that parser matches `zone`/`keepout` only to skip the block. What shipped is the whole chain up to the host: geometry, snapshot field, TypeScript type, `drawPour`, and a test binding it to the Gerber.
- DONE: **the pour reaches the screen, and it did not need the parser decision.** The blocker was recorded as "the viewer builds its snapshot with the JavaScript `parseSource`", which is true and turned out not to matter: `WasmPcbEngineAdapter.load_source` already hands that snapshot **to the engine** through `load_snapshot`. What was missing was zones in the snapshot itself. `BoardSnapshot.zones` carries them both ways now - the host parses an outline, a layer and a net, the engine spawns them and sends back the copper it computed in `pours`, and `drawPour` paints it.
- **The geometry stayed in one place, which was the point.** JavaScript parses `zone`/`keepout` blocks and nothing else; the fill - the outline minus every piece of foreign copper and its clearance, thermal spokes included - is `cypcb_world::copper::fill_zone`, the same call the Gerber writer makes.
- Two collisions found by testing rather than reasoning: `net GND` inside a zone was being read as the start of a net block, because the same three words mean two things depending on where they sit; and a zone with no `bounds` line would have been sent as a rectangle of zero size. Both have tests.
- Proof: `a_zone_sent_in_a_snapshot_comes_back_as_filled_copper` sends the browser's shape of snapshot through `load_snapshot_json` (new, native-only, so the viewer's path is testable without a browser) and gets one pour of the right rectangle back; `zone-parsing.test.ts` checks the outline, the layer mask, the net, keepout against pour, and the zero-size case. `cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop` -> 70 ok targets, 1367 passed; `npx vitest run` -> 156 passed; `npx tsc --noEmit` silent; wasm build clean.
- DONE: **checked that a zone violation reaches whoever holds the snapshot, and it does.** The adapter takes its violations from the engine on every `get_snapshot`, and the engine runs DRC after `load_snapshot`, so `ZoneOverlapRule` firing in Rust is already on the panel with no extra wiring. Proven both ways in one test: two planes on different nets over the same copper report, and the same two moved apart report nothing - a check that passes on any board is not a check.
- **Three NEXT-ACTIONs elsewhere in this file were finished work.** The clearance narrow phase, the courtyard rule and the crowded-nets-first experiment had all landed or been reverted while their lines still read as open. A fresh session reads this file first and would have picked one of them up. Struck through with what actually happened, and the file's own date corrected.
- DONE: **a keepout is visible now.** It was carried in the snapshot, checked by DRC and avoided by the router, and drawn by nothing - so the designer had to remember where they had put it. `drawKeepout` paints it as a dashed outline before the copper, and deliberately not as a fill: a keepout is an absence of copper, and filling it would say the opposite of what it means. It disappears with its layers, so a keepout on the bottom does not clutter a top-side view.
- Three tests fix the shape of that: it strokes and never fills, the rectangle is the size the design gave it (3mm at 1e-5 px/nm is 30 pixels), and a hidden layer draws nothing. `npx vitest run` -> 159 passed.
- DONE: **a pour cut into islands is reported now.** `PourIslandRule` is the sixteenth rule: it fills every pour the way the exporter does, groups the pieces into sheets of touching copper, and reports the sheets no thermal spoke reaches. Copper connected to nothing is dead weight at best and an antenna at worst, and it is invisible in a Gerber preview because it looks exactly like the rest of the plane.
- **Connectivity, not counting.** Two rectangles that meet at an edge are one sheet, so a piece with no spoke of its own can still be perfectly connected - the rule unions the touching pieces first and reports one violation per sheet, not per rectangle. A plane whose net has no pad under it at all is therefore one report rather than a dozen.
- Proven on boards rather than on rectangles: a ground pour with its pad low and a signal trace across the middle reports **1** island; the same board without the trace reports **0**; the same board with the pad moved outside the pour reports **1**. Plus four unit tests on the sheet grouping itself.
- **It caught a live case in another test the moment it was registered.** `overlapping_planes_are_reported_to_whoever_holds_the_snapshot` built two planes with no components at all - which is two islands, correctly - so its control assertion had to be narrowed to the rule it is actually about. A rule that changes nothing else's results is usually a rule that never fires.
- DONE: **the stranded copper is outlined on the board, not just listed.** A violation can now carry an area beside its point - `DrcViolation.area`, `ViolationInfo.area`, and `drawOrphanedCopper` on the canvas - because zooming to the centre of a pour island shows copper that looks exactly like every other part of the plane. Outlined rather than filled: the pour already painted that copper, and covering it would hide the thing being pointed at.
- The point stays the middle of the sheet, so the existing click-to-zoom keeps working; the rectangle is what the outline uses. Tested on both sides: the Rust test asserts the reported area has a size and sits above the cut, and two vitest cases assert the canvas strokes and never fills, at the size the checker reported.
- DONE: **`cypcb check` says how big the stranded copper is.** A violation with an area prints its size and corners under the line that names it, because a coordinate in the middle of a plane points at copper that looks like every other part of the plane. Run on the new `examples/pour-island.cypcb` - a ground pour whose only ground pad sits below a signal trace crossing it:

```
1 DRC violation(s) against jlcpcb_2layer:
  pour-island at (20.000mm, 27.613mm): Pour island: this copper reaches no pad of its own net
      copper 30.000mm x 14.773mm, from (5.000mm, 20.227mm) to (35.000mm, 35.000mm)
```

- That run is the whole chain end to end on one file: the parser reads a zone, sync spawns it, the fill cuts it against the trace, the rule finds the sheet with no spoke, and the CLI prints the copper. A `cypcb-cli` test asserts all three lines, so the numbers cannot drift quietly.
- DONE: **the eighteen boards in `examples/` are checked now, including the four that exist to fail.** `cargo test` never opened that directory - it is the first thing a reader tries, and a change that broke any of it would have been found by a user. The test walks the directory rather than listing it, so an example added tomorrow is covered without anybody remembering: every file must parse, except `invalid.cypcb` and `unknown_keyword.cypcb`, which must not - and if one of those starts parsing, that is reported too, because a file that demonstrates a parse error and does not is no longer teaching anything.
- The two boards written to fail DRC keep failing: `pour-island.cypcb` must still report `pour-island`, `drc-test.cypcb` must still fail and say why. Neither pins a count - the numbers move whenever a rule is refined, and pinning them would make every rule change read as a break.
- **Proven to fail, not just to pass:** an example with `layers` and no value dropped into the directory makes it report `_probe.cypcb: stopped parsing` and the suite goes red. Removed after the check.
- DONE: **read the grammar for words that mean different things in different blocks, and fixed the one that was a trap.** `layer` took `top` in a zone and `Top` in a trace: one capital letter decided whether a line was correct or a syntax error, depending on which block it sat in. Both spellings are accepted in both blocks now - grammar regenerated, `parse_layer_name` made case-insensitive, and the zone mask with it - which is strictly widening, so no file that parsed before stops.
- The other overlap is `net`, and it is legitimate: `net GND` opens a net block at the top level and names the pour's net inside a zone. Both readings parse, only one is right, and the viewer's own parser had the wrong one this cycle. `one_word_one_meaning` pins it: a zone with a `net` line yields one zone and **zero** net definitions.
- `docs/SYNTAX.md` says both spellings are accepted and why they used to differ.
- DONE: **read `README.md` and `docs/architecture.md` against the code, and four claims were wrong.** The README called copper pour unimplemented - it has been filled, drawn, exported and checked for several cycles - and named it as the project's main gap. `cypcb-core`'s type list named `Length`, `Angle`, `Area`, `Circle` and `Polygon`, **none of which exist**; the crate's vocabulary is `Nm`, `Point`, `Rect` and now `pour`. `cypcb-world`'s component list named `Component`, `Net`, `Track` and `Text`, which are not the component names either. And the DRC section said violations are "stored as ECS entities" and that checking "runs incrementally - only checks affected regions": neither is true, and `run_drc_incremental` is a full re-run under a name that promises less work than it does.
- The four DRC checks listed are now seventeen, so the list ships as the command that counts them rather than as a copy that goes stale - and the two commands in the doc were run to make sure they answer what the sentence beside them claims.
- DONE: **`cypcb export` refuses a board with copper touching copper.** It warned about every violation and wrote the files regardless, so a shorted board went to a fabricator with a line of text between it and a wasted order. The line drawn: a gap under spec stays a warning - whether to build a board 0.01mm inside the fab's spec is the designer's call - and a short is not a call, because the board cannot work as drawn. `--force` writes the files anyway and says it did.
- Measured on `examples/drc-test.cypcb`, which has one short among fifteen faults: without the flag the command fails with `1 of the violations are copper touching copper` and **the output directory is not created**; with `--force` it writes 13 files and prints `Forcing:`. Both are tests.
- DONE: **exported all eighteen example boards in a row, and two of them could not be made.** Nobody had run the deliverable-producing command across the examples before. `v2-imports.cypcb` checked clean and then failed with `unknown module: 'Divider'` - `check`, `route` and `score` all resolve imports against the importing file's directory and **`export` was the one command that skipped it**, so a design built from a block library was the one thing the tool could not turn into files. Fixed, and it now writes 13 files.
- `v2-interfaces.cypcb` answered `Export failed: NoBoardSize`, which reads like a missing setting rather than a file nobody meant to manufacture. It declares interfaces and no board: export says so now - "declares no board, so there is nothing to make from it ... a library - import it from a design that has a `board` block".
- Both have tests. The sweep that found them is worth repeating after any change to parsing or sync: `for f in examples/*.cypcb; do cypcb export "$f" --output /tmp/sweep --force; done`.
- DONE: **every example board is exported and read back now, not just run.** `source_to_fabrication.rs` checks one board in depth; this checks all fifteen that declare one, against the invariants that hold for any board whatsoever - so a new example is covered the moment it lands. **135 Gerber layers** read back: each carries its coordinate-format header and its end-of-file marker, because a layer without `M02*` is a file a fabricator's loader stops reading part way through. **15 outlines** measured against the size their source declares, to 0.001mm, because a board that declares 50x30mm and cuts to something else is scrap and nothing else in the file set would show it.
- Both counts are asserted, not just printed: a sweep that inspected nothing passes every assertion it makes. The two tests also write to separate directories - sharing one, they raced, and the first to finish deleted the files the other was still reading.
- DONE: **the copper is read for content now, not only for completeness.** Every pad of every placed part, rotated the way its component is, must be flashed in the layer its footprint names - **185 pads** across the examples, matched to 0.002mm. A pad that never reaches copper is a part that cannot be soldered, and the file set looks perfectly well formed without it.
- **The test failed its first run on itself, which is the useful part.** It looked for `.gtl` and `.gbl`; the JLCPCB preset writes `-F_Cu.gbr`, so it read an empty string and reported every pad as missing. Two things came out of that: the matcher takes both spellings, and a layer it cannot find is now a panic naming the files that were there - reading an empty file and concluding "no copper" is exactly the silent-zero this project keeps banning. It also proves the assertion fires.
- DONE: **the drill file is checked against the copper.** Every pad that states a `drill` must have a hole at its centre, of the size it asked for - **17 drilled pads** across the examples, position matched to 0.002mm and diameter to 0.002mm. Copper without a hole is a through-hole part that cannot be fitted, a hole of the wrong size is a lead that will not go in, and neither shows in a Gerber preview: the drill file is a separate format most previews do not overlay. The Excellon reader handles the tool table the exporter writes - `T1C1.000000` sets a size, `T1` selects it, the coordinates that follow use it.
- **Proven to fail:** doubling the expected diameter in the test makes it report `hole at (4.730mm, 35.000mm) is 1.000mm, the footprint asks for 2.000mm` on four boards. Reverted after the check.
- The fabrication sweep now holds four invariants over every example that declares a board: the layers are complete Gerbers, the outline matches the declared size, every pad reaches copper on its own layer, and every drilled pad has its hole. 135 layers, 15 outlines, 185 pads, 17 holes - each count asserted, because a sweep that inspected nothing passes every assertion it makes.
- DONE: **the assembly half of the deliverable is swept too.** The CPL is what an assembly machine follows and the BOM is what somebody buys from; a board whose CPL is short by one part is assembled with a gap, and neither shows in any Gerber. **72 parts** matched to a CPL row at the position the board holds, to 0.002mm, with the row count held to the part count; **72 parts** accounted for in the bills of materials, each exactly once, with the quantity column summing to the number of parts on the board.
- The BOM groups designators as `"C1,C2"`, so the reader keeps quoted fields whole - splitting on every comma would have turned one line into two and made the count come out right for the wrong reason.
- **Proven to fail:** shifting the expected position by 1mm reports `C1 sits at (49.000mm, 2.000mm) and the CPL says (48.000mm, 2.000mm)` across the examples. Reverted; `git diff` shows only the new tests.
- The fabrication sweep now holds six invariants over every example that declares a board: complete Gerber layers (135), outline against the declared size (15), every pad in its own layer's copper (185), every drilled pad with its hole (17), every part in the CPL where the board puts it (72), every part in the BOM exactly once (72).
- DONE: **the sweep exports each board once instead of six times.** Six tests each re-exported every example for its own invariant - six times the work for the same answer, and the cost grows with the directory. Folded into one pass that exports, then checks everything the files can show. Measured over three runs each: **1.99s -> 1.05s**, and the counts are identical - 135 layers, 15 outlines, 185 pads, 17 holes, 72 placements, 72 BOM lines - so the same board is being checked, faster.
- Each count is still asserted separately, so the pass cannot go green by quietly checking less: a missing drill file is a panic naming the board, and a copper layer nobody could find is a panic listing what was exported.
- DONE: **a routed board goes through the same six invariants.** Every example was checked as its designer drew it, and the router is what changes copper after the designer stops - so nothing held what it writes to the same standard. `blink.cypcb` is routed with the in-house router, applied, exported, and put through the whole sweep: layers, outline, pads, holes, CPL, BOM. The checks were extracted into one function rather than copied, so the two callers cannot drift.
- The invariant only a routed board has: **46 routed segments against 46 copper draws** in the layer files. A trace that reaches the board model and not the Gerber is a connection the fabricator never makes. Proven to fire - demanding twice the draws reports `the router laid 46 segments and the copper layers draw 46 times`.
- DONE: **the routed copper is compared by geometry, not by count.** A polyline that replaces a short segment with a long one leaves the draw count unchanged and the board different - `source_to_fabrication.rs` learned that on one board, and the sweep was counting. Every routed segment's endpoints must now lie **on a stroke the layer actually draws**, within 0.0127mm, which is a tenth of the narrowest trace this project allows. On a stroke rather than equal to a vertex, because the smoother is allowed to merge collinear runs and a merged run keeps the copper while losing the midpoint. **92 endpoints** checked on the routed board.
- Proven to fire: shifting a routed endpoint by half a millimetre reports `a routed end at (4.183mm, 19.939mm) on TopCopper has no copper under it` for every trace on the board. Reverted; `git diff` shows only the new checks.
- DONE: **a four-layer board was exporting as though it were two-layer, and the check that found it is in the gate now.** Running the geometry check over the three benchmark fixtures - already routed in that stage, so it costs one export each - `multi_ic` reported routed ends on `Inner(0)` with no copper under them.
