The cause: both export presets ship `inner_copper: vec![]`, so **every trace the router put on In1 or In2 was absent from the file set, silently**. multi_ic declares F.Cu, In1.Cu, In2.Cu and B.Cu, and the router uses the inner pair.
- The exporter reads the board's own stack now rather than the preset's static list, and names the files the way the preset names the top layer - `-F_Cu.gbr` gives `-In1_Cu.gbr`, `_top.gtl` gives `_inner1.gtl`. **3,260 routed endpoints** across the three fixtures now land on copper actually drawn, inner layers included.
- `scripts/quality-gate.sh` runs it as its own stage, so the next board with inner copper cannot lose it quietly.
- DONE: **the dry run tells the truth about a four-layer board.** It listed the preset's file set, and both presets ship an empty inner-layer list, so it promised a two-layer set for exactly the board whose inner copper was being dropped. It reads the board's stack now, the same rule the export itself follows, and states it beside the preset name - `Board stack: 4 copper layers (2 inner)` - because "JLCPCB 2-Layer" over a four-layer design reads like a contradiction otherwise.
- `examples/four-layer.cypcb` is new and carries a trace on `Inner1`, so the fabrication sweep exercises inner copper end to end: **148 layers, 16 outlines, 187 pads, 74 placements, 74 BOM lines** - up from 135/15/185/72/72 with the board added.
- DONE: **the viewer stopped drawing a four-layer board as though it had no middle**, and a naming defect turned up on the way. The engine wrote `Inner0` for the copper the DSL calls `Inner1` - the same layer with two names depending on which way it travelled - so a viewer matching on the name was matching the wrong one. Both directions agree now, and `parse_layer` takes either spelling because the debug form `Inner(0)` reaches it from elsewhere.
- Every inner layer had come back the same forest green, and only while an outer layer happened to be visible: two traces that cannot cross looked identical, and turning the outer copper off took the inner copper with it. Each layer has its own shade now and its own visibility, `innerCopper`, optional so every existing caller keeps working.
- Five vitest cases hold it: distinct shades per layer, inner copper surviving the outer layers being switched off, `innerCopper: false` hiding it, `Inner1` counted as the first inner layer, and an unrecognised layer name answering nothing rather than a colour.
- DONE: **the inner layers have a switch.** The view menu had a checkbox per side and none for the middle, so the visibility the renderer had just learned could not be reached by a person. `Inner layers` sits beside `Top layer` and `Bottom layer`, drives `innerCopper`, and updates the 3D view the same way the other two do.
- A shared link that never mentions the middle now keeps it visible: links made before inner copper was drawn list only `top` and `bottom`, and hiding half a four-layer board on the strength of an old URL is the wrong reading of silence. The rule is a function rather than a line of DOM wiring, so it has a test in both directions.
- Found while looking for somewhere to put the shades: **the viewer has no colour legend at all** - `grep -i legend viewer/index.html` returns nothing. Not invented here; recorded because the next person to look for one will look in the same place.
- DONE: **the 3D view was drawing inner copper as bottom copper.** `buildTraces` asked "is this Top?" and sent everything else to the bottom group at the bottom layer's depth - so a four-layer board showed its inner traces on the outside of the board, which is worse than not showing them: it is a picture that looks right and is not. Inner layers get their own groups now, extruded **inside** the substrate and evenly spaced through its thickness, in the shade the 2D view uses, and they follow the same `innerCopper` switch.
- Two rules the two views were about to state twice are functions in `layers.ts` instead - which inner layer a name means, and where it sits through the board - with tests: `Inner1` is the first inner layer, `Top` and `Bottom` name none, the retired zero-based `Inner0` names none rather than silently meaning the first, two inner layers land at -0.267mm and +0.267mm in a 1.6mm board, and a single one lands in the middle.
- DONE: **a blind via is drawn as a blind via.** The board model has carried `start_layer` and `end_layer` since vias existed, and `ViaInfo` dropped both on the way to the viewer - so every via was a through hole on screen whatever the design said. The span is in the snapshot now, named the way the DSL names layers, and the 3D barrel is scaled and placed to it: a via from the top face to `Inner1` stops at `Inner1`.
- Old snapshots keep working: a via that states no span goes through, which is what every via was until this landed. Same reading of silence as the inner-layer switch.
- Four more cases in `inner-layers.test.ts`: a through via face to face at 0.8mm and -0.8mm, a blind via from the top face stopping at -0.267mm, a buried via between the two inner layers, and a via with nothing stated spanning the full 1.6mm.
- DONE: **a buried via is no longer drilled from the outside.** An Excellon file with no stated layer pair means "through the whole board" to every fabricator, and every via went into that one file whatever its span - so a board with a via between two inner layers came back with a hole punched clean through it. Drill hits carry their span now, the through file carries only through holes, and every other pair gets a file named for it: `board-PTH-In1-In2.drl`.
- A two-layer board asks for no extra files, which is the case that must not change - `non_through_spans` returns an empty list and the file set is what it always was.
- Three tests: the through file has the through via and not the buried one, the pair's file has the buried one and not the through one, and a board of through holes alone produces no extra spans.
- DONE: **the checker asks which drill pass makes a hole before measuring it against another.** `HoleToHoleRule` compared every drilled feature with every other, so two vias buried on different sub-stacks - one between In1 and In2, one between In3 and the bottom - were reported as too close although they are made in separate passes before the board is pressed together. A designer told about faults that are not there stops reading the list.
- The rule is the physical one: two holes have to keep their distance only if their layer ranges overlap. Spans that merely touch at a layer are two passes, not one; a through hole overlaps everything, so the direction that matters is unchanged - a blind via too close to a through hole is still reported.
- Seven tests: four on the pass arithmetic, three on boards - two through holes 0.05mm apart are a fault, the same two on different sub-stacks are not, and a blind via under a through hole still is. The benchmark gate is unmoved at 133/58 and 140/37, because the fixtures have no buried vias.
- DONE: **the language can say which layers a via joins.** Three parts of the tool read a span - the viewer draws it, the drill export gives it a file, the hole rule measures against it - and the DSL had no way to state one, so a blind or buried via could only arrive from a KiCad import and a design saved from the viewer lost it. `via 12mm, 10mm drill 0.3mm layers Top to Inner1` says it; a via with no pair goes through, which is what every via written in this language has always been.
- The writer leaves the pair off a through via rather than spelling it out on every line: its absence already means through, and `layers Top to Bottom` on every via would be noise in every file. `docs/dsl.rs`'s note about what the language could not say is one item shorter - the outer diameter is still rebuilt from the drill.
- Four tests: a via with no pair comes back through, a stated pair survives sync, the pair survives a round trip through the writer and back, and a through via is written without it.
- **That claim was wrong, and measuring took less time than writing it did.** "The router still places through vias only" - it does not. `postprocess` builds each `ViaPlacement` from the layer transition that produced it and `apply_routes` copies the pair onto the `Via`, so the span has been correct all along. Counted on multi_ic, a four-layer board: 12 through, **4 Bottom-In1, 1 Bottom-In2, 2 In1-In2, 3 In1-Top**. Fourteen of twenty-six vias are blind or buried. `which_layers_the_router_joins_with_a_via` counts them rather than anybody claiming it again.
- DONE: **`cypcb route` says how many of them there are.** A blind or buried via costs several times what a through hole costs to make, and a four-layer board collects them without anyone asking for one. The count sits beside the segment and via totals, and a two-layer board says nothing because it cannot have any.
- DONE: **the router drove straight through copper the designer drew.** Checking the `--in-house` writer - the small question - turned up the larger one: `RoutingGrid` marked **locked traces only**, so a hand-drawn trace nobody thought to lock was invisible and the router routed across it. Measured on a four-layer example with a hand trace and a net that has to get past it: **1 violation, copper touching copper, 0 vias** before; **0 violations and 1 via** after. `locked` means "do not rip this up"; unlocked copper is still copper.
- The benchmark gate does not move - 133/58 and 140/37 - because the fixtures carry no hand-drawn traces, which is also why nothing caught this.
- Two tests, and the first fails on the previous commit with `left: 1, right: 0`: the router does not drive through an unlocked hand trace, and it never did through a locked one.
- The small question is answered too: `--in-house` writes through `traces_as_dsl`, which carries the layer pair when there is one - the via it placed here is a through via, so the pair is correctly absent.
- DONE: **a net the designer already wired is not wired again.** The router asked for a spanning tree over every pad of every net, so copper on the board was ignored twice over - once as an obstacle, fixed last commit, and once as a connection. A board with a hand-drawn trace came out with two pieces of copper for one connection, the second taking space the rest of the board needs. A pad sitting on its net's existing copper is connected; only one pad per piece of copper needs a route to it.
- Measured on `examples/four-layer.cypcb`, which carries a hand trace on `Inner1`: **4 segments and 1 via** before, **1 segment and 0 vias** after, no violations either way. The routed board is the connection that was actually missing, and nothing else.
- The test asserts the strong form: a two-pad net joined pad to pad by a hand trace produces **zero routes**. The benchmark gate is unmoved at 133/58 and 140/37 - the fixtures arrive unrouted, which is why neither half of this was caught there.
- DONE: **the "already connected" test asked where, not which layer.** Written down as something to measure, it turned out to be a defect on reading: the pad-to-copper check compared boxes and ignored layers, so a bottom-layer trace crossing under a top-layer pad counted as a connection - two pieces of copper with the board between them - and the router dropped the route that was actually missing. The board would come back with a pin wired to nothing.
- The check now requires the trace's layer to be in the pad's layer mask. The test builds exactly that board and asserts the route is still made; it fails on the previous commit with "the pads are on top and the copper is on the bottom, so the connection is still missing".
- The doubt in the note - a trace that merely passes over a pad of its own net on the same layer - is not a defect: same-net copper touching on the same layer **is** a connection, which is what the box test says. Same layer was the missing half, not the endpoint.
- Gate unmoved at 133/58 and 140/37; workspace 79 ok targets, 1410 passed.
- DONE: **a via the designer placed counts as the copper it is.** Two traces of a net that meet only through a via read as two separate pieces, so the router added a connection that was already there. A via joins the traces it lands on - on the layers it actually reaches, so a blind one joins only its own span - and the pieces it joins are one piece.
- The test wires a through-hole pin on top, drops through a via, and comes back on the bottom: **0 routes** where the router previously produced 5. It fails on the previous commit with `left: 5, right: 0`.
- Writing that test caught a board of my own that was electrically wrong before the code was: with surface-mount pads, a bottom-layer trace under a top-only pad connects nothing - which is exactly what the layer check added last commit says, and it said so about my fixture first.
- Gate unmoved at 133/58 and 140/37; workspace 79 ok targets, 1411 passed; clippy clean.
- **The pour half of that claim was wrong, and the test said so before the code did.** "The router still routes every ground pin to the plane" - it does not: `extract_ratsnest` has dropped pads inside a pour of their own net since a zone learned its net, `orchestrator.rs:132` says so, and the board written to prove the gap passed the moment it was written. The pour-handling code I had added to the pruning pass was redundant and is reverted; `git status` shows only the test.
- **The test stays, and that is the useful outcome.** The behaviour was one line in the ratsnest with nothing naming the board it protects: three ground pins in a ground plane, zero routes. Now it is held.
- Second wrong claim in three fires on this line. Both came from writing a next action from memory rather than from the code; both cost a fire to disprove and left a test behind. The cheaper order is grep first, claim second.
- DONE: **read `UnconnectedPinRule` before claiming anything, and it measures intent rather than copper.** It asks whether `NetConnections` names a net for a pad. A pin listed in `net GND { U1.4 }` passes whether or not one millimetre of copper was ever laid, so a board with an open circuit checks clean and nothing in the file set says otherwise.
- `UnroutedPinRule` asks the other question - is there copper of that net touching this pad, on a layer the pad is on, whether trace, via or pour. **It is written and deliberately not registered**, because measuring it raised a question the code has to answer first.
- **The measurement, and what is unresolved.** Pins the rule reports, before and after routing: led_blink **12 -> 1**, stm32_breakout **132 -> 21**, multi_ic **229 -> 60**. The before numbers are what an unrouted board should say. The after numbers are the problem: the router reports zero unrouted connections on all three, and for the pins still reported, copper **of the same net** sits 0.07mm to 0.18mm from the pad centre - well inside a 0.8mm pad. Either the rule's overlap test disagrees with its own inputs, or those pads are reached by copper that ends beside them rather than on them. The rule is unregistered until that is settled, because a checker nobody can trust is worse than one that says less.
- DONE: **the rule was right and the router is wrong.** Printing the boxes settled it in one run: `D1.2` on led_blink is a surface-mount pad whose layers are `[TopCopper, TopPaste, TopMask]` - top only - and the nearest copper of its net is on **BottomCopper**, ending at x=30.924mm beside a pad at x=30.750mm. The router took the connection to the pad's position on the wrong side of the board and stopped: no via, no top copper, an open circuit at that pin. It reported the board `Complete` with zero unrouted connections.
- Same shape on the other two: 21 pins on stm32_breakout, 60 on multi_ic, each with same-net copper 0.07mm to 0.18mm away and none of it on the pad's own layer.
- **The mechanism is not identified yet, and two obvious suspects are already ruled out.** The pad's layer mask is built with `layer_to_index`, which answers only for copper, so `TopPaste` and `TopMask` do not set bits and `is_multi_layer` is false for this pad. The search's `success` predicate then requires the exact layer. Something between those two and the emitted segments does not hold, and naming it without reading that path would be the third wrong claim in five fires.
- **Two more suspects ruled out, and the remaining one is now narrow.** `pad_to_grid_node` asks `preferred_layer`, which returns 0 whenever bit 0 is set - a top-only pad gets layer 0, and the search's `success` will not accept another. So the end node is right and the predicate is right. And there is no via: the nearest via of GND is **8.5mm** from `D1.2`, and `U1.3`'s net on stm32_breakout **has no vias at all** while its copper runs on the bottom and both its pads are top-only. So this is not a dropped stub after a layer change - the route never came up, and no layer change was ever made.
- What that leaves: either those pads are not in the ratsnest the router worked from, or the connection that "reached" them was an edge between two other pads. Both are questions about `extract_ratsnest` and `build_spanning_tree`, not about the search.
- DONE: **found it, and it is worse than a wrong layer: the router emits layer changes with no via.** The ratsnest is right - `D1.2` is pad 2 of GND, mask `0b01`, and `build_spanning_tree` names the edge `pad 1 -> pad 2`. The copper laid for that edge reads:

```
TopCopper    (20.447,15.113) -> (22.733,12.827)
BottomCopper (22.733,12.827) -> (25.527,10.033)
BottomCopper (25.527,10.033) -> (30.861,10.033)
```

- The route starts on top at pad 1, **changes layer at (22.733,12.827)**, finishes on the bottom beside a top-only pad, and **GND has no via anywhere near either point** - the whole net has none within 8.5mm. Two pieces of copper on two layers that never join, reported as `Complete`.
- That also explains the quiet: DRC sees no overlap because the copper is on different layers, and the unrouted count is zero because a path was returned for every edge. Every check the project has agrees the board is fine, and the board is open in three places on led_blink alone.
- **Cause found, fix measured, fix reverted - and the numbers say why all three.** `paths_to_output` deletes every via whose grid cell carries `CELL_PAD`, on the stated grounds that a through-hole pad already joins the layers. `CELL_PAD` covers a pad **plus its clearance**, and a surface-mount pad joins nothing, so a route that changed layer anywhere near any pad lost the via that held it together.
- Keeping the vias fixes the electrical fault: pins no copper reaches go **led_blink 1 -> 0, stm32_breakout 21 -> 6, multi_ic 60 -> 22**. It also makes the checker say what was always true - those vias do not fit where the router puts them: violations go 140 -> 386 on multi_ic, 133 -> 212 on stm32_breakout, and the shorts with them. Four tests move, including the round trip, which gains a `GND <-> VCC` short only after a save and reload.
- **Reverted rather than shipped.** Turning silent open circuits into 250 loud violations is progress in truth and a regression in the board; shipping it half-understood would have re-ratcheted three gates on a change whose second half is not written. The evidence is committed: `what_the_router_was_asked_to_connect` prints the copper for one net, and the tracker carries the before and after.
- DONE: **the router no longer hands back a board in two halves.** The search refuses to change layer on a cell carrying `CELL_PAD` - the rule `paths_to_output` used to apply after the fact by deleting the via - and every via the path asks for now reaches the output. Pins no copper reaches: **led_blink 1 -> 0, stm32_breakout 21 -> 6, multi_ic 60 -> 23**, and `UnroutedPinRule` is registered, so the gate counts them from here on.
- **The price is measured and paid in the open.** Those vias are copper now and the grid does not model a via's ring, so they land closer to things than the fab allows: violations go 133 -> 250 on stm32_breakout and 140 -> 375 on multi_ic, shorts 58 -> 130 and 37 -> 194. led_blink is unchanged at 2/0 with its open pin gone. The ratchets carry the new numbers with the reason written beside them, and the note says which direction they move next.
- Three tests moved with it, each restated rather than loosened: the hand-trace pair now asserts what it was always about (locked and unlocked copper treated the same) and allows the one via-ring fault; the DRC sandbox exempts the new rule on a board assembled from raw spatial entries with no footprints behind it.
- **What this bought, in one line:** before, a board could come back with an open circuit and every check in the project agreed it was fine. That cannot happen now.
- **Measured what the new violations are made of before acting on them, and the plan on record was wrong.** "Model the via ring" assumed vias were the problem. Counting the pairs on multi_ic: **139 part-to-trace, 38 `VCC_3V3` trace-to-trace, 22 `GND` trace-to-trace**, and single figures for the via shapes. The routing itself moved - 706 segments became 945 - because refusing a layer change on a pad pushed routes somewhere worse. The vias were not the story.
- DONE: **replaced that refusal with a price.** A layer change on a pad's copper costs the search 50 instead of being forbidden - the shape that has paid in this vector against five measured vetoes that did not. stm32_breakout **250 -> 239** violations, multi_ic **375 -> 336** and shorts **194 -> 166**; open pins unchanged at 6 and one better at 22 - and led_blink stays at 2/0 with none. Ratchets lowered to the measured values; stm32_breakout's shorts move the other way, 130 -> 136, which is written beside them rather than hidden.
- DONE: **cross-tabbed the dominant shape, and the grid is the thing that cannot tell.** `grid_vs_checker` on stm32_breakout: of 118 part-to-trace faults, **109 sit on cells the grid marked `CELL_PAD`** - the router is inside keepouts it drew itself. It is allowed to be: a net's pad zone opens every cell within about 0.76mm of any of its own pads so a route can reach them, and that opening does not know which pad. A GND route entering J1's zone to reach J1's ground pin runs 0.02mm from J1's VBUS pin, and both the checker and the fab call that a fault.
- **Failed experiment, measured, reverted: closing the pad gate by default.** `pad_zone_blocks_foreign_copper: true` makes stm32_breakout **239 -> 326** violations with **4 connections abandoned** and open pins 6 -> 24; multi_ic 336 -> 453. Sixth veto measured in this vector and the sixth to cost more than it buys. multi_ic's open pins improve, 22 -> 18, which is not worth 117 violations.
- **The mechanism, read rather than guessed:** `populate_pads` marks `CELL_PAD` and never touches `net_map` - the query does not even read `NetConnections`. The grid knows a cell is somebody's pad and not whose, so the pad zone has nothing finer to open than "all of it".
- DONE: **the grid knows whose pad a cell carries.** `populate_pads` reads each part's `NetConnections` and records the pad's net in a map of its own - kept apart from `net_map` because a rip-up clears that one and a pad is not ripped up. It is the fact the grid was missing: it knew a cell was somebody's pad and not whose, which is why a net's pad zone had nothing finer to open than all of it.
- **Failed experiment, measured, reverted: shutting a foreign pad inside the zone.** With ownership known, refusing those cells costs stm32_breakout **six abandoned connections** and takes multi_ic from 336 to 451 violations. Seventh veto in this vector, seventh to cost more than it buys.
- DONE: **priced instead, and it is a per-board trade** - which is what the variant mechanism is for. `foreign_pad_penalty` is a config knob, default 0, and `PathFinder Pad Aware` sets it to 20. Measured as a default: multi_ic **336 -> 267** violations and **166 -> 106** shorts, stm32_breakout 239 -> 280, led_blink gains a short. Best-of-seven: **multi_ic now picks Pad Aware** at 267/106, stm32_breakout keeps `Low-Via`, led_blink keeps `High-Density`. The default path is untouched at 239/136 and 336/166.
- **Failed experiment, measured, reverted: weighting the pad price by depth into the pad's disc.** The hypothesis was written here last fire - a cell on a pad's copper is a short and a cell in its clearance is a near miss, so one flat price charges them the same. Built (full weight on the copper, tapering to nothing at the outer edge) and measured at the same prices: **multi_ic 267 -> 413 after and 106 -> 242 shorts at 20**, stm32_breakout better at 5 and 50, worse at 20, and no price where both boards improve. Reverted. The model is more honest than the flat one and the router does not care, which is the third time in this vector that a better-reasoned cost has lost to a cruder one.
- DONE: **the pad price is swept now instead of assumed.** `pad_price_sweep` walks 0, 5, 20, 50 and 100 on all three fixtures and prints both readings. **multi_ic wants the price** (336 -> 257-267 after, 166 -> ~105 shorts), **stm32_breakout does not** (worse at every price, and it picks `Low-Via` anyway), and **led_blink trades the wrong way** - two near misses become one short at every price above zero, which is backwards under this project's own ranking and is why it keeps `High-Density`.
- **The 5 that reads best on multi_ic is not a better value, and the test that says so ships with it.** `how_much_of_the_pad_price_is_noise` routes multi_ic at 4, 5, 6 and 7: 288, 257, 278, 276 after - **31 violations of spread, 21 of shorts**, which swallows the 10 separating 5 from 20. The variant stays at 20. This is the second knob in this vector to be left alone on its own noise band, and the pattern is now written at the top of `docs/routing.md`.
- DONE: **the quality gate's clippy stage was red and nothing said so.** `cargo clippy --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop --all-targets -- -D warnings` is stage 2 of `scripts/quality-gate.sh`, and it failed on seven lints spread across the last few fires: a 14-argument search function, two complex tuple types, a `&mut Vec` where a slice does, a test module with items after it, and two needless borrows in the CLI export test. All fixed rather than allowed - the search's six prices are a `Search` struct now, which is why the argument list stopped growing. Stage 2 passes: `Finished` with no diagnostics.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: ... **the grid does not model a via's ring**~~ **Stale: it does.** `RoutingGrid::mark_ring` and `unmark_rings` exist, `congestion_map.set_ring_penalty(config.via_ring_penalty)` prices them, and `Priced Via Rings` ships as a variant - all recorded further up this same vector. Written from this file rather than from the code, which is the third next-action to go stale that way; read the module before planning around it.
- DONE: **started the cost model with two measurements, and neither is a knob.** First: `variant_picks_per_board` ranked as the router ranks - the shipped default comes **fourth of eight on all three boards**, and the winner is different each time. `led_blink` 1/0 against the default's 2/0, `stm32_breakout` 216/86 against 239/136, `multi_ic` 248/106 against 317/166. Not a bad setting, nobody's best. `route --in-house` is unaffected because it routes best-of-eight; what runs on the default is `--fast`, the viewer's single-shot path and every CI ratchet.
- Second: `grid_vs_checker`, introduced clearance violations cross-tabbed against what the grid thought was in the cell. **More than half of every violation on both dense boards is a trace on a part's pad, in a cell the grid had marked `pad`** - stm32_breakout 109 of 206, multi_ic 112 of 215. The grid knew. The search routed there because a pad zone switches every obstacle off within its radius so a route can reach the pad it is heading for, and a foreign part's pad goes off with the rest. That is the mechanism behind half the defect count, and the margin sweep already recorded moves exactly it: two cells instead of three costs stm32_breakout 50 fewer shorts.
- **What that does not license, written down so the next fire does not do it anyway.** The margin wants 3 on `led_blink` and 2 on the dense boards, and the boards separate by routes per grid cell - 0.0011 against 0.0120 and 0.0322, an order of magnitude, so any threshold between reproduces every board's own best. It is not implemented: a two-regime rule fitted on three boards and tested on the same three is a restatement, not a measurement, and it would win by construction where the fourteen instruments in the dropped table lost honestly.
- `--fast` says what it costs now: `cypcb route --help` prints "ranked against the other seven it comes fourth on every benchmark board", with the three pairs. A flag that buys wall clock should name what it spends.
- DONE: **the fourth benchmark board exists, and it found a defect in the variant ranking on its first run.** `shift_driver.kicad_pcb` - three 74HC595 driving 24 LEDs, 55 parts, 156 pads, 2 layers, 68x48mm - generated by `tests/fixtures/benchmark/make_shift_driver.py`, which declares the circuit and knows nothing about the router. Through-hole DIP beside 0805 chips, a mix the other three lack. It routes complete at **81 after / 72 introduced**, and reports nothing but the board outline before routing: the placement was spaced until courtyard, clearance and mask-bridge were all zero, because a benchmark whose parts overlap measures the router on a board nobody would fabricate.
- **The ranking hands over a board with 109 violations because it has 5 fewer shorts.** Ranked on the new board: `Bare Centre Line` first at 109/28, `Default` third at 81/33, `Pad Aware` fourth at **75**/35. The order is lexicographic - complete, then shorts, then composite - so seven fewer shorts bought thirty-four more violations. On the three older fixtures the two keys agree and this never showed. Whether that trade is the one this project wants is an open question with numbers behind it, and it wants its own sweep rather than a guess.
- **The test that caught it was asserting the opposite of the documented rule**, and hiding its own evidence: it compared the winner's composite against the default's - which the ranking deliberately does not order by - and printed the table *underneath* its assertions, so the failing run showed a panic and no numbers. The table prints first now, and the assertion is the one the feature actually promises: nothing the ranking picks may be beaten on shorts and violations at once.
- DONE: **swept the ranking rule, and it says the last fire overstated the finding.** `ranking_rule_sweep` scores seven rules against one routing pass per board - the shipped lexicographic order, and `violations + W x shorts` for W in 0, 1, 2, 5, 10, 20 - with the criterion written into the test before the numbers: a rule is out if it ever picks a **dominated** board, meaning another complete variant has fewer violations *and* no more shorts.
- **No rule picks a dominated board, including the shipped one.** `Pad Aware` at 75/35 does not beat `Bare Centre Line` at 109/28 - it is better on one axis and worse on the other, so both are on the Pareto front. What `shift_driver` revealed is not a defect: it is that the front is **wide** there, 34 violations across for 7 shorts, where on the three older boards it is a point. Calling it "the ranking hands over a worse board" was wrong and is corrected in `docs/routing.md`.
- **Only one of the four boards separates the rules at all.** led_blink, stm32_breakout and multi_ic pick the same variant under every rule; the whole question rests on `shift_driver`. The shipped rule wins the pre-registered criterion - no dominated picks, fewest shorts at 220 against 223 and 227 - so it stays. The number that would actually decide it is not in the table: how much a fab's yield suffers from a 0.05mm gap against a short. Nobody here has it.
- DONE: **the fifth board, and it killed the hypothesis I refused to implement.** `qfp_fanout.kicad_pcb` - one LQFP-64 at 0.5mm pitch escaping to headers on **two layers**, 17 parts, 138 pads, 46x46mm. `stm32_breakout` has a TQFP at 0.8mm and `multi_ic` an LQFP-100 on four layers; getting 64 pins out at 0.5mm with only a top and a bottom is the case a two-layer router is hardest on, and it shows: **331 introduced against `multi_ic`'s 247**, the hardest of the five. Clean before routing, like `shift_driver`.
- **The pad margin swept on all five says the density story was a coincidence of three samples.** Two fires ago the boards separated at 0.0011 / 0.0120 / 0.0322 routes per cell and any threshold between reproduced each board's best; the rule was not implemented because it would have won by construction. `shift_driver` sits at **0.0039** - exactly where that threshold would have gone - and wants margin **3**, the sparse board's answer. `qfp_fanout`, the densest fine-pitch board of the five, wants **3** as well. Only the two boards the rule was fitted on want 2. The shipped constant is the best answer on **three boards out of five**.
- **Eight instruments in the dropped table lost after being built; this one lost before, for the price of two fixtures.** That is what the refusal bought.
- The ranking question is unchanged: every rule picks the same variant on `qfp_fanout`, so four of five boards agree under all seven rules and it still rests on `shift_driver` alone.
- Both generators now share `tests/fixtures/benchmark/kicad_emit.py`. Proven safe rather than assumed: regenerating `shift_driver.kicad_pcb` through the extracted emitter leaves it **byte-identical** - `git diff --quiet` returns clean.
- DONE: **the same silent default was in six more places, and now none of them.** Fixing the footprint position last fire left pad position, pad width and height, pad drill, trace segment start and end, and via position all reading through `unwrap_or` - a pad on the footprint origin, a pad invented at 1 x 1mm, a hole that is not a hole, a segment collapsed onto the board origin. `parse_pad`, `parse_segment` and `parse_via` return `Result` now and every one of those numbers goes through `coordinate`.
- `crates/cypcb-kicad/tests/a_malformed_number_is_refused.rs` puts a comma in a footprint position, a comma in a pad position, a unit suffix in a pad size and a stray character in a drill, and demands each be refused by name - and then parses a board whose numbers are all numbers, because refusing a good board would be the worse defect. Mutation-checked: the pad test fails with only `pcb_parser.rs` reverted.
- DONE: **`multi_ic` had a comma in it, and the importer read a malformed coordinate as zero without a word.** `(at 105, 80)` on line 884 and `(at 140, 55)` on line 908 - the only two of their kind in five fixtures - put a ferrite bead and an Ethernet transformer **50mm to the left of the board**, which is exactly the ten off-board pads the new fixture test had been reporting. Every routing number ever measured on `multi_ic` was measured with them there.
- The parser refuses a coordinate it cannot read now: `footprint position x is not a number: String("105,"). A coordinate must be a plain decimal, so `(at 105, 80)` is one comma away from `(at 105 80)`.` `unwrap_or(0.0)` on a board file is a silent wrong answer of the kind this project keeps finding - a file is written by machines and edited by people, and putting a part 50mm from where the file says is worse than refusing to read it.
- **The fixture test caught its own list going stale**, which is what it was shaped for: `multi_ic.kicad_pcb: 0 pads outside the board outline, 10 known`. `stm32_breakout` (8) and `shift_driver` (6) still match theirs and are still a decision rather than a fire.
- Re-measured with the two parts back on the board: **978 routes, 281 before, 318 after, 247 introduced, 177 shorts**, band 63 violations wide against the 30 measured when they were out of the way. Ratchet 381 / 210.
- DONE: **the importer keys its footprint library by geometry now, not by name.** `resolve_library_key` compares the pads a part declares - number, shape, position, size, drill, layers - against what the name already holds: the plain name when it is free or already holds exactly these pads, and a numbered suffix otherwise. Two parts differing only in rotation still share a key, which is what the component's own `rotation` is for; a hundred 0402s naming one library stay one entry, which a real board depends on.
- Proven both directions in `crates/cypcb-kicad/tests/two_parts_one_name_two_geometries.rs`, with two boards written for it: one header along y beside the same name along x becomes `Test:TwoPin` and `Test:TwoPin#2`, and the pads land a pitch apart in y and in x respectively; the same geometry named twice stays one entry. Mutation-checked with only `pcb_parser.rs` reverted - the two-geometry test fails with `got: ["Test:TwoPin"]`.
- **The first version of that test counted the whole library and expected 1**, which is fourteen built-in footprints out. Wrong answer, right reason: it made the fix look broken when the fix was working. Corrected to count only what the board contributed.
- ~~DONE: **the importer keys its footprint library by library name alone,~~ so two parts sharing a name and differing in geometry collapse into whichever was read first.** `if !library.contains(&library_key)` in `pcb_parser.rs`. Found by asking a new question of the fixtures - is this a board? - and then chasing a contradiction: the file said two of `qfp_fanout`'s four headers run along x, and the model said all four run along y. Two headers therefore ran off the board *in the model* while the file was correct. Any KiCad board with a rotated or modified variant of a footprint imports wrong.
- **I got the measurement wrong twice before it was worth believing.** The first version squared each pad - `max(width, height)` on both axes, the bound the routing grid uses - and reported eleven of `qfp_fanout`'s pads as off the board where a check against their real extents found none; an LQFP pad is 1.5 by 0.3mm. The second was an `awk` that counted the library string twice per footprint and read the wrong block. Neither error reached a claim, because each contradicted an earlier measurement and the contradiction was chased rather than explained away.
- The fixture works around it by giving the horizontal pair a library name of their own, and `qfp_fanout` is now clean: **0 pads outside the outline**. That moved the board a third time, so its numbers were measured a third time - 1504 routes, **336 after / 336 introduced / 179 shorts**, band 296 to 336, ratchet 376 / 260.
- **`crates/cypcb-autoroute/tests/every_fixture_is_a_board_somebody_could_make.rs` counts off-board pads per fixture and holds each against what is known.** `stm32_breakout` runs two ten-pin headers past its 65mm height (8 pads), `multi_ic` places an inductor and an Ethernet transformer 50mm to the left of the board (10), `shift_driver` has its bypass capacitors 3mm above the top edge (6). A new fixture with copper off the board fails the test; these three are named with their counts so they cannot be forgotten.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: **key the footprint library by geometry, not by name.**~~ **Done, and this line outlived it.** `resolve_library_key` and `two_parts_one_name_two_geometries.rs` are three bullets above, with the mutation check. Fixing the three fixtures that hang off their boards is still a separate and larger decision: every ratchet and every table in `docs/routing.md` moves with them.
- DONE: **the fifth fixture was a board nobody could make, and I built it.** `qfp_fanout`'s violations clustered at y = 45.5 to 46.1 on a board 46mm tall, which is what gave it away: two 21-pin headers are **50.8mm of pins on a 46mm board**, so three pads per header sat past the outline. The router has been scored on that since the fixture landed, and its ratchet has been in CI guarding it.
- A 46mm edge holds fifteen pins at 2.54mm once margins are taken, so two sides cannot hold forty-eight. `kicad_emit.header` grew an `along_x` option and the headers are **one per edge**; a script checking every pad against the outline reports **0 outside**. Regenerating `shift_driver` through the shared emitter leaves it byte-identical, so the change is confined to the board that needed it.
- **The valid board is much harder, which is the honest outcome**: escaping to all four edges rather than two takes it from 343 after / 331 introduced to **676 / 665**, with 1971 routes against 1177. Its band was re-measured too - 606 to 665 across prices 0.22..0.28, **59 wide**, narrower than the 102 the broken board showed - and the ratchet is the new value plus that band, 735 / 500. `benchmark_all_fixtures_drc` passes.
- DONE: **sixteenth dropped instrument, and it closes the last untested dimension of the pad zone.** *Which* pads it opens and *how wide* were both measured long ago; *when* was not - the zone is open for the whole search, so a route can cut through a stranger's pads on the far side of the board as easily as reach its own pin. Priced rather than forbidden, because eight vetoes have lost: `foreign_pad_penalty` tapered by distance to the target, free at the pad and full twenty cells away. `Pad Aware` on stm32_breakout **280 -> 332**, qfp_fanout 558 -> 568 with shorts **330 -> 353**, multi_ic 248 -> **244**. One board better by 4, inside its band of 30; one worse by 52, outside its band of 38. Reverted.
- **Read with the fifteen before it, the pattern is now clear enough to write down**: pricing *where* copper may go has lost as a veto, as a flat price on a board that does not want it, as a taper by depth into a pad, and now as a taper by distance to the goal. The lever that keeps working is the one that changes what the search **sees** - the trace footprint reservation and the via ring - not what it is charged for crossing. That is the direction the cost model should take, and it is in `docs/routing.md` beside the numbers.
- DONE: **fifteenth dropped instrument, and it is the one that looked most like a plain bug.** `PadZone` is a disc in x and y with no layer - `in_pad_zone(x, y, zones)` - so a pad zone switched obstacles off on **every** layer, and a surface-mount pad on the top made foreign copper invisible on the bottom, where that pad has no copper at all. Giving the zone the pad's own `layer_mask` and honouring it: **stm32_breakout 239 -> 290, multi_ic 317 -> 382, qfp_fanout 343 -> 437**, led_blink and shift_driver unchanged. Three boards worse by 51, 65 and 94, all far outside their measured bands. Reverted, with the numbers, into `docs/routing.md`.
- **The mechanism is the one that table has recorded eight times, in a new hat: a restriction loses.** The blind opening was letting a route escape a crowded pad area by dropping to the other layer. Closing that escape is correct about the copper and worse about the board. Worth the fire: it is exactly the change somebody would otherwise make on faith, because it reads as an obvious modelling error rather than as a trade.
- DONE: **nine build artefacts were tracked, and they had polluted two commits.** `examples/*.dsn`, `*.ses` and `*.routes` for three boards - what `cypcb route` writes beside the design it read. Running `route` on any example rewrote them, so they arrived in whatever commit came next: once needing `chore: restore examples/blink.dsn` of its own, once caught in an amend. Nothing reads them - grepped across the tree, the only mention is a doc comment about writing to `/tmp`. Untracked, and the patterns joined the `*.routed.cypcb` rule that was already there and had missed them.
- Proven rather than assumed: after `cypcb route` **and** `cypcb export` both run on `examples/blink.cypcb`, `git status --porcelain` reports **0** untracked files. The full suite passes with the files gone, which is the other half - a fixture nobody reads is only safe to delete once something has looked.
- **Two next-actions I proposed were not defects, and measuring said so before I wrote any code.** `export` and `route` do not print violations at all - they print a count and send the reader to `cypcb check`, which is a better design than duplicating the list. And `export`'s refusal of a shorted board already names `--force`. Recorded because a fire spent confirming a non-defect is still a fire that found something out.
- DONE: **`--preset` means two different things and only one command said so.** `cypcb check --preset oshpark` works; `cypcb export --preset oshpark` says `Unknown preset 'oshpark'. Available presets: jlcpcb, pcbway`. The checker takes a **design-rule** preset - what a house can etch, eight of them - and the exporter takes a **file convention** preset - what a house wants the Gerbers called, two of them. A board can be checked against OSHPark and not yet exported for it, and nothing explained that. The refusal does now, in three sentences, and it comes **before** the build rather than after the file is read, parsed, imported, synced and warned about.
- **The short forms are safe and it is worth knowing why**: `--preset oshpark` resolves to `oshpark_2layer` and the output header says so - `27 DRC violation(s) against oshpark_2layer`. A board checked against the wrong house in silence is the failure that would matter, and the naming is what prevents it. Pinned by a test.
- The README claimed all four commands take "jlcpcb, pcbway, oshpark and the rest". False for `export`. It now names both lists and says why they differ; every one of the eight design-rule names in it was run and each reports itself back.
- DONE: **ran the whole gate again - all eight stages pass** - and then finished the thread on the side that had been left out. Seven commits had touched `cypcb-render`, `cypcb-world`, the viewer and the wasm bundle since the last full run; this file records twice what skipping that costs.
- DONE: **`cypcb check` printed millimetres and no line.** `unconnected-pin at (14.050mm, 10.000mm)` is not something a reader can search a text file for; the definition it belongs to is. Every violation that names an entity now carries `path:line:` in front, which a terminal and an editor both jump to:
```
examples/drc-test.cypcb:21: unconnected-pin at (18.730mm, 15.000mm): Unconnected pin: J1.1
examples/drc-test.cypcb:27: unconnected-pin at (14.050mm, 10.000mm): Unconnected pin: C1.1
```
- Checked against the file rather than trusted: `sed -n "21p;27p"` gives `component J1 connector "PIN-HDR-1x2" {` and `component C1 capacitor "0805" {`. Mutation-checked: the test fails with only `check.rs` reverted.
- DONE: **the placeholder fields are gone, and one of them was printing a fake location.** `SourceSpan` carried `start_line` and `start_column` beside its byte offsets, and every caller filled them with a constant - `1, 1` for components, `0, 0` for traces - because the line is not known where the span is built. Nothing outside the type read them, which is the only reason nobody had been misled: the LSP computes its own positions from the document. **Its `Display` did read them**, so any `SourceSpan` that was ever formatted printed `1:1` or `0:0` - a location that is always the same location. It prints the byte range now, which is what it knows.
- Removed rather than filled. Filling them means teaching every construction site the line, which is work with no reader; the way to a line is to convert the byte offset against the source, which is what the two fires above already do. A field that always lies is worse than a field that is absent.
- DONE: **and every DRC marker sat on line 1 too, which put the whole report on the `board` keyword.** A violation is discovered in board coordinates - two pieces of copper too close - so it has no line of its own, and `DrcViolation::source_span` is never filled: all **seventeen** construction sites in `cypcb-drc` pass `None`. The route that does exist is the entity the violation names and the span sync attached to it, and nothing walked it.
- `ViolationInfo::from_drc_located` walks it: entity, `SourceSpan`, byte offset, line. Proven at the engine's own boundary rather than in the browser - two overlapping 0805s defined on lines 6 and 11 produce violations carrying **6 or 11**, never 1. Mutation-checked by reverting only the two render files: `at least one violation has to name the line of the part it is about`.
- **The stored line and column on that span are placeholders and now say so** - `EcsSourceSpan::new(comp.span.start, comp.span.end, 1, 1)` for components, `0, 0` for traces. Only the byte offset beside them is real, which is why this converts from bytes rather than trusting the fields.
- DONE: **the editor underlined line 1 whatever line the fault was on.** The engine returned its messages as one blob of text - `format!("{e}")` - dropping the span at the boundary, and `updateDiagnostics` recovered a line by scanning that text with `/[Ll]ine\s+(\d+)/`, falling back to **1**. No parse or sync message writes the word "line": the location lives in the span that was just thrown away. So the fallback ran every time and Monaco confidently underlined the word `board` for a fault ten lines below it.
- `PcbEngine::get_diagnostics_json` carries each message with its line, column and end - converted once from the byte offset in `SourceDiagnostic::from_span`, counting characters so a board with a non-ASCII comment still points at the right column. The bridge uses it and the regex is gone. **The browser also shows the warnings now**, which it never did at all - the CLI learned to print them two fires ago.
- Mutation-checked by reverting only `lsp-bridge.ts`: all three tests fail, on the line `const lineMatch = errorMsg.match(/[Ll]ine\s+(\d+)/)`. The test drives the function with a fake monaco rather than the browser, because the app does not expose monaco on its debug surface and widening that surface for a test is the wrong trade.
- DONE: **only `check` was printing them.** Measured rather than assumed, on a board whose size has no unit: `check` printed the warning twice, `score`, `export`, `route` and `parse` printed it **zero** times. All four build the same board through the same `sync_ast_to_world` and dropped what it said - so a board whose size was assumed **exported at that size in silence**, and `export` is the command whose output goes to a fabricator. All five print it now.
- `parse` writes JSON to stdout and something reads it, so the warning goes to stderr and the document stays parseable - checked by piping stdout through `json.load` and by a test that does the same. Mutation-checked by reverting only the four command files: `cypcb score has to say what it assumed`, 2 of 6 tests fail.
- DONE: **warnings became diagnostics too, and the first real one is a silent unit assumption.** `SyncResult::warnings` was `Vec<String>` - a sentence with no line, printed straight after the errors that had just learned to point at one. It is `Vec<SyncWarning>` now: message, help, source and span, `miette::Severity::Warning`, rendered by `check` the same way errors are.
- **A bare number is millimetres everywhere, and nothing said so.** Measured through `cypcb parse`: `size 20 x 20` gives `width_nm: 20000000`, `at 5, 10` gives `5000000, 10000000`, `[width 0.3]` gives `300000`. The grammar's rule, and not one that should change - but somebody thinking in mils who writes `size 800 x 600` asks for 800mm. The board size is where getting it wrong resizes everything, so that is where it warns:
```
  ⚠ board size 20 has no unit, read as 20mm
   ╭─[2:10]
 2 │     size 20 x 20
   ·          ─┬
   ·           ╰── assumed here
  help: write `20mm` to say so, or `20mil` if that is what you meant
```
- It cannot be noisy: **no example board omits a unit**, checked across all 19. Placements and widths carry the same silent default and are left alone for now - a placement grossly wrong lands off the board and `edge-clearance` reports it, which the board size does not.
- `Dimension` gained `unit_written`, and **both parsers had to learn it**, which is what caught the sloppy edit: the tree-sitter path is behind a feature flag, so `cargo test --workspace` compiled none of it and passed while that file did not build. `differential` and `error_parity` are the tests that see it - 3 and 4 passed.
- DONE: **every semantic error already carried its line, and nothing was showing it.** `SyncError` holds the source text, a span and a help string and implements `miette::Diagnostic`; the CLI printed it with `Display` - `Semantic error: unknown component: 'R9'`, no file, no line, no column, on a board that may be five hundred lines long. Parse errors, three lines earlier in the same command, went through `miette::Report` and rendered the offending line with a caret. Five call sites, one renderer each, changed to the report the errors were built for:
```
cypcb::sync::unknown_pin
  × component 'R1' has no pin '3'. It has: 1, 2
    ╭─[12:5]
 11 │ net SIG {
 12 │     R1.3
    ·     ─┬
    ·      ╰── this footprint has 2 pins
 13 │ }
    ╰────
  help: use one of the pins the footprint declares: 1, 2
```
- The duplicate-designator error has two spans and now shows both - `first defined here` and `duplicate definition` - which is the whole question that error asks. `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/an_error_points_at_the_line_that_caused_it.rs` runs the binary, because the defect was in which renderer a command chose and nothing below the command can see that. Mutation-checked: **all four fail on the previous commit.**
- DONE: **a typo in a pin number cost a connection and said nothing.** Reading what the tool says for the five mistakes a user actually makes turned it up: `net SIG { R1.3 }` on a two-pad part stored a connection to pin 3, so the ratsnest had one end and nothing to route, and `cypcb check` reported only that R1.1 and R1.2 were unconnected - which reads as the design's own fault rather than as a three-character typo. Proven from the model: `cypcb parse` showed `pins: [('R1', [{'pin': '3', 'net': 'SIG'}])]`, a connection to a pin that does not exist. Now: `component 'R1' has no pin '3'. It has: 1, 2`, with the label `this footprint has 2 pins` and the help `use one of the pins the footprint declares: 1, 2`.
- **The check is deliberately narrow and the narrowness is tested.** It fires only when the footprint is known: a supplier's part has no pads until its fetch lands, and erroring on those would refuse every board that uses one. An unknown footprint is still reported once, by `UnknownFootprint`, without every pin of it being reported as missing on top. All 19 example boards stay clean. Mutation-checked: the first of the three tests fails on the previous commit.
- The other four mistakes read correctly today: `unknown component: 'R9'`, `unknown footprint: 'NOSUCH-99'`, `duplicate reference designator: 'R1'`. One is worth recording rather than fixing: **`size 20 x 20` with no unit is silently read as millimetres** and passes. That is a defensible default rather than a wrong answer, but nothing says it was assumed.
- DONE: **ran the whole gate end to end, first time since it grew a stage and the fixture list grew to five.** `=== All stages passed ===`, all eight: fmt, clippy, cargo test, eslint, vitest, playwright (rebuilding `viewer/pkg` first, 96 tests), the benchmark stage with its five checks including the new `router-is-repeatable`, and jscpd. Two of the last six fires changed `scripts/quality-gate.sh` and neither ran it; this file already records what that costs twice over.
- DONE: **the central table in `docs/routing.md` had drifted, and the drift is instructive.** Re-run on all five fixtures, `multi_ic` reads 270 before / 317 after where the table said 289 / 336 - **with `introduced` unchanged at 247**. The board and the routing are identical; what dropped is nineteen violations the fixture already had, and they went when the exporter started clipping the legend off copper and the courtyards started enclosing their own land pattern. `before` and `after` move whenever the *checker* changes; `introduced` is the column a routing experiment should be read on. The table now carries all five boards, their shorts, and that explanation.
- DONE: **the router is deterministic, and now something checks that it stays so.** Every number in `docs/routing.md` is one run - fourteen dropped instruments, two sweeps, five ratchets are all differences between single runs - and none of it means anything if re-running gives a different board. Rust randomises `HashMap` iteration order per process, so one map walked to order work would turn the lot into coin tosses with nothing noticing. Measured on all five fixtures, three runs each: **identical violations, shorts, segments, vias and total copper to the nanometre.** `is_the_router_repeatable` holds it, and stage 7 of the gate runs it.
- **That settles what the noise band is.** `qfp_fanout`'s 102-violation spread across prices 0.22..0.28 is not the router wobbling - it is negotiated congestion amplifying a hundredth of a price into a different rip-up order and a different board. The wording in `docs/routing.md` said "what negotiated congestion does on its own", which a reader could take either way; it now says which, and says the measurements are reproducible.
- DONE: **the two new boards were not guarded by anything, and now they are.** `DRC_RATCHETS` is a hand-written list and it still held three entries, so a regression on `shift_driver` or `qfp_fanout` would have passed the gate silently - the fixtures were in `BENCHMARKS` and outside the only thing that checks them. Both added with their measured value plus their own measured band, which is the rule this file already states: `shift_driver` 81/33 with a band of 12, ratcheted at **93/40**; `qfp_fanout` 343/183 with a band of **102**, ratcheted at **445/256**.
- **`qfp_fanout`'s band is a third of the value it guards** - 298 to 400 across prices 0.22..0.28, against `stm32_breakout`'s 38 and `multi_ic`'s 30. Negotiated congestion is least stable on a fine-pitch escape with only two layers, and that is worth knowing before any number measured on that board is read as a result: a 50-violation difference there is inside the noise. Its ratchet catches a collapse, not a regression, and saying so is better than a threshold nobody can trust.
- Verified with the gate's own stage: `benchmark_all_fixtures_drc` passes on all five - PathFinder at 2, 239, 317, 81, 343 against ratchets of 2, 239, 336, 93, 445 - and `routed_copper_reaches_the_files` passes 4 tests over the five boards.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. Five boards, fourteen dropped instruments,
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: a fourth and a fifth benchmark board that nobody tuned against~~ - the fourth is in. A fifth is still worth having before an adaptive pad margin is fitted.
- ~~NEXT-ACTION: the cost model, which `docs/routing.md` calls a project rather than a fire~~ - started; the first two measurements are above.
- The owner's two questions are unchanged: `interface`, and whether the router may place blind and buried vias - fourteen of twenty-six on the four-layer benchmark, at a board-house multiple.
- DONE: **the legend prints the names of the parts it labels.** A fabricated board with no `R1` beside R1 cannot be assembled by eye - the person holding the reel reads the design file instead - and the silkscreen carried courtyard outlines and a crosshair per part, nothing more. Gerber has no text a fabricator is obliged to honour, so `gerber/font.rs` draws letters as strokes the way the rest of the layer is drawn: capitals, digits and the three separators a designator or a net name can hold, on a unit box, with anything else printing nothing rather than a wrong character. Measured on `examples/blink.cypcb`: the top legend goes from **54 draw commands to 133**. A part with no refdes, or a name the font cannot spell, still gets the crosshair.
- Its own tests hold the font honest: every character a designator can hold has a glyph, a character outside the font draws nothing, and every stroke stays inside the box placement measures it against - the tail of a Q being the one deliberate exception, which is why it is named.
- DONE: nothing this cycle.
- DONE: **a copper pour can say what it is poured to.** The grammar has read `net` on a zone since the beginning, the parser filled `ZoneDef.net`, and sync ended at `let _ = net;` under a comment saying it would matter one day. `Zone` carries a `NetId` now, and the first thing that uses it is the ratsnest: a pad of that net sitting inside a pour on a layer the pour reaches is already connected by the plane, so the router is not asked to reach it. Proven both ways in one test - the same two-resistor board yields a GND net to route without the plane and none with it, while the signal between them still has to be routed.
- DONE: **modules can be instantiated, which is what makes them worth having.** `module Divider { ... }` parsed and defined a block nothing could reach - there was no instantiation syntax at all. `use Divider as DIV1 { IN = VIN, OUT = SENSE_A }` exists now, through the whole stack: a grammar rule and a regenerated `parser.c`, `ModuleInstance`/`PortConnection` in the AST, a converter in the parser, and expansion in sync. An instance copies the module's components under its own name, so two dividers are `DIV1_RTOP` and `DIV2_RTOP` rather than two `RTOP`s; an internal net is local the same way (`DIV1_MID`), while a net named by one of the module's pins becomes the design's net, which is the entire point of a pin. Expansion happens before every other pass, so nothing downstream needs to know modules exist. Two errors were added rather than left implicit: instantiating an undefined module, and leaving a declared pin unwired - which would otherwise silently become a local net and look connected. Proven end to end through the CLI: two instances of one divider yield four components, and the checker sees them as four.
- DONE: **an instance says where it goes.** `use Divider as DIV1 at 10mm, 5mm rotate 90` - without it every instance of a module landed on the last, and the two-divider board reported 8 violations that all said "these parts are in the same spot". A module places its parts in its own coordinates; the instance decides where those land. A turned instance turns its parts about that origin and adds its angle to each, so a rotated block behaves like the same block. Verified on the same board twice: `DIV1_RTOP` at (15mm, 10mm, 0deg) and `DIV2_RTOP` at (20mm, 10mm, 90deg) from one module whose RTOP sits at 5mm, 5mm - and `cypcb check` goes from 8 violations to `passed DRC`.
- DONE: **modules nest.** A module can be built from other modules, and expansion recurses: names compose (`BANK_LEFT_R1`), placements compose through both frames, and a port two levels down still reaches the design's own net - `SUPPLY = VIN` on the outer instance is what the inner `IN = SUPPLY` resolves to. Verified on a `Pair` of two `Divider`s placed inside one `BANK` at 20mm: the parts land at 22mm and 32mm, and `BANK_LEFT_IN` does **not** exist, because a pin wired through must not also become a local net. A module that instantiates itself, directly or through another, is reported as `module instantiates itself: A -> B -> A` rather than expanding for ever; no depth limit is needed, since a chain without repeats cannot outlast the file's module count. The grammar had to admit `use` inside a `module` body, which it did not.
- DONE: **`assert` is checked.** The grammar, the AST and the parser have carried assertions for a long time and nothing read them. They ride onto the board model like net constraints do, and `AssertionRule` evaluates them with the rest of DRC, so `cypcb check` reports them. `assert board.width <= 100mm` on a 120mm board says so with the number; `board.layers >= 2` on a 2-layer board is silent. Operands are typed - a length is refused a comparison with a resistance rather than being coerced into one. **An assertion the checker cannot evaluate is reported, not skipped**: `R1.value` comes back as "not something the checker can read yet", because a statement that quietly does nothing leaves the board looking checked, which is worse than one that fails. Known today: `board.width`, `board.height`, `board.layers`, and any literal.
- Also: violation messages no longer invent `entity #0:` for something the world cannot name. An assertion is about the board, not a part, and its message already reads as a sentence. Named entities are still labelled - `R1 ↔ R2: Clearance violation` is unchanged.
- DONE: **a component's value can be a quantity, and an assertion can read it.** The decision this needed had already been made by the language: `value_property` accepts `value 10kohm`, and the parser was flattening it - "convert the raw text to a StringLit so the component value field remains compatible". The type is kept now, alongside the string, and rides onto the board as a `TypedValue` component. `assert R1.value >= 47kohm` on a 10k part reports `but the board has 10000ohm`. A part written `value "10k"` is refused with the fix in the message: *write `value 10kohm` rather than `value "10k"`, or the checker has only a label to go on* - because "10k" is ten kilohms only if you already know the part is a resistor, and the checker should not be the one guessing.
- DONE: **assertions read nets.** `VBUS.current`, `VBUS.width` and `VBUS.clearance` resolve to what the net's own block states, which the board model has carried since M1. The field decides what kind of name it is - a part has a `value`, a net has those three - and `board` is neither, which is a collision the first attempt walked straight into: `board.width` matched the net branch and made a working assertion fail. A net that states nothing is not treated as satisfying everything; it is reported with the block to write.
- **Writing the test found a hole in the grammar.** `physical_unit` listed `uA` under a comment claiming `mA` and `A` were "already handled by current_unit". They were not listed, so `current 2A` parsed inside a net block while `assert VBUS.current >= 1A` was a syntax error. Both are units now. Proven end to end: `VBUS.current >= 1A` is silent on a 2A net, `VBUS.width >= 1mm` reports `but the board has 0.800mm`.
- DONE: **`import` works, so a module library is worth writing.** A path resolves relative to the file that wrote it - the way every other language reads a path in a source file, needing no project root to configure and no search path to get wrong. Only reusable definitions cross a file boundary: modules, footprints and interfaces. A board, a component or a net belongs to the design that wrote it, and letting those through would make `import` textual inclusion under another name. `import "x"` takes everything reusable, `import A, B from "x"` takes what it names and reports what is not there. Libraries can be built from libraries, and a file that imports itself is reported with the chain rather than followed. Wired into `check`, `score` and `route`, with import errors printed and the rest of the design still checked. Proven end to end: a `Divider` imported from `lib/blocks.cypcb` and instantiated twice passes DRC, and renaming it to one the library lacks reports `'lib/blocks.cypcb' does not define 'Regulator'`.
- DONE: **the language is visible now.** Everything shipped this week - modules, instantiation, placement, nesting, imports, assertions, typed values - existed only in tests. `examples/v2-modules.cypcb` defined two modules under a comment reading "Top-level board using the modules" and never used one; it instantiates three now, including a rotated one, and its internal nets are named so the pins actually map. `examples/lib/blocks.cypcb` plus `examples/v2-imports.cypcb` are new: a library with no board of its own, and a design that imports two blocks from it and places four instances. Both pass `cypcb check`, and all 17 examples still behave.
- DONE: **the README stopped saying the module system does not exist.** Two claims were false in the same document that describes the feature: "Where it's behind: no module/package system yet" and "The main gaps are copper pour, module system, and autorouter". The feature table now says what `module`, `use`, `import` and `assert` do and points at the examples; copper pour says what works (a zone carries a net, the ratsnest honours a pour) and what does not (nothing fills one); and the GitHub Actions row says CI is not planned rather than pending, which is the owner's decision from this session.
- DONE: **net classes.** `netclass Power [width 0.5mm clearance 0.3mm] { VCC GND }` states a rule once for a group of nets instead of repeating it on each. Precedence is the part worth getting right and is explicit: classes are applied before any net block, and a net that states something overwrites **only the field it states** - so `net VCC [width 0.8mm]` keeps the class's clearance. Everything downstream already honours per-net constraints, so the router draws a class's width and the checker enforces its clearance without another change. Proven end to end through assertions: on a board whose only statement is the class, `VCC.width >= 0.5mm` and `GND.current >= 1A` are silent while `VCC.width >= 1mm` reports `but the board has 0.500mm`.
- DONE: **the board has a real edge.** `BoardSize` is a width and a height, so a cutout, a slot and a chamfer all lived inside the same rectangle and edge clearance passed copper that sits outside the actual board. A `BoardOutline` ring answers that, the KiCad importer assembles one by walking Edge.Cuts segments end to end - in any order, either direction - and `EdgeClearanceRule` measures the box against the ring's edges, reporting zero for anything whose centre falls outside it. An outline that does not close yields nothing rather than a guess: a partial ring would put an edge where the board has none, and three sides of a rectangle is exactly what a half-finished file looks like. Proven on an L-shaped board where a pad in the removed corner is flagged and one well inside is not, and on Edge.Cuts written out of order with a segment reversed. **The benchmark fixtures now measure against an assembled ring rather than a bounding box and their numbers do not move** - 0/167/110 - which is the equivalence check for a rectangular board.
- DONE: **a board that is not a rectangle, end to end.** The outline had a model and an importer; it had no syntax and no way out to a fabricator, which meant an L-shaped board still exported as a rectangle - not a board with a cutout, but the wrong board. `outline { point 0mm, 0mm  point 40mm, 0mm ... }` writes one, and the Edge_Cuts gerber emits it. Proven on one file: a 40x40 board with the top-right quarter removed reports `edge-clearance at (30.000mm, 30.000mm): R2 ... 0.00mm actual` for the part standing in the notch, and its gerber walks six points and closes, where before it walked four corners of the bounding box. One ordering bug found by the board refusing to fail: the outline pass ran before the loop that creates the board entity, so `board_entity()` was empty and nothing was stored - it runs after now, which is the only order that can work.
- DONE: **a footprint can carry its own silkscreen.** The library held pads, bounds and a courtyard, so the only legend anything could reason about was the rectangle the exporter derived - which is why the silk-clearance rule measured a box rather than ink, and why a footprint fetched from a supplier arrived with artwork that had nowhere to go. `Footprint.silk` is a list of segments and circles in footprint coordinates. The checker measures those when they exist and falls back to the courtyard when they do not; the gerber prints them **instead of** the courtyard, because drawing both would put a box on the board the footprint never had. A circle is walked as a polygon fine enough that the error is under the width of the line drawing it. Proven with a part whose legend runs 4mm past its own courtyard onto a neighbour's pad: only the artwork path reports it.
- DONE: **a fetched footprint can bring its legend.** `register_footprint` took pads only, so the artwork a supplier sends had a home in the model and no road to it. It takes silk as well now, in the shape the viewer already uses - tagged by `type`, nanometres, relative to the footprint origin. An arc is parsed and dropped rather than refused: the board model has no arc, and rejecting a whole footprint over a rounded corner would be the worse trade. Malformed silk is an error rather than silence, like malformed pads. Two tests: a footprint carrying a segment, a circle and an arc keeps two shapes, and garbage comes back as `Failed to deserialize silk`.
- **Found while wiring it: the viewer cannot call `register_footprint` at all.** The interface method and the adapter passthrough went in with the parser swap and came out with it when that was reverted, so `registerDynamicFootprint` fills a JavaScript map and tells the engine nothing. `tsc` and vitest are clean because nothing references the missing method - which is exactly why it went unnoticed. Re-adding it belongs with the swap rather than before it, since the mock engine would need the method too and the mock is slated to go.
- DONE: **wired it, and the road runs both ways now.** `PcbEngine` declares `register_footprint`, the WASM adapter passes it through as JSON, and `registerDynamicFootprint` hands every fetch to the engine instead of only to the JavaScript map. A fetch that lands before the engine exists is replayed when it is created - a part fetched during startup used to be known to the drawing code and unknown to the model for the rest of the session. The test found a hole in the fix itself: the replay went into the WASM branch only, so the fallback engine still missed everything registered early. Both branches replay now. `npx vitest run` -> 14 files, 150 tests; `tsc --noEmit` and `eslint` clean.
- DONE: **and the copper arrives, which is the half wiring does not prove.** `crates/cypcb-render/tests/fetched_footprint_reaches_drc.rs` registers a two-pad part the way a supplier fetch does, places two of them 5mm apart and then 1.7mm apart, and the checker has to notice: pad copper reaches 0.8mm either side of a part's centre, so the close pair leaves a 0.1mm gap against the 0.127mm the preset asks for. Mutation-checked by registering the name and dropping the pads, which turns both tests red. The second one records something better than the failure it was written to catch: a board naming a footprint the engine does not know is **refused by name** rather than loaded padless and reported clean - pinned so nobody softens it into a warning.
- DONE: **a fetched arc is ink now instead of nothing.** A supplier's footprint draws rounded outlines and pin-one arcs, the board model has no arc, and `SilkInfo::Arc` was parsed and thrown away - so a part arrived with its artwork and printed without it, silently. Worse than that: the viewer sends `startAngle` and `endAngle` and the Rust struct did not have the fields, so the angles were dropped at deserialisation before the shape was dropped at conversion. `SilkInfo::Arc` carries both now (defaulted, so a payload written earlier still reads as the full circle it meant), and `to_shapes` walks it into segments at 32 to the turn - the same approximation the Gerber exporter already uses for a circle, where the polygon differs from the curve by less than the width of the line drawing it.
- **Found while testing it: the snapshot carries no silkscreen at all.** `build_snapshot` gives each component its pads, body size and 3D model and nothing about its legend, so the viewer draws silk from its own JavaScript copy of the footprint rather than from the engine. That is the same duplication V4 exists for, and it is why this had to be tested on the conversion rather than end to end.
- DONE: **it does now.** `ComponentInfo` carries the footprint's artwork, `SilkInfo::from_shape` describes what the model holds, and the field is omitted from the JSON when a part has no legend so a board without one costs nothing. The TypeScript side already declared `silk: SilkShape[]` on a component and filled it from its own parse - the engine simply never sent any. Proven on a registered part with a segment, a circle and a quarter arc: the snapshot comes back with **9 segments and 1 circle**, the arc having become eight of the segments. `SilkShape.layer` is optional now, because neither producer sets it and the renderer draws every shape in the silkscreen colour without asking - a required field nothing supplies is a type that describes nobody.
- DONE: **and closed the hazard that opened with it.** The viewer's JavaScript silk-clearance check read `shape.layer` to decide which face a legend prints on. The EasyEDA parser sets one; the engine does not, so the moment engine-supplied artwork reaches that check every shape would have compared `undefined` against every pad, matched nothing, and skipped silently - the exact shape of failure this project keeps finding. The component's own copper decides now when the shape says nothing. Mutation-checked: reading only the shape turns the new test red.
- **The larger dedup is still blocked.** Making the viewer draw from the engine's `silk` rather than its own is not a rendering change - `WasmPcbEngineAdapter.load_source` parses in JavaScript and builds the whole snapshot there, so the engine's components never reach the screen at all. That is V4 and it waits on the parser decision.
- DONE: **a footprint written by hand can carry its legend.** `silk line -1mm, 0.6mm to 1mm, 0.6mm width 0.15mm` and `silk circle -1.2mm, 0mm radius 0.15mm` inside a `footprint` block. A width left unstated takes the exporter's own stroke, so a legend written without one prints as the ink the exporter would have drawn anyway. Proven end to end: a part with one line and one circle emits **39** draw commands on the top silkscreen - two for the line, thirty-three for the circle walked as a polygon, four for the designator crosshair - and no courtyard rectangle, because a footprint with artwork prints that instead.
- DONE: **the command line can route with the project's own router, and the traces come back as source.** `cypcb route` shells out to a Java jar - its help says "using FreeRouting autorouter" and it wants `freerouting.jar` - while `score` uses the in-house PathFinder only to print numbers. So the README's headline promise, that traces persist as readable DSL code, had no command-line path at all. `cypcb route --in-house` routes with PathFinder, needs no Java, and writes the design plus its `trace` blocks to a new file. Proven on blink: 72 segments and 4 vias out, **14 `trace` blocks** in `blink.routed.cypcb`, and `cypcb check` parses that file straight back. The default stays on FreeRouting because which router this project bets on is D1's to answer, not mine.
- The writer moved rather than being copied: `export_traces_as_dsl` lived as a method on the WASM engine, which the CLI cannot reach. It is `cypcb_world::dsl::traces_as_dsl` now, the engine delegates to it, and its round-trip test moved with it - a value written and read back has to be the value that went in, which is what makes the DSL a storage format rather than a report.
- DONE: **every via was being lost on reload, and nothing said so.** Checking the second headline claim - that traces round-trip exactly - turned up a data-loss bug in the storage format.
